FOREWORD 

The problems and uncertainties which have arisen under the 1954 Code are many and varied. And decisions and rulings under the old 1939 Code may not always be helpful under present law. This analysis is intended to provide answers, where possible, in the obscure areas of the new Code: 

The death of a taxpayer disrupts the customary procedure for assessing and collecting taxes on income earned in his lifetime. His death terminates the taxable year already begun, the unit regarded as basic for the true reflection of income. The problems raised by this circumstance are unique, requiring for their solution modified concepts of tax accounting. 

Contained in this book are the fundamental rules to be followed in correctly preparing the decedent's last return and the return of an estate or trust. The discussion is highlighted with many examples-over 100—to more clearly illustrate the technical provisions frequently involved. 

For continuing developments, reference to the publishers' FEDERAL TAX 

REPORTS is recommended.

PART I-THE DECEDENT'S LAST RETURN What To Do About The Decedent's Return and Tax 

[1] When a return is filed for the decedent and who must file. When a person dies, a return of his income must usually be made for that part of the year during which he was alive. A return must be filed for a decedent if his gross income from the beginning of the taxable year to the date of his death was $600 or more ($1,200 or more if he was 65 or older), or if his net earnings from self-employment were at least $400.¹ If the decedent's gross income was under $600 (or $1,200 for 65-year-olds) but tax was withheld or estimated tax was paid, a return should be filed to get a refund. 

The $600 (or $1,200) test applies to gross income and therefore items which are not included in the statutory meaning of that term are not to be taken into consideration. Accordingly, if the decedent's only income consisted of old-age pension benefits received under the Social Security Act or Railroad Retirement Act or tax-exempt bond interest, a return for the decedent would not have to be filed. 

Ordinarily, the decedent's last return is to be filed by the executor or administrator of his estate. Since the return is for the decedent and not the estate, it is prepared and signed on his behalf. If no appointment of an executor or administrator is made (as where the estate is not probated), the return is to be filed by some person charged with the property of the decedent, such as the surviving spouse. The heirs could act jointly, or appoint one of their number to take charge of the estate for this purpose. Such parties must assume responsibility for making the return or otherwise they will subject themselves to penalties for wilful neglect or tax evasion. [¶ 1.01] Signing the return.—If the return for the decedent is filed by the appointed legal representative, it should be signed by him on the line indicated for taxpayer; as, for instance: "John Smith, Administrator of the Estate of Harry Roe, Deceased", or "John Doe, Executor of the Last Will and Testament of John Brown, Deceased." If no legal representative has been appointed when the decedent's return is due to be filed, the surviving spouse or other person or persons entitled to receive the decedent's property should sign his own name as taxpayer "for and on behalf of [decedent's name], deceased, who died [date of death]." A certified copy of Letters Testamentary or 

Letters of Administration (as the case may be) should accompany the return. executed by the legal representative, or, if other parties sign on the decedent's behalf, an affidavit should accompany the return which shows the relationship of the parties to the decedent and his estate. 

If the surviving spouse desires to make the decedent's last return a joint return, and no legal representative has been appointed, the return should be signed by the surviving spouse, with the following appendage thereto "individually, and for and on behalf of [deceased spouse's name], who died on [date of death]." The "name" designation at the top of the return should include both spouses' names, with "Deceased" added to the name of the deceased spouse, such as "John Smith and Mary Smith, Deceased." 

As to requirements for filing a joint return, see 6 below. 

1 Code Sec. 6012(a)(1). 

¶ 1

In the case of the death of a minor or insane person, the legal guardian (presumably, the guardian of his estate, if separate guardians of estate and person were appointed), conservator, or committee should file his last return. [12] When does the estate file a return and who files it?—A decedent's estate must account for any of its income for the period beginning the day after the death of the decedent, and for succeeding taxable years, until administration of the estate is completed. 

An income tax return must be filed if the estate's gross income for the taxable year is $600 or more.2 The return is to be filed by the executor or administrator of the estate.

In filing its first return an estate may choose a calendar year or any fiscal year it wishes. If it chooses the same accounting period as the decedent had, its first return will be for a short period to fill out the unexpired full year of the decedent.

Example: A, who filed his returns on the basis of the calendar year, died 

May 6, 1957, leaving a substantial estate. On June 2, 1957, the B bank was appointed executor. In due course, as required, B files a final return for the decedent. It also files a first return for the estate, electing to use a calendar year. The final return for the decedent will cover the period beginning January 

1, 1957, and ending May 6, 1957. The first return for the estate will cover the period beginning May 7, 1957, and ending December 31, 1957. Both returns will be due on or before April 15, 1958. The decedent and the estate are separate taxable entities.

For additional information on return requirements of estates, see ¶ 38. [13] Which form to use. The last return of a decedent may be on 

Form 1040 or on Form 1040A (the punch card form) provided, of course, that the special requirements for Form 1040A are met. The surviving spouse may use the standard deduction or Optional Tax Table only if the last return (if a separate return) of the decedent used one of them.

If the decedent was a farmer, Schedule F (Form 1040) must accompany the regular return.

The return of an estate or a trust is filed on Form 1041. [4] When must the return be filed and tax paid?—The final return of a decedent is to be filed on or before the 15th day of the fourth month following the close of the 12-month period with which his taxable year would have closed had he lived. If the decedent was on a calendar-year basis, the return is due on or before April 15 following the close of the year in which he died. 

Accordingly, for calendar-year decedents dying in 1957, the due date is 

April 15, 1958. The return of an estate (and also a trust) is to be filed on or before the 15th day of the fourth month following the close of the estate's (or trust's) taxable year. Therefore, for an estate (or trust) which chooses as its accounting period a calendar year, its return would be due on or before April 15 following the close of the calendar year. In the example at ¶2 above, therefore, both the decedent's final return and the estate's first return would be due on or before April 15, 1958.

The tax shown due on the decedent's last return must usually be paid in full when the return is filed.5 However, if Form 1040A is filed for the decedent, and the person filing elects not to compute the tax from the optional tax table but to wait until the District Director sends him a bill if a tax is due, 2 Code Sec. 6012(a)(3). 

3 Code Sec. 6072(a). 

4 Code Sec. 6072(a). 

5 Code Sec. 6151(a). 

¶ 2

What to Do About Decedent's Return and Tax payment of the tax must be made within 30 days after the District Director mails him the bill."

The tax rates for individuals are applied to the income of the decedent for the short taxable year ending with the date of his death without any proration or "annualizing.” 

Upon a showing of cause, the District Director has authority to extend the time for filing returns and payment of tax. Generally, extensions of time may be granted for periods up to six months. As a matter of policy, the initial extension is for 90 days. If an extension is granted, interest at 6% will nevertheless be payable from the original due date of the tax. 

The IRS has provided Form 1310 whereby a claimant, on behalf of the deceased taxpayer, may obtain a refund of an overpayment of tax, even though the estate is not to be probated. It apparently is intended to effect payment of any refund admittedly due the decedent. This form is not, however, intended as a substitute for Form 843 (the regular claim for refund) wherever it is necessary to employ Form 843, but may accompany it in anticipation of allowance of the claim. [15] Where is the return filed? The decedent's last return is to be filed with the District Director for the district in which is located the legal residence or principal place of business of the person making the return. Therefore, the district for filing will not necessarily be the one in which is located the legal residence of the decedent at the time of his death. If the person making the return has no legal residence or principal place of business, then the return is to be filed with the District Director at Baltimore, Md.8 

For returns of estates and trusts, see ¶ 37-44 below. [6] Can a joint return be filed?—A joint return may be filed where one or both spouses die during the year, and the taxable year of both began on the same day, whether such year is a fiscal or a calendar year. The joint return is made for the regular taxable year of the survivor and the short period of the decedent.

Example (1): A husband and wife are on a calendar-year basis. The wife dies on March 20, 1957. The husband files a joint return. The return will cover the wife's income and deductions for the period January 1-March 20, and the husband's income and deductions for the full 1957 taxable year. 

If the surviving spouse remarries before the close of his taxable year in which the decedent spouse died, a joint return cannot be filed. However, the survivor may file a joint return with the new spouse provided all other requirements are met. Also, a joint return cannot be filed by the survivor if the taxable year of either is a fractional part of a year resulting from a change of accounting period.

Example (2): A husband and wife make their returns on a calendar-year basis. The wife dies on May 16, 1957. Thereafter the husband receives permission to change his accounting period to a fiscal year beginning July 1, 1957. 

A joint return cannot be made for the short taxable year ending June 30, 1957. 

A joint return generally can be made only by the executor or administrator and the survivor. The surviving spouse, alone, however, may make a joint return for himself and the deceased spouse if all three of these requirements are met: 

• Code Sec. 6151(b)(1). 

7 Code Secs. 6081 (a) and 6161(a)(1). 

8 Code Sec. 6091(b)(1). 

• Code Sec. 6013(a)(2). 16

(1) No return was made by the decedent for the taxable year for which the joint return is made. (2) No executor or administrator was appointed before the time the joint return was made. (3) No executor or administrator was appointed before the last day prescribed for filing the return of the surviving spouse, including any extensions.10

Even though the surviving spouse should comply with all the conditions set out above and file a valid joint return with the deceased, the executor or administrator, later appointed, may still disaffirm the joint return. Such a disaffirmance must be made within one year after the last day prescribed for filing the return of the surviving spouse. The disaffirmance is made by filing a separate return for the decedent. If a disaffirmance is made, then the survivor's return will be considered to be his separate return. His tax will be computed on the basis of the joint return he filed, with all the items properly includible in the return of the deceased spouse deleted from it. 

There is no compulsion on the executor or administrator of a deceased spouse to disaffirm a joint return made by the survivor. However, in making his decision certain factors should be considered. If the joint return is allowed to stand, the administrator may work out an agreement with the survivor under which a division of the joint tax may save money for the estate. On the other hand, when the separate return of the deceased is figured out, it may be found that the estate can save money by disaffirming the joint return and filing a separate return. No automatic extension of time is provided for the executor's or administrator's return, and if it is filed after the regular due date for the deceased's regular return, all penalties will attach to it. Primarily, however, it will be a question of adjustment between the executor or administrator and the surviving spouse whether a joint return made by the survivor should be allowed to stand.

In one case, where a co-executrix refused to enter into a joint return on behalf of the decedent, a state court found the refusal to be "unreasonable and arbitrary" and directed execution of a joint return.¹¹

If a separate return is originally filed for the decedent, the executor or administrator may later elect to change to a joint return if the total tax shown on the joint return is paid at or before the time it is filed. A joint return made after the filing of a separate return must be filed within 3 years of the last date prescribed by law for the filing of the separate return (by April 15, 1961 for calendar-year 1957 returns), and this period is determined without taking into regard any extensions of time.12

A joint return may be filed even though the decedent and the surviving spouse were not living together at the date of the decedent's death. How ever, a joint return cannot be filed if they were legally separated under a decree of divorce or separate maintenance on such date. 13 [¶ 6.01] Special benefits for surviving spouse after year of decedent's death. A surviving spouse may use the joint return tax rates for the two taxable years following the year of death of the deceased spouse. But such rates can be used only if the survivor maintains as his home a household which, for the entire taxable year, is the principal place of abode of a son, 10 Code Sec. 6013(a)(3). 

Floyd Est., (Orphans' Ct. of Penn.) 51-2 

USTC. 9415.

13 Code Sec. 6013(d)(2). 

¶ 6.01

How Does Death Affect Exemptions stepson, daughter, or stepdaughter who is a member of the household of such surviving spouse and for whom the taxpayer is entitled to the exemption for a dependent.14

Thus, full income-splitting benefits are accorded such a surviving spouse, as if a joint return were filed. Such a widow or widower uses the joint return tax table or the joint return columns in the Optional Tax Table in computing the tax. 

If the taxpayer remarries after the death of the spouse, he loses this benefit, but can file a joint return with the new spouse. Also, this benefit is afforded a survivor only if he or she was entitled to file a joint return with the deceased spouse during the latter's lifetime.

For the calendar year 1957, therefore, a widow or widower with dependent children is entitled to income-splitting benefits (but not any of the decedent's exemptions) if the spouse died at any time during 1955 or 1956. 

It should be emphasized again, that this "surviving spouse" provision makes the joint return tax rates applicable; it does not authorize the surviving spouse to file a joint return or claim any personal exemptions but his own and those of the dependent or dependents necessary to meet the definition of a “surviving spouse. 

Example (3): A "surviving spouse," with two dependents, would file a separate return, but would claim three exemptions in determining the amount of taxable income. He would then compute the tax from the joint return tax table or the joint return columns in the Optional Tax Table, just as though, at this point, a joint return were being filed. [7] What should be done about estimated tax payments?-A declaration of estimated tax is not required after death. If a joint declaration has been made by a husband and wife and one of them dies during 1957, the survivor must continue to make the payments.15 However, the survivor, if he or she wishes, can file an amended declaration on which he shows his separate estimated tax for the taxable year. On the other hand, if one of the spouses makes a separate declaration of estimated tax but dies before completing his installment payments, the survivor does not have to continue to make the payments and there is no obligation on the estate of the deceased spouse to pay. But if the survivor files a joint return with the deceased on or before the income tax return date, a credit may be claimed for any installments paid by the deceased on his or her separate declaration. The filing of decedent's last return is not excused, however, merely because payments made by him on the declaration of estimated tax leave nothing to be paid, where a last return would otherwise be required.

An estate or trust does not have to make declarations or payments of estimated tax.

How Does Death Affect Exemptions? 

[8] Generally speaking the exemptions allowed to a taxpayer are his 'personal" exemption of $600 and the $600 exemption allowed for each dependent. There are "additional" personal exemptions allowed for taxpayers who are 65 years old or older and for taxpayers who are blind. For a husband and wife filing a joint return there are at least two "personal" exemptions of $600, since each is regarded as a taxpayer, plus any exemptions for dependents; for a married person filing a separate return, all the "personal" exemp- 

14 Reg. § 1.2-2. 

15 Code Sec. 6015(b). 

18

tions of the other spouse can be claimed if that spouse has no gross income and is not the dependent of another person.16 

Exemption amounts are not required to be prorated because of the death of a taxpayer, his spouse or a dependent during the taxable year. 

Example (1): A child is born on December 31, 1957 and dies on January 

1, 1958. A full $600 exemption is allowed on the 1957 return of its parents and another full $600 exemption is allowed on the 1958 return. 

No dependency exemption may be claimed for a still-born child or an unborn child.17

Death of one spouse will not deprive the survivor of the right to claim exemptions of the decedent. The crucial date for determining married or "blind" status is ordinarily the last day of the taxable year, but in case of death, it is the day of death.

Thus, where a blind spouse qualifies for the $600 blind exemption and dies during the survivor's taxable year, the survivor can take the deceased's ordinary $600 exemption plus the deceased's $600 "blind" exemption on a separate return, if the deceased had no gross income in the calendar year in which the taxable year of the survivor began, and if the deceased was not a dependent of anyone else. The survivor can also file a joint return and claim the decedent's two $600 exemptions, if he is qualified, under the rules at ¶ 6. 

The additional $600 exemption for "old-age" will not be allowed on a return of the survivor for a spouse who dies before reaching 65 years of age, even though the decedent would have attained 65 before the end of the survivor's taxable year.18

On the final separate return of a decedent, there may be claimed the decedent's own exemptions and the exemptions for the surviving spouse if the survivor had no gross income and was not a dependent of anyone else. The 

IRS has ruled that where a widow remarries within the same calendar year in which her husband died and she had no gross income and was not the dependent of another taxpayer, the full marital exemption is allowable on the final separate return of the deceased husband and also on the separate calendar-year return filed by the second husband.19 Further, since dependency exemption is based upon furnishing over half the support during the calendar year in which the taxable year of the taxpayer begins, a deceased who furnished over half the support to a person otherwise qualifying as a dependent would be entitled to the full exemption for such dependent, without proration. 

Example (2): A is supporting his aged father, furnishing full support to the date of his (A's) death, September 1, 1957. Assuming this was over half the support for the calendar year, a $600 exemption would be allowed for the father on A's final return. But assuming that A died on April 1 and his brother (B) supported the father for the balance of the year, incurring a larger expense than A did during the first part of the year, B alone would be entitled to the exemption. 

If the support in the latter case were furnished equally by B and another brother C, from April 1 on, and if neither A, B, nor C furnished over half the support for the calendar year, any one of them (including the executor of A's estate, on A's final return) could take the exemption if the other two would renounce their right to the exemption under a multiple support agreement for that year. 

Regardless of support, no exemption is allowed A, B, or C for the father, if the father has gross income of $600 or over during the calendar year 1957.

What Is And What Is Not The Decedent's Income 

13 

What Is and What Is Not the Decedent's Income? 

[19] Only amounts received and properly accrued are taxable to decedent.-A taxpayer's death often leaves suspended a number of incomplete transactions initiated in his lifetime. Whether money collected after death is gross income taxable to the decedent or to the estate, or is only capital, is a problem that confronts the executor or the administrator. 

When a taxpayer who was on the cash basis dies, only income actually or constructively received up to the date of death is to be included in his final return. If the decedent was on the accrual basis, his income accrued up to the date of death is to be included in his final return. But income accruable only because of his death is not to be included.20 All other amounts of income not includible in the decedent's last return are to be included, when received, in the income of the estate or person receiving such amounts by inheritance or survivorship through the decedent, regardless of whether or not such recipient reports his income on the cash or accrual basis. For further discussion of amounts taxable to the decedent's estate, heirs, or beneficiaries, see ¶ 11 below.

Example (1): A, who reported on the cash basis, was an executive in a small factory and earned $300 per week. He worked 14 weeks during the taxable year before he died. For the 8 weeks preceding his death, A was not paid, since the factory was unable to pay at that time. Two months after his death, the factory paid A's executor the 8 weeks' wages, $2,400 less deductions. A's final return will include the 6 weeks' wages of $1,800 which he actually received. 

His final return will not include the 8 weeks' wages of $2,400 which were paid to the executor after his death, but the estate's income tax return will. 

Example (2): B, a broker, keeps his books on the accrual basis. Under a contract which he has with one firm, the firm has agreed, in the event of his death, to pay his estate 25% of his last year's commissions by way of settlement for deals in process but uncompleted. He dies, and under this 25% clause the firm pays his estate $4,000. The firm also pays his estate $2,000 for commissions on completed deals. The $2,000 is includible on B's final income tax return. 

The $4,000 is not includible on that return but on the return of the estate, although B was on the accrual basis. (On the cash basis, both amounts would have been taxable only to the estate.)

A deceased partner's share of the net income of a partnership for the period ended with his death is not includible on his final return unless, under the partnership agreement, his death caused a termination of the partnership. 

For discussion on decedent partners, see [ 30-33. [10] Can decedent be taxed on income not actually received?—A taxpayer on the cash basis must usually actually receive income before tax liability is incurred. An exception to this rule, however, occurs where there is "constructive receipt" of income. Under the doctrine of constructive receipt a taxpayer is taxable on income which is credited to him without restriction, and made available to him during the taxable period, to the extent that it may be drawn upon and brought immediately within his possession or control. 

There must be no substantial limitations or conditions on this right. The constructive receipt doctrine is applicable to a decedent in determining income to be reported on his last return. 

¹ Code Sec. 691(a)(1)(A). 

20 Code Sec. 451. ¶ 10

Thus, it has been held that interest on bonds, due at the date of death but not collected because of the decedent's illness, was taxable as constructively received by a cash-basis decedent. The same rule was followed where interest coupons matured prior to a decedent's death and were not presented for payment because of his physical condition.³ 

Where an assignment or conveyance is made of income after it has been earned, the income is constructively received by the assignor at the time it is collected by the assignee (or unconditionally credited to the assignee). The receipt of a check representing income just prior to death, but in time so that the money could have been obtained before death, is income taxable on the decedent's last return, even though the check is not cashed or used until after death or another is given to replace it after death. As to when a check may be taken as a deduction by a deceased payer, see ¶ 24. [11] What amounts are taxable to the estate, heirs, legatees, etc.?————

Items of income which are not taxable to the decedent on his last return under the rules described in ¶9 and ¶ 10 above, are taxable to the party who eventually receives them. This would usually be the decedent's estate. If the estate does not collect such amounts, but distributes the right to receive them to a testamentary trust, or to the heir, next of kin, legatee, or devisee, who inherited or was bequeathed or devised such right, it would be such trust, heir, next of kin, legatee, or devisee that would be taxable on them when received. Such items are to be included in the income of the estate or other party only when received by him even though he reports income on the accrual basis. This income, which is taxed to the recipient and not to the decedent, is termed in the law "income in respect of a decedent." 5 

Example (1): A decedent, who kept his books on the cash basis, was entitled at the date of his death to a large salary payment to be made in equal annual installments over five years. His estate, after collecting two installments, distributed the right to the remaining installments to the residuary legatee of the estate. The estate must include in its gross income the two installments received by it, and the legatee must include in his gross income each of the three installments received by him.

Example (2): A claim owned by D, who reported his income on the cash basis, was in the process of litigation at the time of his death. Before administration of D's estate was complete, the claim was settled for $10,000. The $10,000 is "income in respect of a decedent” and is required to be included in the income of the estate in the taxable year when received. 

Example (3): The same facts as in Example (2) except that D's estate, which makes its returns on the cash basis and a calendar year, received $6,000 on account of the claim on February 15, 1957. On March 20, 1957, before it collects any more, the estate is wound up and the account receivable for the balance is distributed, together with other assets, to H, D's sole heir. H makes his returns on the accrual basis and a calendar year. He collects the balance on 

February 4, 1958. The estate includes the $6,000 on its 1957 return and H includes the $4,000 on his 1958 return. H is not to accrue the $4,000 in 1957, the year he was distributed the right to receive it, because it is taxable to him only in the year he receives the money even though he is on the accrual basis. 

Example (4): A decedent owned a defense bond, with his wife as co-owner or beneficiary, but died before the bond was paid. The entire amount of interest 

2 E. C. Loose, Exrx., 15 BTA 169, Dec. 4818 (Acq.). 

3 E. C. Loose, Exrx. v. U. S., (CA-8) 4 USTC ¶ 1362, 74 Fed. (2d) 147. 

4U. A. Lavery, (CA-7) 46-2 USTC 1 9406, 158 

Fed. (2d) 859.

Code Sec. 691(a)(1). 

¶ 11

accruing on the bond and not includible in income by the decedent, not just the amount of interest accruing after the death of the decedent, would be treated as income to his wife when the bond is paid. 

Example (5): X, prior to his death, acquired 10,000 shares of the capital stock of the Y Corporation at a cost of $100 per share. During his lifetime, X had entered into a contract with Y Corporation or with other shareholders whereby Y Corporation or other shareholders agreed to purchase and X agreed that his executor would sell the 10,000 shares of Y Corporation stock owned by X at the book value of the stock at the date of his death. Upon X's death, the shares are sold by X's executor for $500 a share pursuant to the agreement. 

Since the sale of stock is consummated after X's death, there is no "income in respect of a decedent" based on the appreciation in value of X's stock to the date of his death.

The rules discussed above also apply to successive decedents provided the right to the income was acquired by the later decedent by reason of the death of the prior decedent or by bequest, devise, or inheritance from the prior decedent.

Example (6): A widow acquired, by bequest from her husband, the right to receive renewal commissions on life insurance sold by him in his lifetime, which commissions were payable over a period of years. The widow died before she received all of such commissions, and her son inherited the right to receive the rest of the commissions. The commissions received by the widow were includible in her gross income. The commissions received by the son were not includible in the widow's gross income but must be included in the gross income of the son. The commissions in the son's hands represent "income in respect of a decedent," the widow being the decedent for this purpose. 

It should be noted that rights to income to be collected after death are usually part of the decedent's estate subject to Federal estate tax, whether or not such items are also subject to income tax in the hands of the recipient when received. However, the law gives partial relief from this duplication of taxes by allowing a deduction to the recipient of the income based on the increase in the estate tax attributable to the inclusion of the value of such income right in the estate. For details on this deduction, see ¶ 28. [12] How should the recipient of decedent's income treat it?—A decedent's income which is taxed to the recipient retains the same character it would have had in the hands of the decedent. Thus, if the income would have been capital gain, exempt income, or interest to the decedent, it continues to be the same kind of income to the recipient. Example (1): The estate of a decedent attorney received a fee which represented services performed by the decedent over a period of more than 36 months, and 80% of the fee was received by the estate in one taxable year. 

If the decedent had lived and included such fee in his gross income, he would have been eligible for the long-term income benefits of Code Sec. 1301. Accordingly, the estate is entitled to the same long-term income benefits and, therefore, the tax to be paid by the estate on the fee would be limited to the increase in tax that would have resulted to the decedent had he received the amount in equal monthly installments over the period of the services. 

Example (2): The decedent sold a piece of property which he had held as an investment for more than six months. He died, however, before the purchase price was paid. The executor of the estate completed the transaction. 

If the decedent had lived, the gain would have been long-term capital gain in his hands. Therefore, the estate is entitled to the same privilege. • Code Sec. 691(a)(3). ¶ 12

[13] If income rights are transferred, who and how much is taxed? 

If a right to receive an amount of income from a decedent is transferred by the owner of the right (the estate, heir, or other beneficiary) to another person, the fair market value of the right at the date of the transfer is to be included in the income of the transferor, plus the amount by which any consideration received for the transfer exceeds the fair market value of the right." 

Thus, if the right to receive the income is disposed of by sale, the fair market value of the right or the consideration received, whichever is higher, is includible in the seller's gross income. If the right is disposed of by gift, the fair market value of the right will be includible in the donor's gross income. 

Example (1): A decedent bequeaths to his son the right to rental payments from certain real estate. To obtain immediate cash, the son sells the right to receive the rental payments to a third party. The fair market value of the right at the date of transfer is assumed to be $10,000. The sales price is $8,000. 

The son will have to include in his gross income in the year of transfer $10,000, the fair market value of the right. If he had made a gift, the fair market value of the right at the time of the gift, here $10,000, would have been includible in his gross income. If he had done neither, the rental income would have been taxed to him as received.

The transfers referred to above include sales, exchanges, or other dispositions, or the satisfaction of installment obligations at other than face value. 

They do not include transmissions at death to the estate of the decedent or transfers to a person who would eventually be entitled to the income by reason of the death of the decedent by bequest, devise or inheritance from the decedent.

Example (2): A trust is bequeathed the right of the decedent to certain payments of income. If the trust terminates and the right to the payments is transferred to the beneficiary, the trust does not have to include the fair market value of the right to receive the payments in its income, but the payments are includible in the income of the beneficiary. 

It is to be noted that generally the basis of property acquired from a decedent is its fair market value at the time of the decedent's death under Code Sec. 1014. Therefore, if the property is sold by the estate or other recipient soon after death, the sales proceeds will approximate such fair market value and there will be little, if any, gain or loss. However, this basis provision is specifically made inapplicable by the Code to transfers of rights to "income in respect of a decedent" under Code Sec. 691. In other words, the fair market value of the right may not be used as a basis to be subtracted from the sales proceeds in determining gain or loss. The rule described above for such transfers is to apply. The right to such income, of course, has a value which is includible in determining the value of the estate for estate tax purposes. But the estate tax, if any, attributable to the inclusion of such value may be deducted as explained at ¶ 28. 

[14] How are installment obligations treated?—Installment obliga tions acquired from a decedent are to be treated as items of "income in respect of a decedent." Therefore, the estate, heir, or other person receiving the installment payments after the death of the decedent is to report as income the same portion of the payments which would have been taxable income to the decedent. Under the 1954 Code, unlike the 1939 Code, no gain is to be 

' Code Sec. 691(a)(2). 

8 Code Sec. 1014(c). 

• Code Sec. 691(a)(4). 13

considered realized upon the death of the decedent and includible in the - decedent's last return.

If the estate, heir, or other recipient transfers the installment obligation by sale or exchange, as described in ¶ 13 above, or the obligation is satisfied at other than face value, the transferor (estate, heir, etc.) is to include in its gross income the fair market value of the obligation at the time of the transfer or the consideration received for the transfer, whichever is greater, reduced by the basis of the obligation in the hands of the decedent. The basis in the hands of the decedent is the excess of the face value over the amount of income that would be returnable were the obligation satisfied in full. 

Example: An heir of a decedent is entitled to collect an installment obligation with a face value of $100, a fair market value of $80 and a basis in the hands of the decedent of $60. If the heir collects the obligation at face value, the excess of the amount collected over the basis is considered "income in respect of a decedent” and includible in the gross income of the heir. In this case, the amount includible would be $40. If the heir collects the obligation at $90, an amount other than face value, the entire obligation is considered a right to receive "income in respect of a decedent." The amount required to be included in the heir's gross income is the consideration received in satisfaction of the installment obligation less the amount of the basis of the obligation in the decedent's hands. In this case, the amount includible would be $30. 

Installment obligations which qualify for the above treatment are those to which the provisions of Code Sec. 453 apply. Under Code Sec. 453, income from three types of installment sales may be reported on the installment plan: (1) personal property regularly sold or otherwise disposed of on the installment plan (refers to dealers in personal property); (2) a casual sale or other casual disposition of personal property for a price exceeding $1,000; and (3) any sale or other disposition of real property. In the case of a casual sale or sale of realty (items (2) and (3) above), the installment method can be used only if the seller receives no payments or payments of 30% or less of the selling price during the year of sale. If payments during the year of sale exceed the 30% limit, the installment method cannot be used. 

Where an installment obligation is received as part of the selling price of a capital asset held in joint tenancy by a husband and wife, such obligation is held by the husband and wife as joint tenants in the same manner as the property sold and upon the death of one spouse the other takes the whole interest in the installment obligation by right of survivorship. Therefore, the surviving spouse is to report as capital gain the total outstanding balance of profit to be realized on the sale as and when realized through payments on the installment obligation made by the obligor.10 

Where property not held in joint tenancy is sold, the reporting of such sale on a joint return, combining the incomes and deductions of both husband and wife, has no effect on the ownership of the property. The spouses, although filing a joint return, continue to retain title to their respective properties. Accordingly, if one spouse dies possessed of an installment obligation from the sale of such property the estate, heir, or other person receiving any payment on the obligation is to return as income the same portion of such payment as would have been reportable by the decedent if he had lived and received such payment."1 

10 Rev. Rul. 267, 1953-2 CB 32. 

11 Rev. Rul. 267, 1953-2 CB 32. ¶ 14

[15] The decedent is not taxed on unrealized profits.-The increase in value of property occurring between the date the decedent acquired the property and the date of his death is not income taxable on the decedent's last return. This is true whether the decedent was on the cash or the accrual basis. The property merely becomes part of the decedent's estate, taxable for estate tax purposes only. 

Example (1): A decedent, on the cash basis, purchased a rare painting for $1,000. At the date of his death the painting was valued at $5,000. The increase in value of $4,000 is not income to be included on the decedent's final income tax return. The painting would become an asset of the estate, valued for estate tax purposes at $5,000. If the estate sold the painting for $6,000 it would have a taxable gain for income tax purposes of $1,000 since the basis for the painting to the estate would be its fair market value or $5,000. 

In the above example there would be no difference in so far as the decedent's last return is concerned if there had been a sale and delivery of, or at least an enforceable agreement to sell, the painting for $6,000 before the decedent's death. No profit would have been includible in his last return. In this situation, however, the estate would be the recipient of a right to "income in respect of a decedent" and therefore it would have to report the sales proceeds as income in the same manner and amount that the decedent would have had to report them had he lived. Accordingly, the total profit to be reported by the estate would be $5,000, the difference between the decedent's basis of $1,000 and the sales proceeds of $6,000. If the decedent had been on the accrual basis, the $5,000 gain on the sale of the painting would have to be included in his last return.

If a decedent operated a sole proprietorship business which involved the use of inventories, an accounting would have to be made as of the decedent's death to determine the amount of income from the business includible in his last return. In fixing inventory values at date of death, care should be taken to follow the valuation method previously used by the decedent, that is, cost, market value, farm price, etc. It will not do to adopt the valuation made for estate or inheritance tax purposes, or use other outside appraisals, since those valuations may have no relation to the decedent's opening inventory or the method that must be consistently used in computing income on the inventory basis.

Farmers. In the case of farmers, the mere raising of livestock or growing of crops does not constitute taxable income includible on the decedent's last return nor does it constitute a right to "income in respect of a decedent." To constitute "income in respect of a decedent" there must have been a sale and delivery of, or at least an enforceable agreement to sell, the livestock or crops before the death of the farmer. If there has been no sale or agreement to sell before death, the_livestock or crops become an asset of the estate for estate tax purposes. The person receiving the livestock or crops from the decedent will have as his basis the fair market value of the livestock or crops at date of death or other applicable valuation date. See further on basis, ¶ 53. 

Example (2): At the date of his death a farmer had the following property:

Livestock, raised, all the costs having been charged to expense, appraised as of the date of his death at $4,000. Wheat, ripened and ready for harvest, appraised at $5,000. Bank stock which had cost $1,200, appraised at $3,000. While the farmer's estate was still in administration the wheat was harvested and sold for $5,900. The cattle was sold for $5,100 net. The bank stock was sold for $3,000. There was a profit of $2,000, based on the appraised values at the date 15

What Is-And What Is Not The Decedent's Income 

19 of death. The profit would be taxed to the estate. If the sales had been made by the decedent's devisees, legatees, or heirs, following distribution to them of the property by the estate, the profit would have been taxable to them. 

The fair market value of a farmer's crop share, under a crop sharing agreement, whether or not received or made available to him at the time of his death, is not includible in income on his last return. The same treatment described above for livestock and crops which he himself has raised is applicable. 

No income is realized by a farmer or other producer by reason of his contribution of farm or other products to a charitable organization. However, he must remove such goods from his cost of goods sold, in order to avoid a double deduction, by an inventory adjustment effecting the removal of the donated asset and the costs pertaining thereto from the opening inventory in the year of the gift. Items of cost of the current year applicable to such property are not deductible by the donor, and similar items which have been deducted in prior years must be removed from the amount of the contribution in order to avoid a double deduction.12 The above rules apply to decedent farmers who have made such gifts prior to their death in determining the income to be reported on their last return. [16] To whom are dividends taxable?-Dividends on corporate stock are subject to tax when unqualifiedly made subject to the demand of the shareholder.13 Therefore, dividends declared before the death of a decedent but payable by the terms of the declaration to stockholders of record on dates which fall after the decedent's death are not income taxable to the decedent on his last return.14 This rule applies even though the decedent was on the accrual basis. The dividend will be income to the estate or other person when it becomes the owner of the stock and receives the dividend. 

It is possible, however, under the doctrine of constructive receipt for a decedent to be taxable on a dividend in his last return although he had not actually received the dividend. 

Example: A dividend check was available to a taxpayer (if he personally appeared and demanded it) prior to the date of his death in sufficient time to cash or deposit it. Pursuant to taxpayer's request, however, the check was mailed to him, and it did not reach his home or office until after the date of his death. 

Under the doctrine of constructive receipt the dividend would probably be taxable to the decedent on his last return.

It should be noted in connection with the above example that constructive receipt would not apply if the corporation customarily mailed the dividend checks to its shareholders. It has been held that even though a taxpayer could have received his dividend check on the last day of the year had he appeared in person, the dividend will not be taxable until received if the corporation customarily mails the dividend checks.15 This rule would appear to be equally applicable in the case of decedents. [17] When is income realized from stock purchase options?-Key employees often receive an option (other than a restricted stock option) to purchase stock of the employer at a bargain price. The U. S. Supreme Court has ruled that when he exercises the option the employee realizes taxable income, 12 Rev. Rul. 55-138, 1955-1 CB 223; Rev. Rul.

in the form of additional compensation, in an amount equal to the difference between the option price and the market value of the stock at the time the option is exercised.1 16 What are the Federal income tax consequences, however, if the employee dies before he exercises the option and the option right does not terminate with his death but passes to his estate? The Internal Revenue Service has ruled that the option in the estate's hands represents a right to "income in respect of a decedent." Accordingly, the existence of the option has no effect on the taxable income to be reflected in the decedent's last return. When the estate exercises the option or surrenders it to the decedent's employer for an amount equal to the difference between the option price and the market value of the stock, it will realize taxable income. The amount of income to be reflected on the estate's income tax return will be the difference between the market value of the stock and the option price.

For estate tax purposes, the option is also includible in the decedent's gross estate at its fair market value. However, to compensate for this duplication of taxes the estate is entitled to an income tax deduction for the estate tax paid on account of the value of the option being included in the decedent's gross estate. Restricted stock options.-Under special provisions in Code Sec. 421, employee exercises a "restricted" stock option and he does not dispose of the stock so acquired within 2 years after the option was granted nor within 6 months after he receives the stock, no income will be realized until he disposes of the stock. At that time the gain is capital gain if the option price was 95% or more of the value. If the option price is between 85% and 95%, part of the gain may be ordinary income and part may be capital gain when the stock is sold. A "restricted" stock option is, generally speaking, one where the option price is at least 85% of the fair market value of the stock, the option is not transferable except on death, and other limitations as to the amount of stock the optionee already owns are met. 

The examples below illustrate how the "restricted" stock option provisions work where an employee dies before and after he has exercised the option. 

Example (1): X corporation gives its employee an option to buy stock at $85. The fair market value of the stock at the option date is $100. The employee dies before exercising the option. The employee's estate exercises the option and one month later transfers to a beneficiary the stock when it is worth $110. The estate realizes $15 of ordinary income ($100 minus $85) and $10 is short-term capital gain. 

Example (2): On January 1, 1955, X, an employee of M Corporation, receives a "restricted" stock option to purchase a share of stock of M Corporation for $85. 

The fair market value on that date is $100. After acquiring the stock on January 

1, 1957, X dies during the year 1957, at a time when the share had a fair market value of $150. The $15 spread which existed at the time the option was granted would be included as compensation in X's gross income for the taxable year closing with his death. This is the rule whether X's death occurs during or after a 6-month period running from the date the share was acquired. The basis of the share in the estate of X would be $150, its market value at the time of death.

[18] Agreements to sell stock at death do not affect decedent's income. Stockholders, or the stockholders and the corporation, often agree that in the event of the death of one of the stockholders the surviving stockholders or the corporation may at their option purchase, and the estate will sell, decedent's stock at a stipulated price. Frequently, the stock purchase funds are provided by the proceeds of life insurance taken out on the lives of the stockholders. The agreement being only executory in nature, an actual sale does not take place until after death (if the option is exercised), and, therefore, title to the stock does not pass upon death to the surviving stockholders or corporation, which have at best only an action on the contract or for specific performance. Accordingly, no income accrues to the decedent to be included in his last return under the contract. Also, if the option is exercised, the money received for the stock by the estate or person who acquired ownership through the decedent is not "income in respect of a decedent." In other words, the money received by the estate or other person would not be taxed in the same manner as if the decedent had lived and received the money. The estate or other person would use the fair market value of the stock at the date of the decedent's death (or other applicable valuation date) in determining the gain or loss realized on the sale of the stock under the agreement. 

The above rules would apply even though there existed at the date of the decedent's death a binding agreement to buy and sell, not just an option. This is true because the decedent was not in receipt of any money under the contract prior to his death and amounts accrued only by reason of death are not includible in his final return. Also, since the decedent on the date of his death had no rights to income under the contract, the money received by the estate or other person acquiring ownership of the stock through the decedent under the contract would not be "income in respect of a decedent." [19] When should interest on U. S. savings bonds be reported?—If the holder of U. S. savings bonds is an accrual-basis taxpayer, the interest must be accrued as the bond, purchased at a discount, increases in value while held. 

A cash-basis taxpayer may also elect to treat the increase in value as income received each year. Once the election is made, it is binding for later years. 

When the holder of such bonds dies, the only amount accrued on his final return is the amount which would have been included on his return for that period if he had lived. The holder of the bonds, that is, his estate or beneficiary, reports the interest as it accrues, or as the bond increases in value from the time of the decedent's death, unless such holder is on the cash basis and has not elected to report the increment, on these bonds and any other like bonds which he may hold, as income. If a cash-basis beneficiary has not made the election, his income is unaffected until the bonds mature or are sold by him. Then he reports the entire increase in value as interest income. 

But the increase in value so included in his income is the increase from the time he acquired the bond from the decedent. The latter had reported the increase up to the date of his death on his own return.

If the cash-basis decedent did not elect to report the increment in value as income, the increment in value up to the date of his death becomes "income in respect of a decedent." It is not includible on his final return, but is reportable by his estate or his beneficiary. If they are on the cash basis and have not made the election to report the increment in value currently, they include it in income only when the bonds mature or are disposed of. In such case, the entire increment from the time the bonds were acquired by the decedent is ¶ 19

includible in their gross income as interest income. It is immaterial that such increment was taken into account in valuing the bonds for estate tax purposes.

If the estate or beneficiary is on the accrual basis, and the cash-basis decedent had not reported the increment as income, it would appear that the proper procedure for the estate or beneficiary would be to report only the increment as it accrues from the date of death. When the estate or beneficiary collects on the bond at maturity or on its sale, the increment in value up to the date of the decedent's death, would be reportable as interest income at that time. [120] Do postponement-of-gain provisions die with the decedent?—It was observed in ¶ 12 that a decedent's income which is taxed to the recipient retains the same character it would have had in the hands of the decedent. 

Does a similar principle apply so as to give the decedent or his estate the benefit of certain nonrecognition-of-gain provisions in the Code, such as 

Code Secs. 1033 and 1034?

Under Code Sec. 1033 gain is not recognized from the involuntary conversion of property if an amount equal to the proceeds from the conversion is invested in property which is similar or related in service or use to the former property. Under Code Sec. 1034, if a taxpayer sells his personal residence and purchases another, which he uses as his principal residence, within one year before or one year after the date of the sale, gain will be recognized only to the extent that the "adjusted sales price" of the old residence exceeds the cost of the new. Therefore, if the purchase price of the new residence is equal to or more than the "adjusted sales price" of the old one, no gain will be recognized on the sale of the old residence. The basis of the new residence, however, is to be reduced by the nonrecognized gain and therefore the gain on sale of the old residence is in effect postponed until the replacement residence is later sold. For purposes of determining whether these postponement-ofgain provisions apply to decedents, it must be assumed that the decedent died before the newly contemplated property is acquired, but that it is acquired after his death by his legal representative, or by his heirs, devisees, and legatees.

As far as the involuntary conversion statute is concerned, the Tax Court has taken the view that the replacement requirements of Code Sec. 1033 are satisfied only if carried out in the taxpayer's lifetime, and that replacement by the executor of the estate of a deceased taxpayer, although satisfying all other requirements of the statute, is not enough to enable the estate to claim the statutory benefit. However, the Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit reversed the Tax Court, holding that the right to postpone recognition of gain on an involuntary conversion does not die with the taxpayer. The recognition of gain may be postponed if the property taken in the involuntary conversion is replaced by the estate or other person after the death of the taxpayer, provided replacement is made within the required period.18

To date there have been no rulings or decisions on the question of whether the right to postpone recognition of gain on the sale of a personal residence dies with the taxpayer. The requirements of Code Sec. 1034, however, are more stringent than those of Code Sec. 1033 and lead to the belief that the benefits of Code Sec. 1034 will usually not survive the taxpayer's death. This 

18 I. Goodman, 17 TC 1017, Dec. 18,673, rev'd by CA-3, 52-2 USTC ¶ 9556, 199 Fed. (2d) 895. ¶ 20

What Is-And What Is Not The Decedent's Income 

23 would appear to be true at least where the estate purchases the new residence, because the Commissioner has ruled that where the title to the new residence is not in the name of the seller of the old residence, the nonrecognition-of-gain provisions of Code Sec. 1034 will not apply.19 Moreover, the Commissioner has also ruled that where a trust is the purchaser of a new residence to be occupied by a beneficiary, the trust is the taxpayer and it cannot qualify as a person who used the property as its principal residence.20 This should also apply with equal force to an estate. 

If the new residence is purchased by the surviving spouse, however, it is possible that the gain may be postponed under Code Sec. 1034. This is so because a husband and wife may be regarded as one taxpayer for the purposes of Code Sec. 1034. This means that, regardless of which spouse owned the old residence which is sold (it could have been owned by only one of them, or as joint tenants, or as tenants in common) or which one buys the new residence, any gain on the sale of the old residence will not be recognized if the requirements of Code Sec. 1034 are met. To be eligible for such treatment the spouses must file consents to corresponding adjustments to basis. In the case of a decedent, a consent filed by the administrator or executor of the estate on his behalf would probably meet that requirement. Permitting gain to be postponed in this situation does not appear to do an injustice to the intent behind the statute and, in fact, would be an equitable treatment. [21] What about salary payments to widow or estate?-When a widow or the decedent's estate receives payments from the decedent's former employer, taxability of the sums received may turn upon the application of several provisions of the Code. The amounts received may be fully taxable under 

Code Sec. 61 as gross income, they may be taxable as annuities under Code 

Sec. 72, or they may be excluded as gifts under Code Sec. 102.

There is a question, which as yet has not been definitely answered, as to whether the payments constitute "income in respect of a decedent" within the meaning of Code Sec. 691 and are therefore includible in the gross income of the estate or in the gross income of the person who acquired the right to receive the payments. Thus, if the widow of a decedent or his estate received amounts by reason of a contract entered into by the decedent, or by reason of a statutory guarantee, it might be argued that the widow or estate has a right to such payments, and under Code Sec. 691 would be taxable thereon. 

This principle has at least been applied by the Commissioner to Government leave pay. He has ruled that a lump-sum payment for accumulated leave on behalf of a deceased Government employee is taxable as salary or compensation for services. If the employee dies and such sum is not includible in his gross income for the taxable period in which death occurred, then it is includible in the gross income of the widow or other beneficiary, or by the decedent's estate, entitled to receive the payment in the taxable year received under Code Sec. 691(a).¹ If the payments were taxable to the widow or estate under Code Sec. 691, up to $5,000 of such payments would probably qualify for the death benefit exclusion under Code Sec. 101(b).

The Commissioner holds that payments made by an employer to the widow of a deceased officer or employee, in consideration of services rendered by the officer or employee, are includible in the widow's gross income. Again, 1 Rev. Rul. 55-229, 1955-1 CB 75.

3 The Tax Court has been more liberal than the Commissioner, and holds that where a payment to a widow is a gift, it remains a gift although the basic reason for the payment is the deceased employee's past association with the employer. The Tax Court has also held that a payment to the estate is a gift, where it is made to the estate rather than the widow in order that it would "result in benefit and comfort to all members of his family who were remembered by him under his will." sa Facts which control the finding of a gift intent are (1) there is no legal obligation to make the payments, (2) the employer derives no benefit, and (3) the widow or estate performs no services. [22] There is no tax on life insurance proceeds.-The proceeds of life insurance which are received under a life insurance contract and are paid by reason of the death of the insured are exempt from tax. The exemption applies to proceeds of life insurance purchased for employees by employers, whether under a group policy or individual employee policies, as well as life insurance policies purchased by an individual for himself. It also applies to proceeds received under an endowment contract when they are payable to a beneficiary if the insured dies prior to maturity of the contract. 

The exemption applies regardless of who receives the proceeds. An individual, partnership, or corporate beneficiary are alike nontaxable. 

The manner is which the proceeds are paid is also of no consequence. 

Lump-sum payments are exempt and so are installment payments. However, if installment payments include an element of interest, the interest will have to be included in income in accordance with a special statutory formula.5 

But installment payments of insurance on the life of a decedent who died on or before August 16, 1954 are fully exempt since the special formula does not apply to them.

Where the life insurance proceeds are held by the insurer under an agreement to pay interest thereon, the interest payments must be included in gross income. For example, where the proceeds are left with the insurer under an agreement to make annual payments to the beneficiary for life in the form of interest and to pay the principal sum to a second beneficiary on death of the first beneficiary, the payments to the first beneficiary are taxable as interest since they are only income from the insurance proceeds, rather than the proceeds themselves.

Where a life insurance contract provides for optional modes of settlement at the election of the insured or the beneficiary, it does not matter which party makes the election. The proceeds will still be exempt because they will be made under the insurance contract and by reason of the death of the insured. 

If the life insurance policy provides no option and the beneficiary and the insurer, by supplemental agreement, arrange for the beneficiary to receive installment payments, the installment payments will not be exempt as life * A. W. Hellstrom Est., 24 TC 916, Dec. 21,191;

insurance death proceeds but will be excludable in accordance with the annuity rules of Code Sec. 72. The reason for this different treatment is that the installments would not be paid under a life insurance contract by reason of the death of the insured. They would be paid under the provisions of the supplee mental agreement, other than by reason of the death of the insured. 

Where a life insurance policy is transferred for a valuable consideration, 1 the proceeds of the policy paid to the transferee by reason of the insured's death are exempt only to the extent of the consideration paid for the transfer plus the amount of premiums and other sums subsequently paid by the transferee. This transferee rule, however, does not apply where a policy is trans- + ferred as a gift or generally in connection with a tax-free exchange. It also does not apply if the transfer of the contract was to the insured, a partner of the insured, a partnership including the insured, or a corporation of which the insured was a shareholder or officer. In such cases, therefore, the proceeds are entirely exempt.'

By specific provision (Sec. 3 of P. L. 262, 74th Cong., 38 U. S. C. 454a) 

none of the proceeds or the cash surrender value of converted "veterans' war risk insurance" policies is taxable either to the veteran or to his beneficiary. [22.01] Annuities. If a taxpayer was receiving annuity payments prior to his death, only the annuity payments actually received up to the date of his death, less the exclusion allowable under the annuity rules of Code Sec. 72, will be includible in his income to be reported on his last return. If the annuity was a joint and survivorship annuity, the survivor annuitant will continue to use the same "exclusion ratio" that the decedent was using in computing the portion of the annuity payments he receives which is to be included in and excluded from his own income. However, to compensate for the increase in estate tax attributable to the inclusion of the value of the survivor's interest in the contract in the deceased annuitant's estate, the survivor annuitant is permitted a special deduction. This deduction is explained at ¶ 29 below. If the annuity was of the refund type, the refund payment in the hands of the beneficiary will generally not be subject to tax because it normally will only represent the remaining unrecovered cost of the deceased annuitant. 

The 1954 Code contains a special exclusion rule for proceeds of an annuity, endowment or life insurance contract received by an employee as an annuity where a part of the consideration for the contract was contributed by the employer and a part by the employee. In the case of such a contract, if during the first three years in which payments under the contract are made, the total amount receivable by the employee will equal or exceed contributions made by the employee, then the employee's receipts are to be excluded from gross income until his consideration has been recovered. All payments theret after will be fully taxable. The rule applies to a beneficiary of an employee !provided the employee dies before receiving any amount as an annuity under the contract.8 [¶ 23] Income taxes of servicemen may be abated upon death. The income of servicemen dying during the induction period while in active service in a combat zone or as a result of wounds, disease, or injury incurred t while so serving is exempt from tax. The forgiveness applies to the year of death and prior years ending on or after the first day of service in a combat E zone after June 24, 1950. Moreover, any taxes for other prior years which

& Code Sec. 72(d). 

⁹ Code Sec. 692.

are unpaid at the date of death are abated. It should be noted, however, that this forgiveness provision has no present application because there are no combat zones now in existence. 

The forgiveness of tax covers income received by the serviceman's estate or others during the balance of what would have been his full taxable year.10 

However, the forgiveness provision does not apply to a serviceman's liability as a transferee.¹¹ 

If a deceased serviceman filed a joint return with his wife, the tax for such year is abated, credited or refunded in an amount which is the same percentage as a tax upon his income computed upon a separate return is of the sum of the taxes on their individual incomes computed on separate returns. 

If a serviceman and his wife filed a joint declaration of estimated tax for the taxable year ending with his death, the estimated tax paid may be treated as the estimated tax of either of them, or may be divided between them, in such manner as his legal representative and such spouse may agree. Should they agree to treat such estimated tax, or any portion thereof, as the estimated tax of the serviceman, the estimated tax so paid is to be credited or refunded as an overpayment for the taxable year ending with his death. 

The abatement upon death is applied to those who are in "active service," meaning those persons who are actually functioning in such service. This definition does not require that such person be in the field or in a theatre of war. Personnel in the inactive reserve or on retirement are not in active service. Periods during which a person is absent from duty on account of sickness, wounds, leave, internment by the enemy, or other lawful cause are periods of active service.

How Are Deductions of the Decedent to Be Treated? 

[24] Who gets deductions not allowable on last return? The deductions of a decedent are treated in the same way as was outlined in ¶9 for income items. If the decedent was on the cash basis, only those items of expense, interest, and taxes actually paid up to the date of death are deductible on the decedent's last return. If the decedent was on the accrual basis, only those deductions properly accruable up to the date of death are to be shown on the final return. But any amount accrued as a deduction only because of his death is not to be included.12 

Expenses, interest, and taxes, if not deductible on the decedent's last return, are usually deductible by the estate when paid. However, if the estate is not liable for their payment, and they are paid by an heir or legatee who received property subject to them, then he is entitled to the deduction.¹³ 

Example (1): A decedent, on the cash basis, was at the time of his death the owner of rental property (real estate). Prior to his death he incurred certain repair expenses all of which were allowable deductions. However, the expenses remained unpaid at the time of his death. Also, the general taxes and assessments on the property remained unpaid for several years and were then a lien against the property, in addition to a mortgage lien existing since the date of purchase, on which mortgage the interest was in arrears. 

10 J. P. Marcelle v. R. Lupia Est., (S. Ct.) 

55-1 USTC ¶ 49,097, 348 U. S. 956. 

11 Rite-Way Products, 12 TC 475, Dec. 16,890. 

12 Code Sec. 461. 

18 Code Sec. 691(b)(1).

How Are Deductions of the Decedent to Be Treated 

27 The repair expenses would be deductible by the estate when paid by it, since they are general expenses. The lien obligations, however, would be deductible only by the heir (or devisee) when paid by him, if the property passed directly to the beneficiary under state law. However, if under state law the property became a part of the decedent's estate before it passed to the beneficiary, then the estate would be liable for the lien obligations and would be entitled to the deduction if paid during administration even though the beneficiary actually makes payment. 

If the decedent in the above example had been on the accrual basis at the time of his death, all of the items mentioned would be deductible only in the decedent's last return. However, even on the accrual basis, situations might exist where the items would be similarly deducted as shown in the example, as where, for instance, the current taxes were unpaid at death because of a dispute as to their legality, and they were compromised and paid after the decedent's death. In such case the taxes are deductible by the estate or other person in accordance with the provisions of Code Sec. 691 (b)(1). 

Similar treatment is given to the foreign tax credit provided by Code 

Sec. 33.

Example (2): John Jones died and left to his son the right to income from a factory he owned in France subject to an income tax imposed by France during the father's life, which tax had to be satisfied out of such income. The son is entitled to the foreign tax credit under Code Sec. 33 when he pays this obligation.

While Code Sec. 691(b) (1) specifically allows deduction for interest on a debt "in respect of a decedent" when paid by either the estate or the beneficiary, it is only the interest accrued before death, not properly deductible in the decedent's last return, that is within the purview of that section. It has no application, for instance, where the debt is the Federal estate tax, since such tax is not a debt of the decedent. The liability for a Federal estate tax, however, may be "transferred" to the recipient of the property to the extent of the assets received, where such liability is determined under Code Sec. 6901. 

The deduction by the estate or other person of items unpaid by the decedent does not include all of the allowable deductions. It includes only business expenses, non-trade or non-business expenses, interest, and taxes. 

As to medical expenses, see ¶ 26. As to depletion, see ¶ 27. 

Generally speaking, a payment by check is considered as payment as of the time the check is delivered to the payee, provided the check is thereafter honored by the bank upon which it is drawn.14 Also, a District Court has held that the date of mailing a check would be the date of payment for cash-basis taxpayers.15 These principles would appear to be equally applicable to decedents. Accordingly, if a check in payment of a deductible item was delivered to the payee or mailed prior to the date of the decedent's death, the deduction would be allowable on the decedent's last return even though the check is not cashed or deposited until after the decedent's death. However, if the check is not honored by the bank upon which it was drawn, it would not be deductible on the decedent's last return. [25] A decedent's losses are deductible.-Losses are ordinarily deductible only for the year in which sustained. If, for instance, stock or debts became worthless during the period covered by the decedent's last return, the loss 

15 Eli B. Witt Est. v. Fahs, (DC, Fla.) 56-1 

USTC 9534.

cannot be taken in the estate's return on the ground that worthlessness was not discovered until appraisal was made for estate tax purposes.' 16 The loss is to be deducted on the decedent's last return. If necessary an amended return should be filed for the decedent to claim the loss later discovered. 

Other losses to be considered as deductible include those due to fire, storm, shipwreck, or other casualty, or from theft. A casualty loss is to be deducted in the year the loss occurred if the extent of the damage is fixed. The fact the taxpayer has an action for damages against another party does not postpone the year in which deduction should be claimed. Where the loss is in part covered by insurance, the Internal Revenue Service says the loss should be computed by deducting from the total loss the estimated amount of recoverable insurance or other recoverable compensation, and the loss as so determined should be deducted in the year in which the casualty occurred. If subsequent events show that the estimate was substantially inaccurate, an amended return or refund claim correcting the mistake should be filed.17 

This treatment is not in accord with some judicial authority. There have been decisions to the effect that where the loss is covered in part by insurance and the insurance company has not agreed as to the extent of its liability, the loss is deductible in the taxable year when the insurance claim is finally settled.18 [26] Extra benefit for decedent's medical expenses.-Ordinarily, medical expenses are deductible only if paid within the taxable year, and this is true irrespective of whether the taxpayer is on the cash or the accrual basis. However, under a special provision in the 1954 Code, if medical expenses for the care of a taxpayer are paid by his estate within one year after he dies, they are treated as having been paid by the decedent when incurred.19 This means that a deceased taxpayer's medical expenses are deductible on the return for the year in which they were incurred. Ordinarily, this will be the year for which the decedent's last return is filed. However, it will also permit the filing of an amended return or refund claim for a year for which a refund or deficiency assessment is not barred by the statute of limitations. 

Example: In 1956, A incurred but did not pay doctor and hospital bills totaling $400 for personal medical treatment. He filed his return for the calendar year 1956 on April 15, 1957, and paid the tax shown to be due. A dies in January, 1958, without having paid the doctor and hospital bills. His estate pays them in April, 1958. An amended return (or refund claim) can be filed for 1956, and the allowable medical expenses can be deducted in that year, prior to expiration of the statute of limitations-ordinarily 3 years from the due date of the return, which in this case was April 15, 1957.

The allowance of a deduction of a decedent's medical expenses in the year in which they were incurred applies only to expenses incurred for the deceased taxpayer himself. This provision has no application to medical expenses which a decedent may have incurred on behalf of a dependent. The statute expressly provides such treatment only for "expenses for the medical care of the taxpayer." The above provision also does not apply if the decedent's medical expenses have been deducted for estate tax purposes. To get an income tax deduction for such expenses, therefore, the estate must file a statement to the effect that the medical expenses have not been claimed or
