[27] Depreciation and depletion-when and how much?-Since the decedent ceases as a tax entity for all purposes from the moment of his death, depreciation allowances on depreciable property are computed to that time, and deduction is taken for that amount in his last return. Thus, the depreciation deduction of the decedent will be a prorated amount, according to the number of months covered by his final return, of the total annual depreciation which would have been allowable to the decedent had he lived. 

After the decedent's death, the estate of the decedent, being a new entity, files a return for the period beginning with the date of death and ending with the close of the calendar or fiscal year elected by it. As far as the estate is concerned, the allowable depreciation deduction is to be apportioned between the estate and the heirs, legatees, and devisees on the basis of income of the estate allocable to each.20 The estate will also have a new basis for depreciation, for which see ¶ 53 below.

The deduction for percentage depletion is allowable only to the person who receives the income in respect of the decedent to which the deduction relates, whether or not such person receives the property from which such income is derived.¹ Thus, an heir who (by reason of the decedent's death) received income derived from sales of units of mineral by the cash-basis decedent shall be allowed the deduction for percentage depletion, computed on the gross income from such number of units as if the heir had the same economic interest in the property as the decedent. Such heir need not receive any interest in the mineral property other than such income. According to the 

Commissioner's proposed regulations (§ 1.691(b)-1(b)), if the decedent did not compute his deduction for depletion on the basis of percentage depletion, any deduction for depletion to which the decedent was entitled at the date of his death would be allowable in computing his taxable income for his last taxable year, and there can be no deduction in respect of the decedent by any other person for such depletion. 

As to deduction of depreciation and depletion allowances by estates, trusts and beneficiaries, see ¶ 62 and ¶ 63 below. [28] Special estate tax deduction for recipient of decedent's income.- 

If a person is required to include in his own income for any taxable year an item of income in respect of a decedent or a prior decedent, which item was also included in the valuation of the decedent's estate for estate tax purposes, such person may deduct for the same taxable year that portion of the estate tax imposed upon the decedent's estate which is attributable to the inclusion in the decedent's estate of the right to receive such amount.² 

For this purpose, the valuation of the income items included in the gross estate can be no greater than the amount included in the recipient's gross income, and is to be reduced by the deductions and credits in respect of the decedent used in computing the net estate. The balance is the "net value" of income items which is then used to determine the estate tax attributable to such items.

Example: Suppose a physician who reported on a cash basis dies, leaving a taxable estate of $200,000, including uncollected fees of $20,000, valued for estate tax purposes at $18,000. Suppose also he leaves unpaid expenses, interest and taxes totaling $5,000 which would have been deductible by him if he had paid them, and which are deducted as claims on the estate tax return. The net value of all these items for estate tax purposes is $18,000 less $5,000, or $13,000. If the taxable estate had not included this $13,000, the estate tax would have been reduced by about 30% (the applicable rate for a taxable estate of $200,000) of that amount, or $3,900. Thus, the estate tax attributable to the entire net value of $13,000 would be $3,900.

Suppose now that of the uncollected fees of $20,000, the estate collected in its first taxable year $17,500 on accounts which had a face value of $18,000 and which were valued for estate tax purposes at $17,000. Suppose also that the remaining accounts, having a face value of $2,000, and a value for estate tax purposes of $1,000, were distributed to X, the sole legatee. Suppose further that 

X collected $500 on those accounts in a subsequent year and that nothing more was collectible. The deduction for estate tax on the income tax return of the estate for the year in which it collected the $17,500 would be computed as follows:

This deduction is in addition to the deduction either has on account of payment of expenses, interest, or taxes of the decedent.

Where income in respect of a decedent is payable to an estate or trust, such income is to be treated as income of beneficiaries who actually receive it rather than as income of the estate or trust for purposes of determining the deductibility of estate taxes. It is treated as income to the beneficiaries for this purpose, however, only if it is properly paid, credited, or required to be distributed to them during the taxable year.

[29] Special deduction for survivor annuitant.-Annuity payments received by a surviving annuitant under a joint and survivor annuity contract are treated, to a certain extent, as income in respect of a decedent for the purpose of allowing the deduction for estate tax. This applies, however, only if the deceased annuitant died after December 31, 1953, and after the annuity starting date.

The portion of estate tax attributable to the survivor's annuity is allowable as a deduction to the surviving annuitant over his life expectancy period. 

If the surviving annuitant continues to receive annuity payments beyond this period, there is no further deduction. If the surviving annuitant dies before expiration of such period, there is no compensating adjustment for the unused deduction. ¶ 29

PART II-DEATH OF PARTNERSHIP MEMBER 

3 [30] What income is reported in deceased partner's last return?-For 

Federal income tax purposes, the death of a partner does not in itself terminate the partnership (even though it might, under State law) nor does it close the partnership's taxable year. The partnership's taxable year, as far as the deceased partner is concerned, continues uninterrupted to its regular closing date. Accordingly, a partner's death does not require a computation of his share of partnership income, actual or estimated, up to the time of his death, as is required when a partner sells his interest or retires during the year. 

However, such a computation may be necessary for estate or inheritance tax purposes in order to evaluate the partnership interest. 

Even if there were only two partners and one of them dies, the partnership is not considered as terminated if the estate or other successor in interest of the deceased partner continues to share in the profits or losses of the partnership business. This is true even while the partnership business is being liquidated, as long as the payments to the estate or successor in interest are being made. In short, as long as the deceased partner's successors in interest are receiving payments from the partnership they are regarded as partners.

Under the above rules, therefore, the last return of a decedent partner is to include only his share of partnership taxable income for the partnership taxable year or years ending within or with his last taxable year, which would be the short taxable year ending with the date of his death. The distributive share of partnership taxable income for the partnership taxable year ending after the decedent's last taxable year is to be included in the return of the estate or other successor in interest. If the estate or other successor in interest of a deceased partner continues to share in the profits and losses of the partnership business, the distributive share thereof is to be included in the taxable year of the estate or other successor in interest within or with which the taxable year of the partnership ends. 

To the extent that any part of a distributive share of partnership income of the estate or other successor in interest of a deceased partner is attributable to the decedent for the period ending with the date of his death, such part of the distributive share is to be treated as "income in respect of a decedent" under Code Sec. 691. This treatment is applicable even though before the partner's death, he withdrew all or a portion of his share of the current year's earnings.'

Example (1): B has a taxable year ending December 31 and is a member of partnership ABC. The partnership's taxable year ends on June 30. B dies on October 31, 1957. His estate (which can adopt any taxable year) adopts a taxable year ending October 31. B's last return for the period January 1 to

October 31, 1957, will include only his distributive share of taxable income of the partnership for its taxable year ending June 30, 1957. The distributive share of taxable income of the partnership for its taxable year ending June 30, 1958, arising from the interest of the decedent B, will be includible in the return of the estate for its taxable year ending October 31, 1958. That part of the distributive share attributable to the decedent B for the period ending with the date of his death (July 1 through October 31, 1957) is "income in respect of a decedent." 

3 Code Secs. 706(c) and 708. 

* Reg. § 1.708-1(b) (1) (i) (a). 

'Reg. § 1.736-1(a)(6). 

Reg. § 1.706-1(c)(3). 

Reg. §§ 1.706-1(c)(3)(v) and 1.753-1(b). 

30

[ 30.01] Exception where purchase agreement in effect. Where the decedent partner's estate or other successor sells or exchanges its entire interest in the partnership, then, instead of following the above described general rule, the partnership taxable year is closed on the date of such sale or exchange in so far as the estate or other successor in interest is concerned. 

However, the sale or exchange of a partnership interest does not, for this purpose, include any transfer of a partnership interest which occurs at death as a result of inheritance or any testamentary disposition. Also, the partnership taxable year, in so far as the estate or other successor in interest is concerned, is closed on the date the liquidation of its entire interest in the partnership is completed.8 

Example (2): Assume the same facts as in Example (1) except that, prior to B's death, B and D had agreed that, upon B's death, D would purchase B's interest for $10,000. When B dies on October 31, 1957, the partnership taxable year beginning July 1, 1957, closes with respect to him. Therefore, the return for B's last taxable year (January 1 to October 31, 1957) will include his distributive share of taxable income of the partnership for its taxable year ending June 30, 1957, plus his distributive share of partnership taxable income for the period July 1 to October 31, 1957.

Example (3): H is a member of a partnership having a taxable year ending 

December 31. Both H and his wife W are on a calendar-year basis. H dies on 

March 31, 1957. Administration of the estate is completed and the estate, including the partnership interest, is distributed to W as legatee on November 30, 1957. Such distribution by the estate is not a sale or exchange of H's partnership interest. No part of the taxable income of the partnership for the taxable year ending December 31, 1957, which is allocable to H, will be included in H's taxable income for his last taxable year (January 1 through March 31, 1957) or in the taxable income of H's estate for the taxable year April 1 through November 30, 1957. The distributive share of partnership taxable income for the full calendar year that is allocable to H will be includible in the taxable income of W for her taxable year ending December 31, 1957, and she may file a joint return. That part of the distributive share attributable to the decedent for the period ending with the date of his death (January 1 through March 31, 1957) is "income in respect of a decedent."

Where a partnership agreement designates a person to succeed to the interest of a partner after his death, the person so designated is to be regarded as a successor in interest for purposes of the above rules. Therefore, where a partner designates his widow as the successor in interest her distributive share of income for the taxable year of the partnership ending within or with her taxable year may be included in a joint return.⁹ 

Example (4): M is a member of partnership JKM which operates on a calendar year. M and his wife S file joint returns for calendar years. In the partnership agreement, M designated S to succeed to his interest in the partnership upon his death. M, who had withdrawn $10,000 from the partnership before his death, dies on October 20, 1957. S's distributive share of income for the taxable year 1957 is $15,000 ($10,000 of which represents the amount withdrawn by M). S shall include $15,000 in her income, even though M received $10,000 of this amount before his death. S may file a joint return with M for the year 1957. That part of the $15,000 distributive share attributable to the decedent for the period ending with the date of his death (January 1 through 

October 20, 1957) is “income in respect of a decedent." 

8 Reg. § 1.706-1(c)(3)(i) and (iv). 

• Reg. § 1.706-1(c)(3) (iii). ¶ 30.01

Death of Partnership Member 

35 [31] How are payments to deceased partner's successor in interest treated? The Code 10 contains specific rules on the treatment of payments made to the estate or other successor in interest of a deceased partner in liquidation of its entire interest in the partnership. The provisions are intended to clarify the extent to which such payments should be regarded as a distribution of the deceased partner's share of the profits and to what extent they are capital gain from the disposition of his capital interest. It is to be remembered that the rules discussed below have no application where the estate or other successor in interest of a deceased partner continues as a partner in its own right under local law. If such were the case the estate or other successor would merely continue to report its distributive share in accordance with the general partnership rules. 

The law makes it clear that capital gain benefits will be available only to the extent that the payments are for an interest in partnership property. 

But payment for an interest in partnership property does not include amounts paid for unrealized receivables or good will in excess of its partnership basis, except to the extent that the partnership agreement provides for a payment with respect to good will. In the event that any of the payments are allocable to the deceased partner's interest in substantially appreciated inventory items, to this extent they will be treated as amounts received from the disposition of a noncapital asset thereby resulting in ordinary gain.¹¹ 

Payments in liquidation of the entire interest of a partner through death are divided into three categories. These categories are as follows: (1) Payments of a distributive share, (2) Guaranteed payments, and (3) Payment for the partnership interest.

The treatment of payments which are not made for an interest in the partnership depends upon whether or not they are determined by reference to the income of the partnership. If they are so determined, they are treated as a distributive share to the estate or other successor in interest of the deceased partner. If not, they are treated as guaranteed payments, that is, payments for services or the use of capital. Thus, if the payments consist of a percentage of partnership income, they are excludable from the gross income of the remaining partners. If they are not so determined, the effect is the same, because they are deductible as business expenses in determining partnership taxable income. In either event the payment will be treated as ordinary income, and not as capital gain, in the hands of the estate or other successor in interest of the deceased partner. The above rules apply irrespective of the length of time over which the payments are to be made. 

Payments to the estate or other successor in interest of a deceased partner for an interest in the partnership's assets are not deductible by the remaining partners because they represent either a distribution or a purchase of the deceased partner's capital interest by the partnership, composed of the remaining partners. 

[31.01] Allocation of payments.-The Commissioner's regulations 12 

provide rules for segregating payments which are in exchange for the interest in partnership property, and treated as a distribution, from those which are regarded as a distributive share or those which are treated as guaranteed 

10 Code Sec. 736. 

11 Reg. §§ 1.736-1(b) and 1.751-1(b). 

12 Reg. 1.736-1(b) (5) (1). ¶ 31.01

payments. If a fixed amount (whether or not it is supplemented by additional amounts) is to be received over a fixed number of years, the portion of each payment to be treated as in payment for the recipient's share in partnership property will bear the same ratio to the total fixed agreed payment for the taxable year (as distinguished from the amount actually received) as the total fixed agreed payments for the interest in property bear to the total agreed payments to be received. The balance is a distributive share, if payable from profits, or a guaranteed payment, if payable without reference to profits. If the total amount received in any one year is less than the amount considered as a distribution of a share in partnership property, any unapplied portion is to be added to the portion of the payments for the following year or years which is to be treated as a distribution of the share of such property.

Example: The estate of a deceased partner is entitled to an annual payment of $6,000 for ten years for his interest in partnership property. It received only $3,500 in 1957. In 1958, it receives $10,000. Of this amount $8,500 may be treated as a distribution for an interest in partnership property—that is, 1958's $6,000 and the $2,500 shortage carried over from 1957. The $1,500 remaining is treated as a guaranteed payment, if it is determined and paid without regard to 1958 profits of the partnership; otherwise, as a distributive share. 

If the payments are not fixed in amount, they are to be treated, first, as in payment for an interest in partnership property to the extent of the value of that interest, and thereafter as payments of a distributive share of profits or guaranteed payments, as the case may be.

In place of the above rules, allocation may be made in any manner to which the estate or other successor and the remaining partners agree, but payments for an interest in partnership property are limited to the fair market value of that property at the date of the deceased partner's death. [32] Partner receiving "income in respect of a decedent."-If a payment to the estate or other successor in interest of a deceased partner represents a distributive share or a guaranteed payment, under the rules explained in ¶ 31 above, such payment is to be treated as "income in respect of a decedent." 13 This means that the payment will be taxable to the estate or other successor when received and the estate tax attributable to the inclusion of the right to such amount in the deceased partner's estate will be allowed as a deduction. Thus, while the estate or other successor in interest of a deceased partner will be required to include in gross income amounts received from the partnership which are attributable to the value of the decedent's interest in unrealized fees or mutual insurance, the recipient will at the same time receive a deduction for the estate tax paid with respect to the inclusion of such rights to income in the decedent's estate. 

The estate or other successor in interest of a deceased partner will also be treated as receiving "income in respect of a decedent" to the extent that amounts are paid to him by a third person in exchange for rights to future distributive share payments or guaranteed payments. 

If a partner dies while he is receiving payments from the partnership in liquidation of his partnership interest, any remaining payments which are guaranteed payments or distributive shares are treated as "income in respect of a decedent." 

18 Code Sec. 753 and Reg. § 1.753-1(a). 

¶ 32

Death of Partnership Member 

37 As already stated in 30 above, when a partner dies, the entire portion of the distributive share which is attributable to the period ending with the date of his death and which is taxable to his estate or other successor constitutes "income in respect of a decedent." This rule applies even though that part of the distributive share for the period before death, which. the decedent withdrew, is not included in the value of the decedent's partnership interest for estate tax purposes.¹4

Example: A and the decedent B were equal partners in a business having assets (other than money) worth $40,000 with an adjusted basis of $10,000. 

Certain partnership business was well advanced towards completion before B's death and, after B's death but before the end of the partnership year, payment of $10,000 was made to the partnership for such work. The partnership agreement provided that, upon the death of one of the partners, all partnership property, including unfinished work, would pass to the surviving partner, and that the surviving partner would pay the estate of the decedent the undrawn balance of his share of partnership earnings to the date of death, plus $10,000 in each of the three years after death. B's share of earnings to the date of his death was $4,000, of which he had withdrawn $3,000. B's distributive share of partnership income of $4,000 to the date of his death is "income in respect of a decedent" (although only the $1,000 undrawn at B's death will be reflected in the value of B's partnership interest on B's estate tax return). Assume that the value of B's interest in partnership property at date of his death was $22,000, composed of the following items: B's one-half share of the assets of $40,000, plus $2,000, B's interest in partnership cash. It should be noted that B's $1,000 undrawn share of earnings to the date of his death is not a separate item but will be paid from partnership assets. Under the partnership agreement, A is to pay B's estate a total of $31,000. The difference of $9,000 between the amount to be paid by A ($31,000) and the value of B's interest in partnership property ($22,000) constitutes "income in respect of a decedent," it being considered to be a guaranteed payment. However, the $17,000 difference between the $5,000 basis for B's share of the partnership property and its $22,000 value at the date of his death does not constitute "income in respect of a decedent." 

If, before the close of the partnership taxable year, A pays B's estate $11,000, of which they agree to allocate $3,000 to the guaranteed payment, B's estate will include $7,000 in its gross income (B's $4,000 distributive share plus $3,000 as a guaranteed payment). The remaining $8,000 of the $11,000 represents a payment for B's partnership interest of which 17,000/22,000, or $6,181.82, is capital gain. In computing the estate tax deduction under Code 

Sec. 691(c), the $7,000 will be considered as the value for estate tax purposes of such "income in respect of a decedent," even though only $4,000 ($1,000 of the distributive share not withdrawn, plus $3,000, the guaranteed payment) of this amount can be identified on the estate tax return as part of the partnership interest.

Of the $10,000 collected in each of the next two years, $3,000 would be taxable to the estate as ordinary income, assuming the parties agreed that such amount should be allocated to the guaranteed payment. The remaining $7,000 would represent a payment for B's partnership interest and again 17,000/22,000 or $5,409.09 would be taxable as capital gain. 1 [33] What about payments for good will?-Good will may be included in the property of the partnership for which the estate or other successor in interest of a decedent partner may receive payment from the partnership (which can result in capital gain instead of ordinary income) under one of two conditions. One is if it was bought by the partnership or otherwise acquired in a transaction resulting in a monetary basis to the partnership. 

14 Reg. $1.753-1(b). ¶ 33

In such a case, a payment for good will can be regarded as a payment for partnership property (resulting in capital gain or loss instead of ordinary income or deductions) to the extent of the partnership basis. Or the payment may be regarded as a payment for partnership property to the extent that it represents a reasonable value of good will, if the partnership agreement provides that upon the death of a partner his estate or other successor in interest be paid for his share of the good will. 

The figure for good will may be stated in the partnership agreement, or the reasonable amount may be determined at the time of the death of the partner. The latter is probably more feasible, since a valuation agreed upon at the time of formation of the partnership may have decreased to zero at the time a partner dies, if the partnership is unsuccessful, or may have greatly increased if the partnership is highly successful. The value may be a round sum agreed upon by all the partners, or it may be determined by use of a formula in the partnership agreement or informally agreed upon by the parties at the time of the death of one of the partners. 

Again, it is emphasized that payments by the partnership for good will cannot be treated as payments for an interest in partnership property if the partnership had no basis for good will unless the partnership agreement provides for payment for good will to the estate or other successor in interest of a deceased partner. It is only in such a case that A. R. M. 34 (explained below) or any other formula can help the estate or other successor in interest of a deceased partner by permitting it to treat the payment as capital gain instead of ordinary income.

What if the partnership does have a basis for good will acquired by it for valuable consideration, from outside parties? Usually, in order to have a basis, it would have to have been acquired from outside parties, because if it was transferred to the partnership as a contribution by one of the incoming partners it would have to take as its basis the basis in the hands of the contributing partner. He would have no basis for it unless he had acquired it from an outsider for a valuable consideration. Or, he may have acquired it by inheritance, having taken over his father's business. Since his basis for the entire business, including good will, would be its value at the date of death of his father, here he could invoke A. R. M. 34 or one of the other formulae and create a basis for it. If it had been separately valued for estate or inheritance tax purposes, that value would apply, unless it could be proved to be wrong. 

Example (1): Upon the death of his father, Jones inherited a hardware store which his father had operated as sole owner. For inheritance tax purposes, the business was valued at $75,000, which included $25,000 for good will. Jones formed a partnership with another individual immediately after the father's death, and turned the hardware business over to the partnership. Jones' interest in the partnership capital and profits was two-thirds, the other's one-third. The partnership agreement stated that the entire $25,000 value of good will on the partnership books was distributable to Jones' estate in case of his death. Six years later, when the basis of Jones' partnership interest was $80,000, he died and the business was sold to outside interests for $150,000, of which the estate's share was $100,000, which it received in cash upon dissolution of the partnership. 

Of the sale price, $45,000 was allocated to good will, that having been the amount agreed upon by all parties to the transaction. The $105,000 remainder of the sale price represented the book value of the tangible assets. (There were no liabilities.) Of the $100,000 payment to the estate, $70,000 (% of $105,000) 33

Death of Partnership Member 

39 represented payment for its interest in tangible assets of the partnership, and $30,000 represented payment for its interest in the good will. However, based on the partnership's basis and also on the partnership agreement, only $25,000 of this is regarded as payment for partnership property. Therefore, of the estate's $20,000 gain on sale of its interest (the difference between the $100,000 received and the $80,000 basis of its interest), $5,000 represents ordinary income and $15,000 represents capital gain. 

Example (2): Assume the same facts as above, except that the partnership agreement provided that a reasonable value be placed by the surviving partner and estate or estates upon the value of good will in case of death of either or both of the partners, and that of such value the entire $25,000 representing the partnership's basis of the good will, and 2/3 of any excess value over the $25,000 as determined by the parties, should be allocated to Jones or Jones' estate. Since the reasonable value was determined to be $45,000, of which the share for Jones' estate was $25,000 plus 3 of $20,000, or a total of $38,333.33, that amount may be regarded as received by the estate for its share of partnership property. Assuming that the estate received also $70,000 as its share of the tangible assets value, or a total payment of $108,333.33, it had a gain of $28,333.33, all of which represents capital gain. [33.01] Formulae for valuing good will.-There are at least three types of acceptable formulae that can be used in determining the value of good will. One, which may be termed a straight capitalization method, is to determine the average net profits over a period of at least five years and capitalize them at an agreed definite rate, as for example, 10% or 15%. 

Example (3): A partnership has had average net profits of $12,000 for the past five years, after making provision for reasonable salaries to the partners for their services (regardless of whether or not they actually received such compensation). If a capitalization rate of 10% is agreed upon, the value of the good will is $120,000 ($12,000 ÷ 10%). The reasonableness of the 10% rate might be questioned.

Example (4): Assume the same facts as above, except that a capitalization rate of 15% was agreed upon. The value of the good will is $80,000 ($12,000 ÷ 15%).

A second method, which may be termed the three or five years' purchase method, is to allow a return of 6% on the tangible assets, deduct this from the average profits for the past five years, and capitalize the remaining profits on the basis of a three years' purchase, or five years' purchase, as agreed upon. 

Example (5): A partnership has had average annual net profits of $12,000 for the past five years. It is agreed that three years' purchase price of excess earnings is a reasonable price for good will. A return of 6% on the tangible assets of $150,000 ($9,000) leaves $3,000 of the average annual profits attributable to the good will, which, on the basis of three years' purchase, amounts to $9,000. 

Example (6): Assume the same facts as in Example (5) except that a five years' purchase was agreed upon. The good will amounts to 5 times $3,000, or $15,000.

A third method is the best known of all three and may be called the income tax method, since it has been applied in countless income tax cases. 

It was sanctioned in the early days of the income tax by the Internal Revenue Service, then the Bureau of Internal Revenue, in a ruling called A. R. M. 34.15 

Under this method, as in the others, the average annual earnings of a representative period, preferably not less than five years, are determined, and 15 2 CB 31. ¶ 33.01

there is assigned to the tangible assets a return of 10%, the remainder of the average annual earnings being attributable to the good will, which can be capitalized at a return of 20% (which is the equivalent of a five years' purchase). 

These are the rates which should be applied to businesses of a more or less hazardous nature, such as distillers and wholesale liquor dealers. In other decided income tax cases, returns on tangible assets ranging from 5% to 9% have been applied, the remaining annual profits being capitalized at from 10% to 33% (the latter percentage being the equivalent of a three years' purchase). A. R. M. 34 recommended, for businesses of a stable nature consisting of manufacture or sale of standard articles of every-day necessity not subject to violent fluctuations, a return of 8% or 9% on tangibles, the remaining profits being capitalized at 15%.

Example (7): An old-established feed and grain business had average annual earnings over a five-year period of $12,500 after provision for reasonable constructive salaries of partners for their services. It had net tangible assets of $100,000. Applying an 8% return on the tangible assets, $8,000 of its average annual earnings was attributable to the tangibles, and $4,500 to good will. 

Capitalizing this at a 15% rate results in a $30,000 value for good will. 

The cases applying a rate of less than 8% to the tangibles, and capitalizing the excess earnings at a rate of less than 15%, were Tax Court cases or cases decided by other courts. The Commissioner might not permit a deviation from the rates set forth in A. R. M. 34 if the formula described therein is used. On the other hand, he has promised in his Regulations 16 "generally" to accept as correct any valuation placed upon good will by an arm's length agreement of the partners, but it must also meet the test of reasonableness. 16 Reg. 1.736-1(b)(3). ¶ 33.01

PART III-THE RETURN OF AN ESTATE OR TRUST The Characteristics of an Estate or Trust 

[¶ 34] Estates and trusts are separate taxable entities.-Estates of deceased persons and trusts are regarded for Federal income tax purposes as separate and distinct entities. They are subject to the same income taxes (but not the self-employment tax) as individuals, with certain differences in the computation of credits and deductions. 

The gross income of an estate or trust is determined in the same manner as that of an individual. The principle for taxing estates and trusts is that all income of whatever kind and from whatever source derived (other than exempt income, such as tax-free interest on municipal obligations, etc.) is taxable to someone-either to the fiduciary or to the beneficiary. Regardless of who is taxed, the income retains its same character and the taxpayer is allowed the credits, exclusions, capital gain benefits, or other privileges attaching to the income on which the tax is imposed. This is called the "conduit" rule for taxing estates and trusts. 

On page 41, there appears a simplified chart showing how the items of income and deduction are entered on Form 1041, the return to be filed by an estate or trust.

Trusts which are taxable as individuals are the kind created by will or a declaration of trust, where the trustees are primarily engaged in conserving or protecting the property.

Trusts or other arrangements for the welfare and benefit of the grantor's wife and children are taxable as individuals. Even where the trust is created for the purpose of effecting a division of the grantor's property among his children, it is taxable as an individual.

In certain types of inter vivos trusts, that is, those established to take effect during the lifetime of the grantor, the provisions of the trust instrument may make the trust nonrecognizable as a separate entity from the grantor and therefore make the income of the trust taxable to him. See further ¶ 89-93. 

Charitable, religious, educational, etc., trusts are exempt, that is, they are free from the income tax in the same manner as some corporations organized and operated for like purposes. The business, or so-called "Massachusetts,' trusts, are taxable as corporations. Common trust funds are taxed in somewhat the same manner as a partnership. Investment trusts and employee trusts are given separate treatment in the Code. [135] How long does the estate or trust exist?—The income of an estate of a deceased person is that which is received during the period of administration or settlement. The period of administration or settlement is that period actually required by the administrator or executor to perform the ordinary duties of administration, such as the collection of assets and the payment of debts, taxes, legacies and bequests, whether the period required is longer or shorter than the period specified under the applicable local law for the settlement of estates.17

Example (1): An executor, who is also named as trustee under a will, fails to obtain his discharge as executor. The period of estate administration continues only until his duties of administering the estate as executor are complete and he actually assumes his duties as trustee, whether or not pursuant to a court order. 

17 Reg. § 1.641(b)-3(a). 

¶ 34

The Characteristics of an Estate or Trust 

43 2 The period of administration of an estate, however, cannot be unduly prolonged. If the administration of an estate is unreasonably prolonged, the estate is considered terminated for Federal income tax purposes after the expiration of a reasonable period for the performance by the executor of all 0 the duties of administration. Further, an estate will be considered as terminated when all the assets have been distributed except for a reasonable amount which is set aside in good faith for the payment of unascertained or contingent liabilities and expenses (not including a claim by a beneficiary in the capacity of beneficiary).

Whether there is undue delay in winding up an estate is a question of fact in every case. Building up a credit rating for the going business of an estate is in no sense a duty or an ordinary function of an executor. It has been held that things alleged to be required to be done, which necessitated a continuance of the administration of an estate, might just as well have been done by the distributee with the advice and assistance of the executor, in accordance with the expressed wishes of the decedent. Where the administration of an estate was not subject to the jurisdiction of a state court and no approval was obtained to extend the administration, the executor was accountable to the Probate Court only for probating the will, filing an inventory and listing the claims of the estate. It was indicated that a different result might have followed as a consequence of a court order.18 A similar result followed in a case where taxpayer was both independent executrix and sole beneficiary.19 

In some instances the question of when an estate ceases for tax purposes is resolved under local law. Thus, a statute permitted a residuary legatee to wind up an estate without waiting for the expiration of the period of probate, by the giving of a bond conditioned upon his paying all debts, claims and legacies. It was held that, where the sole beneficiary of the estate was the executor, the date of approval of such a bond marked the close of the period of administration, and thereafter the income from the estate was taxable to the beneficiary.20 Otherwise, where the fiduciary is not the sole beneficiary, final distribution to the beneficiaries usually determines the closing of administration of the estate.¹

Generally, the trust entity will begin where the estate leaves off. A question may arise, however, as to whether a testamentary trust may begin to function as a separate and independent tax entity, without regard to a prior settlement of the estate, thus enabling them to operate side by side to the tax advantage of both. This appears not to be possible, according to a decision of the Third Circuit in U. S. v. Britten. Until distribution is properly made, a decedent's property is presumably part of his general estate. 

The mere passing of title to an executor or administrator on the death of the decedent is not a realization of income by the estate even though the property may have appreciated in value since the decedent acquired it.³ 

It is recognized that, upon acceptance of the appointment, there vests in the administrator or executor, as of the date of the decedent's death, title to all personal property belonging to the estate. Such vesting of title is deemed necessary for the proper administration of the estate and its distribution. 

Upon distribution the title to personal property is regarded as reverting to the legatees as of the date of decedent's death. The interim title vests in such entity as a matter of convenience and necessity only. 1 I. T. 3556, 1942-1 CB 130. 2 47-1 USTC 1 9273, 161 Fed. (2d) 921. *O. D. 731, 3 CB 210; A. R. R. 249, 3 CB 145. 

Brewster v. Gage, (S. Ct.) 280 U. S. 327, 2 USTC ¶ 451. 

18 W. G. Farrier Est., 15 TC 277, Dec. 17,846 (Acq.). 

19 J. Stewart, (CA-5) 52-1 usTc ¶ 9315, 196 Fed. (2d) 397. 

20 I. T. 2925, XIV-2 CB 148. ¶ 35

[35.01] Trust termination.-Generally, the termination of a trust depends upon whether the property held in trust has been distributed to the persons entitled to succeed to the property upon termination of the trust rather than upon the technicality of whether or not the trustee has rendered his final accounting. A trust does not automatically terminate upon the happening of the event by which the duration of the trust is measured. A reasonable time is permitted after such event for the trustee to perform the duties necessary to complete the administration of the trust. 

Example (2): Under the terms of the governing instrument, the trust is to terminate upon the death of the life beneficiary and the corpus is to be distributed to the remainderman. The trust continues after the death of the life beneficiary for a period reasonably necessary for a proper winding up of the affairs of the trust.

The winding up of a trust, however, cannot be unduly postponed and if the distribution of the trust corpus is unreasonably delayed, the trust is considered terminated for Federal income tax purposes after the expiration of a reasonable period for the trustee to complete the administration of the trust. 

Further, a trust will be considered as terminated when all the assets have been distributed except for a reasonable amount which is set aside in good faith for the payment of unascertained or contingent liabilities and expenses (not including a claim by a beneficiary in the capacity of beneficiary).5 

If a trust or the administration or settlement of an estate is considered terminated for Federal income tax purposes, the gross income, deductions, and credits of the estate or trust are, subsequent to the termination, considered the gross income, deductions, and credits of the person or persons succeeding to the property of the estate or trust. [36] Can a trust be a partnership member?-Regardless of state law, trusts can be recognized as general or limited partners, for income tax purposes. Finality of the gift to the trust is usually determinative. Thus, the appointment of independent trustees free to withdraw from the partnership,' or of a corporate trustee, or authorizing the beneficiaries to terminate the trust, is likely to result in a valid partnership even though the grantor or a member of his family is a co-trustee. And, conversely, any arrangement that leaves the grantor taxable on income, under Code Secs. 671-678 (see [89-93), would be likely to make the trust ineffective as a partner. 

What Are the Return and Tax Payment Requirements for Estates and Trusts? [37] When must a return be filed, who does the filing, and what form is used? A return must be filed for an estate (1) when the gross income of the estate for the taxable year is $600 or over; or (2) when any beneficiary of the estate is a nonresident alien. 

A return must be filed for a trust (1) when the trust has any taxable income, or gross income of $600 or more, regardless of the amount of taxable income; or (2) when any beneficiary of the trust is a nonresident alien.10

What Are the Return and Tax Payment Requirements for Estates and Trusts 

45 དྲ The return is to be filed by the fiduciary, that is, the executor, administrator, trustee, etc., of the estate or trust for which he acts.11 

Form 1041 is the income tax form required to be filed by estates and trusts. 

On page 41, there appears a simplified chart showing how the items of income and deduction are entered on Form 1041. Except where all the trust income of a taxable year must be distributed in that year, trusts claiming charitable, etc. deductions under Code Sec. 642(c) (see [70) must, in addition, file Form 1041-A. 

In the case of joint fiduciaries, the return must be executed by one of them. The fiduciary so executing in any case must declare (1) that he has sufficient knowledge of the affairs of the estate or trust to enable him to make the return, and (2) that the return is to the best of his knowledge and belief true and correct.12 [37.01] Fiduciary capacity of guardian.-A fiduciary acting as guardian for a minor, or as the guardian or committee of an insane person, is required to file an individual return (Form 1040 or 1040A) for such person in any case where such individual is subject to the requirement for filing a return unless, in the case of a minor, the minor himself makes a return or causes it to be made.13 For the taxable year during which an incompetent is declared competent and the fiduciary is discharged, the former incompetent will file his own return. The estate of an incompetent is not a separate tax entity distinct from the incompetent and, therefore, a fiduciary return on Form 1041 is not required.¹4 [37.02] Receivers and special trustees. When a trustee in bankruptcy operates the estate of a bankrupt individual or partnership, income of the estate is reported on the fiduciary return Form 1041. But a trustee for a corporation in bankruptcy makes out a corporate return on Form 1120. A bankrupt individual must file his own income tax return for a full taxable year, notwithstanding intervention of a bankruptcy proceeding. The right of a bankrupt individual to a refund of income tax erroneously paid for a period prior to bankruptcy passes to the trustee, but payment of such refund will be made to the trustee only if he files proper claim for refund. 

Receivers, which term does not include trustees in bankruptcy, are not required to file fiduciary returns. Instead, they should file returns as agents, reporting the income as if made by the taxpayers for whom they act. The same rule applies to trustees in dissolution or in bankruptcy, and assignees operating the property or business of corporations.15 

A receiver in charge of only a part of the property of a corporation, as in the case of a mortgaged parcel of property, or in partition proceedings, is not required to make any return; the real, beneficial owner being the one required to account for the net income therefrom in its return during the period of the receivership.

Accordingly, corporate returns of income (Form 1120) are required of receivers, trustees in dissolution, trustees in bankruptcy, and assignees who are in full custody and control of or otherwise operating the corporate business or property. A return on Form 1065 is required of a receiver in charge of a partnership business. A receiver of all of the property of an individual must make return on Form 1040 on behalf of such individual. A receiver in charge of only part of the property of an individual or a corporation need not make a return of income.

What are the due dates for the return and where is it filed?—

Returns by a fiduciary on behalf of the estate or trust for which he acts are due on or before the 15th day of the fourth month following the close of the taxable year of the estate or trust. Nontaxable returns must also be filed on or before the 15th day of the fourth month following the close of the taxable year.16

The estate or trust return is to be filed with the District Director of the district where the fiduciary resides or has his principal place of business.¹7 

A fiduciary, like any other taxpayer, must elect in its first return whether it will account for the estate or trust income on a calendar or a fiscal year, and whether on a cash or accrual basis. It is not held to any accounting period or method used by the decedent, but having made its election in the first return, it must continue to account on the same basis unless permission is granted by the Commissioner for making a change. If the fiduciary for the estate adopts the same accounting period (calendar or fiscal year) as the decedent used, its first return will cover the unexpired term of his regular taxable year. 

See also in this connection, [[ 2 above. The short taxable year is also applicable to an estate's or trust's first return where the fiduciary elects a taxable period which begins less than 12 months from the date of the decedent's death or the inception of the trust.

Example (1): D, who filed his returns on the basis of the calendar year, died March 20, 1957, leaving a substantial estate. On April 8, 1957, the K Bank was appointed executor. In due course it files a final return for the decedent. 

Later it also files a first return for the estate, electing to use a fiscal year ending January 31. The final return for the decedent will be on Form 1040 and will cover the period beginning January 1, 1957, and ending March 20, 1957. The first return for the estate will be on Form 1041, the fiduciary form, and will cover the period beginning March 21, 1957, and ending January 31, 1958. 

Example (2): Continuing from Example (1), assume that on August 3, 

1958, pursuant to order of the court, all of the assets of the estate are transferred to a trust provided for under D's will, and the K Bank, pursuant to the will, is appointed trustee of the trust. The bank decides to use for the trust a fiscal year ending March 31. The estate's final return, on Form 1041, will cover the period beginning February 1, 1958 and ending August 3, 1958, and the first return for the trust, also on Form 1041, will cover the income for the period beginning August 4, 1958 and ending March 31, 1959. The trust is a separate taxable entity, although the same bank is fiduciary. 

The return by an estate or trust which was not in existence throughout a taxable period of 12 months is the return for the fractional part of the year during which it was in existence.18 The filing date is computed from "the close of the taxable year.' The actual "close" of such a fractional part of a year would be the date the estate or trust passed out of existence. Accordingly, in Example (2) above, the estate's final return would be due on or before the 15th day of the fourth full month following the date the estate was closed. (August 3, 1958); that is, on or before December 15, 1958. [39] When is the tax to be paid?—The fiduciary of an estate may elect to pay the tax due in four equal installments. The installment due dates on the calendar-year basis are April 15, July 15, October 15 and January 15. On the fiscal-year basis, the installments are due on or before the 15th day of the 

4th, 7th, 10th and 13th months following the close of the fiscal year."

Director. 

The fiduciary of a trust cannot pay the income tax due in installments. 

Therefore, the tax must be paid in full when the return is filed, the filing due date for which is the 15th day of the fourth month following the close of the taxable year. [40] May extensions of time be granted?-Extensions of time for filing estate or trust returns on Form 1041 are granted in somewhat the same manner as requests by corporations, and are conditioned upon the filing of a tentative return, and payment of the tax shown thereon to be due (one-fourth of the tax in the case of an estate electing the installment procedure) on or before the statutory date specified for filing. The granting of an extension of time for filing an estate or trust return does not carry with it the right to postpone payment of the tax. But, if it is shown to the satisfaction of the Commissioner that the payment of the tax or any part or installment thereof upon the date or dates prescribed for payment will result in undue hardship to the estate or trust, the District Director may grant an extension of time for payment. [41] What type of return is required of administrators in other states? -If a decedent leaves property in a state other than the state of his domicile (usually where he lives), administration may be granted in such foreign state to collect the assets and pay the debts there, bringing what remains into the general administration of the estate. This is referred to as "ancillary" administration.

An ancillary representative who collects income of a decedent from property located outside the state of the domicile of the decedent is required to file an information return on Form 1041, showing the gross income received by. him and the deductions to be claimed against that income, including any amount of income properly paid or credited by the ancillary representative to a legatee, heir, or beneficiary. But the domiciliary representative files a return showing all the income and deductions of the estate, including that reported by the ancillary representative. Therefore, any tax to be paid would be reported on the return of the domiciliary representative, and, although there are no cases or rulings on the point, it would appear that only the domiciliary representative would get the $600 exemption. There is a probable exception to this rule, however, where the domiciliary representative is a nonresident alien and the ancillary representative is a resident or citizen. In such a case, the ancillary representative must file the return otherwise required of the domiciliary representative and therefore would probably be allowed the estate's $600 exemption.20

The information return of the ancillary representative of the estate is required to be filed with the District Director for the district in which the ancillary representative is located. [42] What instruments are to be filed with the return?-If the gross income of the estate or trust is $5,000 or over, a copy of the will or trust instrument, sworn to by the fiduciary as a true and complete copy, must be filed with the return. The fiduciary is also to attach to the return a statement expressing his opinion (indicating the relevant provisions of the governing instrument upon which he relies) as to the extent to which the income of the 

20 1939 Code Reg. § 39.142-6(b). 

42

estate or trust is taxable to the estate or trust, to the beneficiaries, or to the grantor or a person other than the grantor.

If a copy of the governing instrument and a statement of the fiduciary have once been filed with a return, they need not again be filed, provided subsequent returns contain a statement showing when and where such documents were filed. However, if a trust instrument is amended after a copy has been filed, a copy of the amendment must be filed with the return for the taxable year in which the amendment was made. A statement must also be filed by the fiduciary giving his opinion as to the effect of such amendment on the extent to which the income of the trust is taxable to the trust, the beneficiaries, or to the grantor or a person other than the grantor. [143] How many returns to be filed in case of two or more trusts?—A trustee must make a single return on Form 1041 for two or more trusts, the income of which is taxable to the beneficiaries, which were created by the same person and for whom the trustee acts. This holds true even though the trusts may arise under separate instruments. Presumably, it will apply where different beneficiaries' shares are treated as separate trusts under Code Sec. 

663 (c). However, if one person acts as trustee for trusts created by different persons for the benefit of the same beneficiary, the trustee must make a return on Form 1041 for each trust separately. 

A grantor or testator may create several trusts for different beneficiaries by one instrument. In such case the income is taxed separately as to each trust. Each receives the $100 or $300 personal exemption (56) and is treated as a separate entity for all tax purposes. The Supreme Court has held that it is not necessary to have a physical division of the corpus of each trust if the intent to create more than one trust is apparent.¹ 2 The fact that the trusts are carved out of one fund does not necessarily defeat an otherwise clear intention to create separate trusts, since an undivided interest in property may constitute the corpus of each of several trusts. If there are separate trusts, the beneficiary may not offset a net loss of one against the distributable income of the other, even though there was one grantor, one trustee, and one beneficiary for all the trusts."

The Eighth Circuit affirming the Tax Court holds that a retroactive reformation of a trust instrument by decree of a local court to create several trusts instead of one is not retroactive for Federal tax purposes. This is true even though the reformation decree may reflect the original intent of the grantor and even though the decree results from an adversary proceeding. 

Tax savings possible.-Multiple trusts can be used to spread income over lower surtax brackets, thereby increasing the income actually available for distribution to beneficiaries. If there is a single corpus, or if an existing trust is broken down into multiple trusts, it should be kept in mind that separate accounts and records should be maintained for each trust. It is not essential to have a physical division of the corpus, if that is impracticable. [44] How about returns for nonresident alien beneficiaries?—A resident fiduciary for an estate or trust, any beneficiary of which is a nonresident alien, is required to file Form 1041, and also a return for the beneficiary (Form 1040B, 1040NB, or 1040NB-a), in all cases in which such a return is required
