He is relieved of the duty of filing Form 1040B, 1040NB, or 1040NB-a for the nonresident alien beneficiary if the latter has appointed a person in the United States to act as his agent for the purpose of filing income tax returns. In such a case, the fiduciary has to make a return on Form 1041 and attach thereto a copy of the notice of appointment of such agent. He must also make an annual return on Form 1042 in connection with income tax to be paid at source on certain payments to nonresident aliens, foreign partnerships, and foreign corporations not engaged in trade or business within the United States. 

Income Which Is Taxable to the Estate or Trust [145] General types of income to be reported. The gross income of an estate or trust is determined in the same manner as that of an individual." 

For instance, interest and rents earned and accrued after a decedent's death are includible in the gross income of an estate or trust to the same extent that they would be if received by an individual. 

The law specifically provides that gross income of an estate or trust includes: (1) Income accumulated in trust for the benefit of unborn or unascertained persons or persons with contingent interests; (2) Income accumulated or held for future distribution under the terms of the will or trust; (3) Income which is to be distributed currently by the fiduciary to the beneficiaries, and income collected by a guardian of an infant which is to be held or distributed as the court may direct; (4) Income received by estates of deceased persons during the period of administration or settlement of the estate; and (5) Income which, in the discretion of the fiduciary, may be either distributed to the beneficiaries or accumulated."

Some of the items listed above, however, may be taxed to the beneficiary in whole or in part instead of to the estate or trust. All of the income specified in (1) and (2) is taxed to the estate or trust; all of the income specified in (3) is deductible by the fiduciary and is taxed to the beneficiary, whether distributed or not, so that none of the income in (3) is eventually taxed to the fiduciary; the income specified in (4) and (5) may be eventually taxed to the fiduciary or to the beneficiary, depending upon the amounts which are properly paid or credited to the beneficiary. See ¶71 et seq.

Broadly speaking, a decedent's earnings which are not income taxable in his last return are income taxable to the estate or others when actually received. There are exceptions, however, which are discussed in connection with the topics which follow.

The following items have been held to result in realization of income by the estate which received them: (1) Back alimony." (2) Amounts received on notes which had a zero value on the date of death.8

Reg. § 1.641(a)-2. 

• Code Sec. 641(a). 'Narischkine Est., (CA-2) 51-1 ustc ¶ 9313, 189 

Fed. (2d) 257. 8 Helvering v. J. Roth, (CA-2) 40-2 ustc || 9738, 115 Fed. (2d) 239. ¶ 45

(3) Profits from a sale of pledged and unpledged securities, to satisfy a debt of the decedent." (4) Transfer of securities in discharge of an obligation.10 (5) Amounts realized from a claim for patent infringement damages which was in process of litigation at the date of death.¹¹ 

In items (1) and (2), ordinary income was realized for the full amount; items (3) and (4) were the equivalent of sales or exchanges resulting in capital gain or loss. Item (5) was ordinary income, compensation for lost income. 

What has been said above in relation to income taxable to a decedent's estate is also true as to income of a trust. For example, assume at the date of a decedent's death, he held a claim to $50,000. If the "right" to the claim, assuming it constitutes "income in respect of a decedent", passed to a trust as legatee or beneficiary, the trust would be taxable on the claim if it subsequently received the proceeds of collection. 

It should be remembered that there is no gain or loss from the passage of property to the executor or administrator from the decedent. This is so because the basis of property acquired from a decedent by will or under the law governing the descent and distribution of the property of decedents is the fair market value at the time of death (see ¶53). It is the subsequent increase or decrease in the value of the property reflected in a sale or other disposition which is recognized as the measure of gain or loss. The unrealized appreciation to date of death is freed from income tax. 

The same is true as to property passing under an instrument which is treated as though it were a will. An example of this is where an inter vivos trust makes disposition of property upon death of the grantor. This would be true even though the grantor in his lifetime had the power to revoke the trust. 

This nonrecognition of gain from disposition of property follows through to a distribution of trust property to the beneficiaries. [146] How are dividends treated by an estate or trust?-Dividends constitute income to an estate or trust the same as they do to an individual. 

An estate or trust is also entitled to the dividend exclusion provided in Code 

Sec. 116 and the dividend received credit in Code Sec. 34.

Dividends are taxable to the person who "owns" them. Ordinarily, this is the taxpayer who owns the stock on the date of declaration, assuming they are not payable as of any other date. In the latter case, they would ordinarily be taxable to the owner of the stock on the record or payment date, whichever governs.

The dividend exclusion.-In determining the amount of the estate's or trust's dividend exclusion, the Internal Revenue Service in its instructions for Form 1041 requires the fiduciary to prorate the dividend exclusion where dividends are distributed to the beneficiary. Although the law actually allows an estate or trust a maximum dividend exclusion of $50, the law also provides that the distribution deduction of the estate or trust is to be reduced by items of income not included in the gross income of the estate or trust.12 Accordingly, in the return, instead of adjusting the estate's or trust's distribution 

• First Trust & Deposit Co., (DC) 44-2 USTC 

I 9478, 58 Fed. Supp. 162. 

10 I. T. 1562, II-1 CB 33. 

11 Rev. Rul. 55-463, 1955-2 CB 277. 

12 Code Secs. 651 and 661. 

¶ 46

deduction for dividend income excluded from gross income, a “short-cut” adjustment is made to the dividend exclusion.

Example: A trust receives $500 in dividends, of which $300 is allocated to the beneficiary. Since only 40% of the dividends is allocated to the trust, only 40%, or $20, of the $50 exclusion is claimed by the trustee on page 1 of Form 1041. This accomplishes the same net tax result as would be obtained from adjusting the distributions deduction. 

It should be noted that under the circumstances of the above example, the beneficiary would be entitled to a full $50 exclusion on his individual return for the $300 in dividends received from the trust. 

The dividend received credit. The dividends received credit of an estate or trust is to be based only on dividends which are not properly allocable to beneficiaries. To the extent that dividends are properly allocable to beneficiaries, the beneficiaries, and not the estate or trust, are entitled to the credit for dividends received.13

A beneficiary receiving dividends from stock owned individually by him and from stock held by an estate is limited to a maximum dividend exclusion of $50. But the dividend income received from both sources may be included in computing the beneficiary's dividends received credit.¹4 [47] Interest payments are taxable.—All interest received by an estate or trust is taxable income, except for interest shown to be exempt. Exempt interest is limited, as in the case of any other person, to that derived from state and local obligations and from some Federal obligations. 

As to when and how interest on U. S. savings bonds is to be reflected in income, see the discussion at ¶ 19 above. [48] Who is taxed on income from real property?—In most jurisdictions title to real estate vests immediately in the heirs or devisees from the date of death. However, real estate may always be sold during probate administration to pay debts if the personal property is not sufficient, or if the will directs the sale for some purpose, or to pay cash legacies. 

With the above principles in mind, it may be stated as a general rule that the estate is not taxable on income from real estate (such as rents) where the decedent did not direct the sale of the real estate, and his personal property is sufficient to pay debts. This is the usual rule in states such as New York and Pennsylvania.15 In some states (as in California), although title to real estate vests immediately in the heirs or devisees, the law gives right of possession and control to the administrator or executor for the period of administration. In such case the income belongs to the estate and is taxable to it alone.16 

The decedent's will may authorize the sale of real property, not merely for payment of debts or legacies, but because it is the express intention of the testator that the executor should conduct such a sale for the purpose of forestalling vexatious and expensive partition proceedings that could ensue in respect to an undivided ownership otherwise vesting in the devisee upon death. The sale for payment of debts and legacies only would be clearly incidental in the ordinary administration of an estate. It might be thought that a distribution by an executor of real property to devisees by means of a sale (if 

18 Reg. 1.642(a) (3)-1. 

14 Rev. Rul. 55-728, 1955-2 CB 36. 

15 Craig, 7 BTA 504, Dec. 2580; Guaranty Trust 

Co. of N. Y., 30 BTA 314, Dec. 8499; I. T. 1596,

II-1 CB 130. 

16 Cohen Est., 8 TC 784, Dec. 15,709. 148

it is determined the property is not susceptible of voluntary division by metes and bounds) is no different in principle from that of a sale for payment of general legacies. However, the extent of the power of an executor to carry out a division and distribution of real property in this manner, and the ensuing question of whether the direction in the will itself effected a conversion of the realty into personalty, depends wholly upon the express provisions of the will and the local law.

It may develop, for instance, that the executor in such a case has in the beginning no title to the property, but only a power in trust to sell if the devisees cannot agree to a voluntary division of the real estate. As stated in one case, a "mere donee of a power is not the owner of an estate, nor to be classed as a juristic entity to which a loss can be attributed." 17 If that were so, gain or loss on a sale would be attributable to the devisees and not to the estate. On the other hand, a view might be taken under local law that, in directing a partition of the property, the executors were charged with active duties not susceptible of fulfillment without possession and dominion of title from the inception of the estate. Inquiry may also be directed to whether such a provision in the will created a discretionary or a mandatory power in the executor.

Assuming a conversion takes place of the realty into personalty by the terms of the will or the local law, a sale of the real estate by the executor or administrator for any purpose changes the character thereof to the extent of making gain or loss attributable to the estate and absolves from tax liability the heir or devisee who would otherwise be entitled to the property. 

Real estate in the hands of either the estate or beneficiary, as the case may be, takes a new basis from that which the decedent had for it. That basis is the value on the date of death or later optional valuation date.18 Whether gain or loss from its sale is ordinary income or loss or capital gain or loss depends on the character of the real estate in the hands of the estate or beneficiary-that is, whether held for personal residence, for investment purposes, for sale to customers, or for use in a trade or business. For capital gain and loss purposes, the holding period of real estate in the hands of the estate or beneficiary begins on the date of death of the decedent. It does not include the decedent's holding period. Also, it does not begin on the later optional valuation date, even though the value on that date may have been used for estate tax purposes and for basis purposes.18ª 

Trust corpus.-No problems ordinarily arise where the real estate is part of the corpus of a trust. The trustee takes full legal title to real estate for tax purposes as if it were received and held by any other tax entity. Sales by trustees are usually made pursuant to powers granted in the trust instrument for investment and reinvestment of the trust corpus, or for making an equitable distribution of the trust property upon termination. Upon any such sale, gain or loss is attributable to the trust. [49] Income from personal property is taxable to the estate.-Income of all kinds from personal property, including gain from sale or exchange of such property, is taxable to the estate. This is so because title to personal property vests in the representative immediately upon his appointment. Title to personal property does not pass to the legatees until the estate is fully 

17 Anderson v. Wilson, 289 U. S. 20, 3 USTC ¶ 1066. 

18 Code Sec. 1014(b). 

18a Helvering v. R. V. N. Gambrill (S. Ct.), 41-1 USTC 9360, 313 U. S. 11; Special Ruling, 9-24-46. ¶ 49

גב administered and distribution is ordered or approved by the court, notwithstanding that the basis of the property distributed relates back to the date of = the decedent's death.

Where a decedent's will calls for the transfer of personal property to a trust, the income from the personal property prior to the transfer is taxable to the estate because the trust cannot come into existence as to specific property until after probate administration. Thereafter the trust will have to report the income. Income from personal property in the hands of an inter vivos trust is taxable to the trust except where it is considered to be that of the grantor under the special provisions discussed at ¶ 89-93. 

In the absence of specific testamentary or judicial directions to carry on a decedent's business, it would seem that an estate does not hold assets for use in a "business" or for sale in the course of trade, so sales would ordinarily result in capital gains or losses.

An executor's liquidation of a decedent's inventory of goods that had been held by the decedent for sale to customers in the ordinary course of his business can result in capital gain (or loss) from sale of a capital asset if the estate (through the executor) did not actively engage in the decedent's trade or business, but sold the inventory on hand as expeditiously as possible in order to close the estate. Thus, the Tax Court (the Commissioner acquiescing) has held that an executor realized capital gain from the sale of a large quantity of skins and some fur garments, both in bulk and at auction sales, after it had sold a deceased furrier's retail fur business, and the skins and garments represented articles which the purchaser of the business did not I want.19 If the executor had purchased more skins or garments, in addition to what was on hand at the time of the decedent's death, the estate probably i would have been regarded as having been engaged in a trade or business. 

The above principles could also be applied to an executor's sale of livestock upon death of a farmer. Whether the sale proceeds represented capital gain or ordinary income would depend upon whether the executor was continuing the farmer's business of farming, or was merely liquidating the farm -11 na it assets. [49.01] Sale of growing crops.-Where crops are sold after the death of a farmer, the gain on the sale is income to the estate or to the heirs. If the farmer had contracted for the sale of the crops before his death, the sale proceeds would be "income in respect of a decedent" under Code Sec. 691 (see 11), which would have the same character as they would have had in the hands of the decedent, that is, ordinary income. If the crops are sold in liquidation of the estate, they would be capital assets in the hands of the estate. But if the executor continues to operate the farm, as a custodian for the ultimate heir or legatee, sales undoubtedly would be considered to have been made in the ordinary course of business, so as to result in ordinary income. On the other hand, a sale of crops pending sale of the farm would seem to be in liquidation of the assets of the estate. If the crops are sold by the executor along with the land, they would be assets subject to capital gain treatment under Code Sec. 1231. 

Where there is a specific devise of real estate or of a "farm," growing crops ordinarily would be included, since they are considered a part of the realty. On a sale of the crops by an heir or devisee, it would apparently be 

19 J. Ferber Est., 22 TC 261, Dec. 20,321 (Acq.). ¶ 49.01

more difficult for him than for an executor to prove that he was not in the business of farming. Of course, if he sold the growing crops together with the land he could have a capital gain under Code Sec. 1231. But if he sold the crops separately, in all likelihood his gain would be ordinary income. A sale of crops, either harvested or unharvested, does not seem comparable to a liquidation of a real estate inheritance. The heir or devisee would realize ordinary income to the extent that the sale proceeds exceed the value assigned to the growing crops for estate tax purposes. 

Just what the tax consequences are when a farmer has delivered crops to a cooperative for sale, but dies before the sale is effected or the proceeds are distributed, is not clear. In one case,20 the Ninth Circuit held that: (1) The net proceeds of sales made by the cooperative before the farmer's death were "income in respect of a decedent", taxable as such when received by the estate and distributed to the beneficiary (the Tax Court had held that this involved a nontaxable collection of a claim, which became and was distributed as corpus of the estate); and (2) The net proceeds of sales made after the farmer's death, and after the estate was closed, were taxable on their receipt by the beneficiary as "income in respect of a decedent" (the Tax Court had held that these would not be "income in respect of a decedent," because the sales were not made until after the farmer's death). 

At the same time, the court affirmed a holding by the Tax Court that the estate tax valuation of the beneficiary's interest in crops not sold by the cooperative before the farmer's death became their basis to the beneficiary, and that the difference between this basis and the amount realized on their sale by the cooperative was capital gain or loss. No attempt was made by the court to fit this holding to the above holdings that the proceeds were "income in respect of a decedent", or to the fact that the proceeds would have been ordinary income to the decedent. 

If an amount is taxable as income in respect of a decedent, it is taxable in full, but the tax is offset to some extent by a deduction allowed for the estate tax paid on the right to receive the income. See ¶ 28. If it is capital gain from sale of a capital asset, the amount received is reduced by the "basis" of the asset, the basis being the estate tax value. [150] Is gain or loss realized when a legacy is satisfied in property?-- 

Generally, no taxable gain or loss results from a transfer of property to the beneficiary under a specific bequest, whether its value at the time of transfer be greater or less than at the date of acquisition by the estate or trust.¹ But gain or loss is realized when a cash bequest is satisfied in property.2 

Example: Trustees of a testamentary trust were required to pay $50,000 to testator's child when the latter became 25 years of age, and payment was made in stock worth that amount. The excess of that amount over the basis (value at death) of the stock in the hands of the trust represents taxable gain to the trust from a "sale or other disposition."

If a stated percentage of the corpus is to be distributed to the beneficiary before termination of the trust, the situation is different. When property is distributed in compliance with such provision, there is not a satisfaction of 

20 Linde, 54-1 USTC 9384, 213 Fed. (2d) 1. 

Cert. denied, 348 U. S. 871. 

1 Nauss, 4 BTA 980, Dec. 1657. 

• Suisman v. Eaton, (DC) 36-2 ustc || 9443, 15 

Fed. Supp. 113, aff'd (CA-2) 83 Fed. (2d) 1019. 50

Ian obligation of the trust for a definite amount of cash or equivalent value in property, but rather a partial distribution of a share of the trust principal. 

Neither the trustee nor the beneficiary will realize taxable income by the distribution and receipt of the property.3

Also, where the will provides for the distribution of the entire net estate between beneficiaries "share and share alike", with no provision for any equal division of the specific property, and no conversion of the assets to cash is made for that purpose, it would seem that a distribution to such beneficiaries in accordance with their agreement that they will each receive certain specific property in satisfaction of their equal claims under the will should not be regarded as resulting in a "sale or exchange", even though one or more of them has gained an advantage by reason of a disproportionate enhancement of the value of their shares since the date of testator's death. The agreement should not be a taxable transaction involving liability to the estate.* 

Where a will bequeaths in trust an amount sufficient to use up the maximum allowable marital deduction of an estate, the dollar amount of the marital deduction is fixed and definite as of the date of death. If on the date of distribution to the marital trust, therefore, the legacy is satisfied in property which has increased in value, the estate realizes capital gain, just as though there had been a sale of the property for the amount of the legacy. By the same token, losses may be offset against the gain realized.* [51] Special relief for income from stock redemptions to pay death taxes and estate expenses. Hardship frequently occurs when an estate must sell or otherwise dispose of its assets to pay an estate or other death tax liability. When the assets consist in the main of stock, as in a family corporation, a liquidation of part or all of such holdings is often inevitable. 

A distribution by a corporation in redemption of stock is treated as being in part or full payment in exchange for the stock. But this general rule applies only if the redemption is not essentially equivalent to a dividend. If the redemption is considered to be essentially equivalent to a dividend, the shareholder, instead of having a capital gain or loss on the redemption of the stock, will be taxed at ordinary rates as though he had received an ordinary dividend."

The law, however, provides a measure of relief to estates faced with the problem of disposing of stock to pay death taxes. It provides that amounts received by estates in redemption or cancellation of stock cannot, if certain specified conditions are met, be taxed as dividend payments, thereby assuring the estate of capital gain or loss treatment.8 

This relief provision applies only to so much of the proceeds of the stock as does not exceed the sum of the estate, legacy, and succession taxes (including interest) imposed because of the decedent's death, and the funeral and administration expenses allowable as deductions from the gross estate. 

The stock must have comprised more than 35% of the value of the decedent's gross estate or more than 50% of the taxable estate, unless all the particular corporation's stock held by the estate is redeemed. If all the stock was redeemed, the distribution would not be an ordinary taxable dividend even without the application of this relief provision. 

8 Rev. Rul. 55-117, 1955-1 CB 233. 

4 M. L. Long, 35 BTA 95, Dec. 9525. 

217. * Rev. Rul. 56-270, 1956-1 CB 325.

Code Sec. 302. 

8 Code Sec. 303. 

151

1 The relief provision applies only to distributions made after the death of the decedent and during (1) the statutory period for assessment of estate tax, plus 90 days, or (2) if a petition for redetermination of a deficiency in such estate tax has been filed with the Tax Court, any time up to 60 days after the decision of the Tax Court becomes final. [ 51.01] Pitfalls to watch.-Code Sec. 303 is so worded that it also creates pitfalls. The provision is applicable to a distribution in redemption of stock to any shareholder, not just the decedent's estate. Also, the stock which is redeemed does not have to be held by the estate at the time of redemption so long as it was included in the decedent's estate. Moreover, the Commissioner's regulations (Reg. § 1.303-2(g)), state that in determining whether the total distributions in redemption of stock made within the period described above exceed the amount of the death taxes, plus interest, and funeral and administrative expenses, account must be taken of all redemption distributions without regard to whether or not any such distribution would be treated as a dividend were it not for Sec. 303. This reference to "all" redemption distributions would include those made not only to the executor or administrator of the estate, but also to heirs, legatees, donees of the decedent (where the gift was included in the gross estate), a surviving joint tenant, surviving spouse, appointee, or taker in default of appointment, or a trustee of a trust created by the decedent. 

It is this broad application of Code Sec. 303 to all types of redemptions during the period mentioned that creates the pitfalls previously mentioned. 

Example (1): A decedent owned stock in Corporation A, valued at $150,000 for estate tax purposes and comprising the entire outstanding stock of the corporation. His taxable estate was $250,000, on which death taxes were $66,000. 

His funeral and administration expenses amounted to $4,000. The corporation had accumulated earnings of $60,000. After the first year of administration, onethird of the stock (and other assets) was distributed to the decedent's widow and, at her request, was redeemed by the corporation for $50,000. The redemption came within the terms of Code Sec. 303, and absorbed $50,000 of the $70,000 "redemption allowance" authorized by Code Sec. 303. Two years later, in order to provide cash with which to pay death taxes, the corporation redeemed one-half of the estate's remaining shares, again for $50,000. This time, however, only $20,000 of the proceeds could be treated as received on a sale or exchange, under Code Sec. 303 as interpreted by the regulations. The tax treatment of the other $30,000 received by the estate would depend on its status under Code Secs. 301 and 302. The result, in most cases, would be ordinary dividend status for the entire $30,000, there being no change in proportionate interests of the stockholders. (the estate is the only shareholder at this point), no redemption of a shareholder's entire interest, no retraction of the business, and no other "business purpose" for the redemption.

Example (2): Assuming the same facts as in Example (1), except that the redemption of $50,000 of the estate's stock occurred before the redemption of the widow's shares, then both the widow and the estate would have treated the proceeds as having been received on a sale or exchange. The redemption of the estate's stock would have come within Code Sec. 303, and the $30,000 non-Sec. 

303 portion of the amount paid to the widow would have come within the scope of Code Sec. 302(b)(3) as a complete redemption of all of the widow's stock. 

This is one example of the pitfalls in Code Sec. 303. Situations in which a liquidation or sale of corporate assets is proposed can also have this result. 

If, for instance, an estate allows a "Sec. 303 corporation" to sell its assets and then liquidate, part of the Sec. 303 "redemption allowance" has been used ¶ 51.01

up, and may foreclose a later capital gain redemption under Code Sec. 303 on redemption of stock by another corporation-a result which might be avoided by having the estate sell the stock itself, or delaying the corporate liquidation until after the key Sec. 303 redemption has occurred.

Throughout this discussion, it was assumed that Code Sec. 303 will be applied to redemptions within its scope in strict chronological order, so that the first redemption (regardless of whether stock is redeemed from the estate or some other person) will be the first one charged against the Sec. 303 "redemption allowance," then the second redemption will be so charged, and so on.

This is not specifically required by either the law or the regulations. 

It is possible, therefore, that redemptions from the estate will be considered first, then those from other persons. However, this does not seem likely, particularly in view of the emphasis which the regulations put on the fact that all redemptions within the scope of Code Sec. 303 will be considered in determining whether (and presumably when) the Sec. 303 allowance will be exhausted. 

[¶ 51.02] If Code Sec. 303 inoperative.-Where the conditions for in voking the above relief provision are not present, or due to other circumstances, it may be advantageous or desirable to liquidate entirely the business. of the family corporation. This can be accomplished in two ways. The corporation can sell its assets and distribute the proceeds to the shareholders or the corporation can distribute the assets to the shareholders and a sale thereof can be made by the shareholders themselves. Every tax aspect of each procedure should be carefully worked out to secure the best tax advantage for the estate. 

[152] How are capital gains and losses to be treated?-Capital gains and losses are generally taken into account in computing taxable income just as if the estate or trust were an individual. In computing gross income no distinction is made between capital gains and losses allocable to income account and those allocable to corpus although for trust accounting purposes a distinction was made.

The gain realized by an estate upon the sale of its capital assets is taxable even though the sale is for the purpose of liquidation to pay creditors or to make distribution of shares to heirs or beneficiaries, and regardless of the fact that the tax will diminish the amount they receive. This rule applies also to a trust.

Whether gain or loss is ordinary income or loss or capital gain or loss depends on the character of the property in the hands of the estate or trustthat is, whether held for investment purposes, for sale to customers, or for use in a trade or business. It is possible for the character of the income to change in the hands of the executor of an estate. Thus, subdivision lots held by a real estate dealer became capital assets in the hands of the executor who held them only for liquidation and not for sale to customers in the ordinary course of business. See also ¶ 48 and 49.

For capital gain and loss purposes, the holding period of property in the hands of the estate begins on the date of death of the decedent. It does not include the decedent's holding period. Also, it does not begin on the later • W. D. Haden Est., 12 TCM 825, Dec. 

19,813 (M). 1 52

optional valuation date, even though the value on that date may have been used for estate tax purposes and for basis purposes. 9ª For a testamentary trust and its beneficiaries, the holding period of the property also dates from the testator's death. Any property later acquired by the trust, must be held for more than six months before the long-term capital gain or loss provisions will apply, but after distribution such required period may include the period of holding of the trust.

In reporting the income from sale of capital assets, estates and trusts may, like an individual, avail themselves of the net capital gain provisions of the Code. Estates and trusts are also entitled to the benefits of Code 

Sec. 1231.

If a part of corpus is sold at a loss, the beneficiary may conceivably suffer to the extent that the sources of future income from the trust or estate will be less than formerly. The trust or estate, as legal owner of the assets, may deduct from its gross income losses resulting from such sales, but the beneficiary, even though he is a remainderman, cannot deduct the losses,¹º except as a capital loss carry-over on termination of the trust or estate.¹ 11 [¶ 52.01] Capital gain deduction. The 50% deduction for the excess of net long-term capital gain over net short-term capital loss is available to the trust or estate for the purpose of computing taxable income. However, if capital gains are distributed to the beneficiaries, such gains must be excluded in determining whether, for the taxable year of the estate or trust, its net long-term capital gain exceeds its net short-term capital loss.12 

Example: A trust realized a gain of $1,000 upon the sale of stock held for 

10 months. Under the terms of the trust instrument all of such gain must be distributed during the taxable year to A, the sole income beneficiary. Assuming 

A must include all of such gain in his gross income, the trust is not entitled to any capital gain deduction for such gain. Assuming A had no other capital gains or losses for the taxable year, he would be entitled to a capital gain deduction of $500.

Where a will or trust instrument is silent on the treatment of gains from the sales of corpus, under most state laws, such gains are not distributable to the beneficiaries. They are added to, and become a part of, the corpus. 

Usually, therefore, capital gains will be taxed entirely to the estate or trust, which will get the capital gains deduction. 

For the effect of capital gains and losses on the computation of an estate's or trust's "distributable net income", which limits the amount deductible by the estate or trust for distributions to beneficiaries and the amount of the distributions taxable to the beneficiaries, see ¶ 72. [53] What is the basis of property received by an estate, trust or other person? If property, real or personal, is acquired by bequest, devise, or inheritance, or by the decedent's estate from the decedent, its basis is the fair market value at the date of the decedent's death. However, if the property would have to be included in determining the value of the decedent's gross estate, and if the decedent's gross estate at the date of death is more than $60,000 in value, the executor may-regardless of whether an estate 

Da Helvering v. R. V. N. Gambrill (S. Ct.), 9360, 313 U. S. 11; Special Ruling, 41-1 USTC 9-24-46.

¶ 52.01

ti tax is or is not actually payable-elect to have the estate's assets valued for estate tax purposes either at the date of death or one year later (called the optional valuation date). The valuation date used for estate tax purposes must also be used by the legatee, devisee, or heir in determining his basis. for the inherited property.18

The estate tax value of an asset is not binding as to the value for the purpose of computing basis. However, there is a presumption that the estate tax valuation is correct, and convincing evidence will be required to prove that a different value is appropriate.¹4

The above provisions apply to property received under a general power of appointment. Such property is deemed to be property passing from the individual who possessed such power, whether or not he exercised such power, by bequest or devise. This may be illustrated by the marital deduction type of trust where, for example, the husband gives his wife a life estate in part of property with a general power of appointment. When the wife dies, then, whether or not she has exercised the power, the person who receives the property takes as his basis the fair market value as of the date of her death or as of the optional valuation date, if an election is applicable and is made.15 [ 53.01] Basis of trust property.-When a grantor creates an inter vivos trust, the transfer of property to the trust as trust principal or corpus usually constitutes a gift. Accordingly, for purposes of determining gain, depreciation or depletion, the trust's basis will usually be the same as the basis of the property to the grantor. For purposes of determining loss, the basis will be either the grantor's basis or the value of the property at the time it was transferred to the trust, whichever is lower.16 

If the property was acquired by the trust by bequest or devise, the basis must be determined under the rules applicable to bequests and devises-that is, fair market value at date of death or as of the optional valuation date.

In the case of a sale of property by the grantor to the trust, the basis of the property to the trust will be the same as the basis to the grantor increased by the amount of any gain or decreased by the amount of any loss recognized to the grantor on the transfer to the trust.

Where property, which in the hands of the trustee has the same basis as in the hands of the grantor, is transferred by the trustee to a beneficiary who is a donee, the basis to such donee will be the same as the basis to the trustee and to the grantor of the trust. 

If a trust during its existence acquires property by purchase or exchange (other than from the grantor), the same general rules applicable to an individual will apply in determining the trust's basis for the property, as distinguished from the special rules described above. In other words, if property is acquired by a straight purchase from a third party, the basis of the property will be its cost. In every case, the basis, whether it be original "cost" or a substituted basis arising out of a tax-free exchange, at the date of acquisition, must be adjusted, that is, increased for capital additions, if any, and decreased for depreciation, depletion, or amortization allowed (but not less than the amount allowable) as deductions to the trust while it held the property.

[¶ 53.02] Basis to remainderman.-A remainderman who receives property after the termination of a trust upon the death of the life tenant receives the property by bequest or by gift, depending upon whether the trust was created by the grantor's will or during the grantor's lifetime. Therefore, if the trust was created by will, the basis of the property to the remainderman will be the fair market value at date of the testator's death or as of the optional valuation date. If the trust was created during the grantor's lifetime, the basis of the property will be that applicable to gift property generally as described above. There is an exception which applies to revocable trusts with life income reserved to the grantor. The remainderman, after the termination of such a trust on the grantor's death, uses the same basis as he would have had had he received the property as a bequest." This exception is also applicable, even though the trust is not revocable, where the grantor has a life income and reserves the right to make any change in the enjoyment of the trust through the exercise of a power to alter, amend, or terminate the trust.18 

For allocation of basis between the life tenant and remainderman while the life tenancy exists, see ¶[ 53.09 below. [¶ 53.03] Joint tenancies or tenancies by the entirety.—If property was acquired by a surviving joint tenant and the other joint tenant, or the spouse in the case of a joint tenancy by husband and wife, died after December 31, 1953, the survivor's basis of that part of the property that was required to be included in the gross estate takes as its basis the value that is used for estate tax purposes.19 The same applies to a surviving spouse in a tenancy by the entirety. Under the 1939 Code, the original basis carried through, unaffected by includibility of any part of the property in the decedent's estate. 

The value to be used for estate tax purposes is governed by the provisions of Code Sec. 2040. The Internal Revenue Service has ruled that under Code 

Sec. 2040, the amount includible in a decedent's gross estate on account of property which was jointly owned or held under a tenancy by the entirety and which was acquired by purchase is that portion of the fair market value of the property that the amount of the purchase price contributed by the decedent bears to the total purchase price of the property.20 

Example (1): A, a resident of Illinois, buys a home in 1941 for $20,000, paying the entire purchase price out of his own funds but taking title in himself and his wife as joint tenants. A elects not to pay a gift tax, indicating thereby that no gift is intended. In 1953 he dies, the property passing under the joint tenancy to his wife. The value of the property determined for the purpose of the estate tax is $40,000, and that entire amount is included in the "gross estate" in computing that tax. Assume now that the wife, in 1957, sells the property for $38,000. 

She has a recognized gain of $18,000, her basis being the original cost of $20,000. 

In no way is the basis affected by the estate tax valuation, where the spouse died before January 1, 1954. 

Example (2): Assume all the facts in Example (1) above, except that A died in 1957. His wife's basis on later sale of the home is the $40,000 estate tax value. She has a loss of $2,000. 

Example (3): Assume all the facts in Example (1) above, except that A died in 1957 and that B, his wife, contributed $4,000 of the purchase price. His wife's basis on a later sale of the house is cost as to 1/5th of the purchase price and the estate tax value as to 4/5ths, or $32,000-since A provided 4/5ths of the purchase price, that portion of the value at his death was included in his estate for estate tax purposes. Thus, the basis for gain on the sale is $36,000 ($32,000 + $4,000), and the recognized gain is $2,000. 

17 Code Sec. 1014(b)(2). 

18 Code Sec. 1014(b)(3). 

19 Code Sec. 1014(b)(9); Rev. Rul. 56-215, 1956-1 CB 324. 

20 Rev. Rul. 56-519, I. R. B. 1956-43, 8. ¶ 53.02

The 1954 Code basis rule, combined with the fact that in a number of states (District of Columbia, Indiana, Maryland, Michigan, Missouri, New York, Oregon and Pennsylvania) income from property held in the entireties by a husband and wife is divided equally between them, regardless of their relative contributions to the purchase price, can sometimes result in difficult computations.

For instance, the Internal Revenue Service has issued a ruling on this type of situation which involved a husband and wife who owned a farm and an apartment house in Indiana as tenants by the entirety. The wife had made the entire down payment on the farm, and they had given a purchase money mortgage for the balance which was paid out of income from the farm and proceeds of off-farm work by the husband. About 10 years after the purchase of the farm, they had purchased an apartment house, which was paid for entirely out of farm income, rental income, and proceeds of the husband's off-farm work. The couple held both the farm and apartment house as tenants by the entireties, and under Indiana law they each had a one-half interest in the properties.

The Internal Revenue Service held that under Code Sec. 1014(b)(9), the value of whatever portion of the farm was includible in the husband's estate on his death in 1955 (whether or not the estate was large enough to require an estate tax return or payment) became its basis to the widow; her basis for the portion not included in the husband's estate would be its cost (the down payment and reinvested income) minus depreciation. 

Under Code Sec. 2040, the portion of the farm includible in the husband's estate was held to be a fraction in which the numerator was the aggregate payments he had made and the denominator was the total purchase price plus improvements. The widow's total basis would be this portion plus the amounts she had paid out of her own funds or income from the property, and minus depreciation on the portion not includible in the decedent's estate.

W furnished a $20,000 down payment from her own funds, and the $46,000 balance was paid from farm income ($30,000) and H's off-the-farm earnings ($16,000). When H died in 1957, the fair market value of the farm was $75,000. Depreciation during H's lifetime was $19,800. W's basis would be computed as follows:

In Rev. Rul. 56-519, the facts indicated that both the farm and the apartment building had been fully paid for at the time of the decedent's death. It was proper, therefore, to summarize the effect of Code Sec. 2040 as requiring inclusion in the decedent's estate of "that portion of the fair market value of the property [which] the amount of the purchase price contributed by the decedent bears to the total purchase price of the property." وو If part of the purchase price had still not been paid at the time of death, a different ratio would have been proper. As Reg. 105, Sec. 81.22 says, "it is not the intent of the statute that there should be [included in the decedent's estate] a greater part or proportion [of the property] than is represented by an outlay of funds, which, in the first instance, were decedent's own (Italics added.) In other words if a husband contributed $4,000 and his wife $1,000 as the down payment on a $40,000 farm, and he died the next day, 80% (4,000/5,000) of the value of the farm would be includible in his estate and would be treated by the widow as having been acquired from a decedent for purposes of the basis rules in Code Sec. 1014(b) (9).

[ 53.04] Property transferred in contemplation of death.-Property transferred by the decedent during his lifetime in contemplation of death, and included in his gross estate on that premise, takes as its basis the estate tax value if it is sold or otherwise disposed of after the donor's death. This rule is applicable only if the decedent dies after December 31, 1953.2 If it was transferred by gift, the basis rules for gift property govern up to the time of the decedent's death. Thereafter, the basis changes to the estate tax value. 

If depreciable income-producing property or business property is involved, and is not disposed of before the donor's death, the donee's basis of the property during the lifetime of the donor is the same as it would have been in the hands of the donor (adjusted to the date of the gift). After the death of the donor, if the property was included in the gross estate because the transfer was held to have been made in contemplation of death, a new basis arisesthe estate tax value. Adjustment of this basis must be made for depreciation allowed or allowable, under the applicable law at the time the depreciation was sustained, during the time the taxpayer held the property. 

Example (6): A father, 75 years old, in January, 1955, transferred an apartment building to his son as a gift. The building had cost him $75,000, and the land, $10,000. He had deducted $15,000 depreciation on the building while he held it, and this amount was allowed and allowable. The son's basis for depreciation of the building was $60,000, which was the donor's adjusted basis. The son estimates the remaining life as 40 years, and deducts $1,500 depreciation each year in 1955 and 1956. In January, 1957, the father dies. Due to his age and the condition of his health when the transfer was made, the estate is unable to overcome the presumption that the transfer was made in contemplation of death, and the property is included in the father's gross estate at its January, 1957, value of $100,000. The son's basis for 1957 and later years becomes $97,000. The property is treated as a new acquisition from the decedent. Although the $100,000 fair market value included in the decedent's gross estate in effect reflects not only appreciation, but also depreciation sustained up to January, 1957, nevertheless the $100,000 is required to be reduced by the $3,000 depreciation the son deducted in 1955 and 1956, under a strict interpretation of the law. [¶ 53.05] Tenancies in common.-An undivided one-half interest in property held as tenant in common is a wholly distinct interest. In such case the part, if any, acquired on the death of the other (or another) tenant in common is property acquired by bequest, devise or inheritance and the basis thereof is determined accordingly.

Example (7): A husband buys a home in 1943 for $16,000, paying for it out of his own separate funds but taking title in himself and his wife as tenants in common. In 1957 he dies, leaving a will under which his half interest in the home goes to his wife. Assume that his debts, if any, are paid off out of other assets of the estate. The result is that upon his death she has the entire property. 

The home is valued, let us say, at $38,000 for estate tax purposes. Only the decedent's half, valued at $19,000, is included in the "gross estate" in computing the tax. Assume now that the wife later sells the property for $34,000. Her gain is computed as follows:

2 Code Sec. 1014(b) (9). 

¶ 53.05

[ 53.06] Community property.-When property passes by devise, bequest, or inheritance, the basis to the devisee, legatee, or heir is the fair market value as of the date of death or as of the optional valuation date. This applies also to any portion of the deceased's share of community property which a surviving spouse may receive. However, special rules govern the basis to the surviving spouse of the share which represents his survivor's interest in the community property, depending on the date of death of the deceased spouse. Where death occurs after 1947 and at least one-half of the whole community interest was includible in the gross estate of the deceased spouse (but without regard to whether or not a tax was payable on the net estate), the survivor's basis in his or her one-half share of the community property is determined with reference to fair market value as of the date of death or as of the optional valuation date. In some states, if the wife dies first, her interest in community property ceases and none of it is includible in her gross estate. Where this is true, as in New Mexico, the surviving husband's basis would be determined with reference to cost of the property (adjusted) to the community. Where death occurred before 1948, the general rule is that the survivor's basis in his or her one-half share of the community property is determined from its cost (adjusted) to the community. [53.07] Income in respect of a decedent.-Income items which would have been receivable by the decedent had he lived, and which are received by his estate or beneficiaries, are taxed to the estate or beneficiaries when received, and retain the same character they would have had in the hands of the decedent. Such income is not affected by the basis rules.* [¶ 53.08] Adjustments to basis. In the hands of every person who acquires the property of a decedent, or any estate or interest therein, by bequest, devise, or inheritance, the basis of the property is always the same (1) whether such person be the executor or administrator, the heir, the legatee, the devisee, the trustee of a trust created by will, or any beneficiary of such trust, and whatever the nature of any such person's interest or estate may be, and (2) whether during or after administration and settlement of the estate of the decedent, during or after the term of any trust under the will, or before or after the distribution by the executor or administrator, or the trustee. 

Adjustments to basis are made in accordance with the same principles. 

Thus, the deductions for depreciation and for depletion are allowed or allowable to a legal life tenant, as if the life tenant were the absolute owner of the property, and constitute an adjustment to the basis of the property not only in the hands of the life tenant but also in the hands of the remainderman and every other person to whom the same uniform basis is applicable. Similarly, the depreciation and depletion deductions allowed or allowable, both to the trustee and to the trust beneficiaries, constitute an adjustment to the basis of the property not only in the hands of the trustee but also in the hands of the trust beneficiaries and every other person to whom the uniform basis is applicable. 

Similarly, adjustments in respect of capital expenditures or losses, tax-free distributions or other distributions applicable in reduction of basis, or other items for which the basis is adjustable, are made without regard to which one of the persons to whom the same uniform basis is applicable makes the capital expenditures or sustains the capital losses, or to whom the tax-free .or other distributions are made, or to whom the deductions are allowed or allowable. • Code Sec. 1014(b)(6). • Code Sec. 1014(c). ¶ 53.06

When the optional valuation date is applied, no depreciation is allowable from the date of death to the optional valuation date. Adjustments to basis for gain or loss, on subsequent disposition of the property, on account of depreciation sustained, etc., are to be made only from the date of death or the optional valuation date, whichever is applicable. 

[ 53.09] Allocation of Basis Between Life Tenant and Remainderman. 

—Where real estate is transmitted at death to a life tenant with the remainder in fee to another person, there is involved not only allocation of the basis of the full property of which the life estate and the remainder are a part between life and the remainder interests at the time of the creation of those interests, but also adjustment of that allocation as of the time of their sale or transfer.4ª 

For the purpose of computing depreciation or depletion, no allocation of basis is required, since Code Secs. 167 (g) and 611(b) provide that the life tenant shall take the entire allowable deduction. 

Adjusted basis of a life tenant (or remainderman) may be computed by application of the following formula: 

Basis of life tenant's (or remainderman's) interest at date of sale (the item to be determined).

Basis of entire property determined in accordance with section 1014 of I. R. C. (uniform basis), adjusted to the time of sale for items required by section 1016 of I. R. C.

Part of the uniform basis attributable to the life tenant's (or remainderman's) interest as of the date of sale. 

Unadjusted basis of entire property determined in accordance with section 1014 of I. R. C. (uniform basis).

If a life interest or a remainder interest in a divided or undivided fraction of the entire property is sold, it is necessary, as a preliminary step, to allocate the proper portion of the uniform basis (adjusted and unadjusted) to the fractional interest being sold, in order to obtain the denominators in the above illustrative equation. 

Example (8): Suppose improved realty worth $20,000 at the date of decedent's death on January 1, 1953, is devised to A for life, with remainder over to B. Four years later, on January 1, 1957, A sells his life estate for $12,500. 

During each of the four years 1953 to 1956, inclusive, A was allowed a deduction of $300 for depreciation. A's age was 35 at the date of decedent's death. The adjusted basis of the property (determined as shown below) is $12,012.84, and the taxable gain to A is accordingly $487.16 ($12,500 (proceeds of sale) minus $12,012.84 (adjusted basis)).

To compute A's basis, it should first be noted that the uniform basis of the property is $20,000, which is adjusted to $18,800 at the date of the sale by reason of $1,200 allowed depreciation. The part of the uniform basis attributable to A's life interest as of January 1, 1957, is computed by multiplying $20,000 by 3½ per cent, giving a hypothetical annuity of $700. By reference to Table I, of the valuation tables appearing in Sec. 81.10 of Reg. 105 (the estate tax regulations), the annuity factor at age 39 is found to be 18.2566. That factor, multiplied by $700, gives $12,779.62 as the part of the uniform basis attributable to A's interest as of the date of sale. Those figures may be conveniently arranged in the form of the following illustrative equation: 

I. T. 3911, 1948-1 CB 66. ¶ 53.09

Since 

A's basis $18,800 ($20,000 less $1,200 depreciation) $12,779.62 $20,000 $12,779.62 $20,000 is equivalent to the decimal .638981, A's basis is $18,800 multiplied by .638981, or $12,012.84.

The same general procedure is followed for a separate sale of a remainder interest, except that the numerator of the right-hand fraction is obtained by multiplying the unadjusted basis by the "column 3" factor shown in the appropriate valuation table of Reg. 105, Sec. 81.10, opposite the remainderman's age.

Where the life tenant and the remainderman join in a sale and transfer of their entire interest in real property, each will realize gain or sustain loss measured by the difference between the basis of his respective interest at the time of the sale and the portion of the entire proceeds of sale attributable to such interest. The part of the proceeds attributable to each interest shall be in the same proportion to the entire proceeds of sale as the basis of each such interest at the time of sale bears to the adjusted uniform basis. 

Example (9): Suppose, as in Example (8), that improved realty worth $20,000 at the date of decedent's death on January 1, 1953, is devised to A, whose age is 35, for life, with remainder over to B, and that four years later, on January 1, 1957, A and B by joint conveyance sell the entire property for $30,000. During each of the four years 1953 to 1956, inclusive, the allowable deduction for depreciation amounted to $300, which deduction was taken by A in his Federal income tax returns under the authority of Code Sec. 167(g). 

The part of the uniform basis attributable to A's life estate on the date of sale, January 1, 1957, for a person 39 years of age is $12,779.62. (See the method of computation set forth above, in Example (7).) The part of the uniform basis attributable to B's remainder on the date of sale, January 1, 1957, is obtained by multiplying $20,000 (the value of the property at the time of decedent's death) by .36102 (the figure opposite the number of years nearest the actual age of A, the life tenant, in column 3, Table I of the valuation tables appearing in Reg. 

105, Sec. 81.10). The part of the uniform basis attributable to B's remainder interest on January 1, 1957, is, therefore, $7,220.40 ($20,000 × 36102). The aggregate value of the uniform basis attributable to the life and remainder interests is $20,000.02 ($12,779.62 + $7,220.40).

Casualty loss on property held by a life tenant.-An individual who has a legal life estate in property may deduct a casualty loss. However, since the damage sustained by the property is an injury to both the life estate and the remainder interest, it is necessary to apportion the loss between the two interests. Both the life tenant and the remainderman are entitled to deduct their proportionate shares, subject to the usual limitation that the deductible loss may not exceed the taxpayer's adjusted basis. This is the import of a 

Tax Court decision which also held, in effect, that the amount of the deductible loss sustained by the life estate interest should be determined by reference to the actuarial tables in Section 81.10 of Estate Tax Regs. 105.4b 

The example below shows how to compute a life tenant's portion of the loss and how to determine whether it comes within the adjusted basis limitation. 

Example (10): Assume that the taxpayer has a life estate in a residence, acquired from her husband, who died in September, 1954. The fair market value of the property at the time of his death was $59,000; the taxpayer added a new roof to the residence in 1955, which cost her $1,000; a storm occurred on

February 12, 1956, causing a $30,000 loss in value of the property; and taxpayer was 45 years of age at the time of the storm. The portion of the $30,000 loss deductible by taxpayer on her return for 1956 is computed by reference to the 

32% Table (Table I) in Section 81.10 of Regulations 105 as follows:

Portion of loss deductible ($30,000 × .576639) 

Cost of removing debris. $16.4754 .576639 $17,299.17 1,341.56

Although the Bliss case, on which the above example is based, involved only the life tenant's deduction, consistency would require that the portion of the loss which was not deductible by the life tenant should be deductible by the remainderman.

Also, it should be noted that the "factor" used above in determining the life tenant's share of the uniform basis (.576639) is computed by the method shown in Regs. 105. In "proposed" income tax regulations under Code Sec. 1014 (Reg. § 1.1014-5) there is a corresponding table in which these factors are already computed. That proposed table, for instance, shows a factor of .57664 for a life tenant at age 45, and a factor of .42336 for the remainderman at that time. It should also be noted that these factors change every year, so that as the life tenant grows older the appropriate factor (and his share of the uniform basis), is reduced.

The Commissioner's regulations under the 1939 Code (Reg. § 39.113 (a) (5)- 

1(e) (2)) are authority for having the life tenant add the cost of the roof, for which he paid, to the fair market value of the property (unadjusted uniform basis) in determining the adjusted basis of the property (adjusted uniform basis). Presumably, such an adjustment would be required by the regulations even where separate property, such as a garage, is added to the property by the owner of the life estate. The rule states that adjustments for capital expenditures or losses, tax-free distributions, or other distributions applicable in reduction of basis, or other items for which the basis is adjustable, are made without regard to which one of the persons to whom the uniform basis is applicable in this case, the life tenant-makes the capital expenditures or sustains the capital losses, or to whom the tax-free or other distributions are made, or to whom the deductions are allowed or allowable. ¶ 53.09

[54] What income is taxed to estate in community property states?- 

The divisibility of income between a decedent's estate and the surviving spouse during the administration of the estate depends upon the state law. If the surviving spouse is permitted to retain ownership of his or her half of the income from community property during the administration of the estate, such income is not taxable to the estate but to the surviving spouse. This has been held to be the rule in Arizona, California, Idaho," Louisiana,8 New 

Mexico, Texas,10 and Washington." In one case (involving Texas law) the wife's estate was not even taxable on her one-half of the community income where all debts had been paid and the husband received the entire community property, although the estate was still being administered so far as her separate property was concerned.¹2 

? 

[55] Deductions are generally the same as for individuals. In general, an estate or trust is allowed the same deductions for expenses incurred in carrying on a trade or business or in the production of income as an individual. 

While an estate may ordinarily take all deductions which the decedent could have taken if he had continued to live, this is true only in a limited sense. 

Except by statutory grace, and upon conditions, the estate could not, for instance, take the deductions allowable in the last return of the decedent on the accrual basis, although his incurred expenses were paid by the estate. 

Moreover, when it is stated that a trust is allowed the same deductions as an individual, it is meant that ordinarily the trust's deductible expenses and losses may correspond to those available to an individual. Special rules govern certain deductions, such as those for depreciation and depletion (see 62 and 63), but for the most part the principles applied are the same as those used in determining whether the expense deductions would be available to an individual.

In addition to the usual deductions, however, a decedent's estate or a trust is allowed deductions for income distributed or distributable to beneficiaries. The determination of an estate's or trust's distribution deduction is subject to special and detailed discussion beginning at ¶ 71. 

It is specifically provided in the law that neither the optional tax table nor the standard deduction may be elected by an estate or trust.13 [156] Personal exemptions and credits are allowable.-An estate is entitled to a deduction for personal exemption of $600. But the exemption for a trust is limited to $100 unless the trust is required to distribute all of its income currently, in which case the exemption is $300.14 The estate or trust is entitled to the full exemption, even where the return is for a fractional part of the year.

A trust which is required to distribute all its income currently will be entitled to a $300 exemption even though it may also, distribute an amount from corpus in the taxable year and even though it is required to make a distribution which would qualify for a charitable contribution deduction as

described at 70. A trust for the payment of an annuity is allowed an exemption of $300 in a taxable year in which the amount of the annuity required to be paid equals or exceeds all the income of the trust for the taxable year.15 

Credit for partially tax-exempt interest.—The credit for partially taxexempt interest under Code Sec. 35 is allowed in full to the estate or trust if no part of the income of an estate or trust is taxable to any beneficiary. Should any part of the income of the estate or trust be included in the income of beneficiaries, then there is allowed to the beneficiaries, as a credit, their proportionate share of such partially tax-free interest.16

Foreign tax credit.-An estate or trust is entitled to the foreign tax credit (Code Sec. 901), but only for so much of the taxes as is not properly allocable to the beneficiaries.17 

Dividends-received credit.—An estate or trust is entitled to the credit for dividends received under Code Sec. 34, but only for so much of the dividends as is not properly allocable to the beneficiary.18 See also ¶ 46. 

[157] Expenses of administration can be deducted by fiduciary.—Rea sonable amounts paid or incurred by the fiduciary of an estate or trust on account of administration expenses, including fiduciaries' fees and expenses of litigation, which are ordinary and necessary in connection with the performance of the duties of administration are deductible as nonbusiness expenses under Code Sec. 212. However, to the extent such expenses are allocable to the production or collection of tax-exempt income they are not deductible. 

Expenses incurred in defending or protecting the estate's or trust's title to property, or recovering such property, are not deductible. They are capital expenditures. Similarly, expenditures incurred in protecting or asserting the trust's rights to property of a decedent as legatee under a testamentary trust are nondeductible capital expenditures. An item which is nondeductible is not rendered deductible by the fact that property held by the estate or trust will be sold to satisfy a claim, which property would otherwise be held for the production of income.

Expenses incident to the preparation of the estate's or trust's income tax return are deductible.

It is immaterial whether the expenses, if deductible, are paid from the corpus or from income of the estate or trust.19 They derive their character not from the fund from which they are paid, but from the purposes for which they are incurred.

Although fire insurance premiums carried on a house owned by the estate and on which it is entitled to receive rent from a tenant are deductible, the payment of a premium on an insurance policy on the life of a debtor to secure payment of his indebtedness to the estate is not deductible.20 

If the estate is engaged in winding up the decedent's business, it is entitled to deduct the ordinary and necessary expenses incurred in connection therewith. It may even take a deduction where it would not have been available to the decedent. Thus, where the decedent operated a farm as a "hobby," that is, without sufficient profit motive, and the estate was compelled to continue its operation pending disposition of the farm, the executor could

properly deduct the ordinary expenses of operations during the period of administration until a sale or other disposition was made of the farm property.¹ [58] Deductions "in respect of a decedent" may be claimed by estate.— 

The business expenses, non-business expenses, interest and taxes for which the decedent was liable, which were not properly allowable as a deduction in his last taxable year or any prior taxable year, are deductible by the estate when it pays them. If the estate was not liable to pay those obligations, then they are deductible when paid by the person who, by bequest, devise, or inheritance from the decedent or by reason of the death of the decedent acquires an interest in the property of the decedent subject to the obligation.2 

For details, see ¶ 24.

No medical deduction is allowable in the case of an estate or trust since it cannot incur the expenses on its own behalf and has no dependents as that term is defined in the law. However, medical expenses for the care of the decedent, paid by an estate within one year after a decedent's death, are treated as having been paid by the decedent when incurred and accordingly may be deducted on the decedent's return for the year incurred (usually the decedent's last return) if not deducted for estate tax purposes. See 26. 3 [59] How much interest is deductible?—An estate or trust is allowed to deduct interest paid (whether out of income or corpus) or accrued on its obligations in the same manner as an individual. Several applications of this deduction are peculiar to estates. Thus, where the estate pays interest for delinquent payment of the Federal estate tax, that interest is deductible. The same rule would apply to interest paid for delinquency in paying state inheritance taxes. Also deductible is interest paid by the estate on overdue legacies, where imposed by local law. In this connection, however, the Court of Claims has held that interest on a specific legacy paid in the year the estate terminated was to be treated as part of the estate's distribution deduction rather than interest as such. As a result the interest on the legacy was exempt from tax in the hands of the legatee to the extent it exceeded the net income of the estate for the year of its termination. See further ¶ 78.01 below.

As in the case of individuals, interest is not deductible on indebtedness incurred or continued to purchase or carry obligations (other than obligations of the United States issued after September 24, 1917, and originally subscribed for by the estate or trust taxpayer) the interest upon which is wholly exempt from Federal income taxes." [60] Can the estate or trust deduct taxes?—An estate or trust is allowed the same deductions for taxes as an individual. Federal income or estate taxes, state inheritance, estate, legacy, or succession taxes, or gift taxes, are not allowable as deductions on any income tax return. Nor are they allowable as deductions "in respect of a decedent" as explained in ¶ 58 above. Federal estate taxes, however, may be deducted under the conditions outlined in ¶ 28 above.

Federal import duties, and Federal excise and stamp taxes may be deducted as business expenses. Stamp taxes on a deed conveying title to estate property are deductible. State or city income taxes are deductible in all cases by

The Commissioner has followed the rule that liability for property taxes attaches on the assessment date, even though the amount is not ascertainable on that date, and therefore, the taxes accrue as of that date. However, in 1956 he accepted a Tax Court decision to the effect that Delaware real estate taxes accrue on the lien date and not the earlier assessment date.6 Where the liability for taxes is contested, there can be no accrual until liability is established. 

Real property taxes on real estate sold must be apportioned between the seller and the buyer according to the number of days in the real property tax year that each holds the property. Proration is required whether or not the seller and purchaser actually apportion the tax, and whether they are on the cash or the accrual basis." 

Example: An estate sells real estate to X on August 1, 1957. Both the estate and X use the cash basis. Taxes for the real property tax year April 1, 1957 to March 31, 1958, become due and payable on May 15, 1958. X pays the real estate taxes when they fall due. Regardless of any agreement between the parties, for federal income tax purposes 122/365 of the real estate taxes are treated as imposed upon the estate and are deductible by it. 

Under the provisions of Code Sec. 266, taxes and carrying charges, otherwise deductible, which, in the opinion of the Commissioner, are, under sound accounting principles, chargeable to capital account, may be capitalized at the election of the taxpayer. By reason of this provision, one court permitted an estate to capitalize real estate taxes and mortgage interest on rental property while it was being rented, where the executor held the property for sale and liquidation and did not deduct the expenses from the incidental rental income during the period the property was being held for sale. The executor contended that rental of the property was incidental to the primary purpose of holding it for an advantageous sale, and that this was the proper treatment for fiduciary accounting purposes. It is to a taxpayer's advantage to exercise an election to capitalize taxes and carrying charges and add them to the basis of the property where a deduction would not result in a tax benefit. 7a [61] Double deductions for certain items are disallowed.-For estate tax purposes, the Code allows certain deductions from the gross estate in computing the taxable estate. Of these, administration expenses and losses during administration might include items which would also be deductible from gross income for income tax purposes. 

To prevent duplication of the deductions, the law provides that amounts allowable under Code Secs. 2053 and 2054 as a deduction in computing the taxable estate of a decedent shall not be allowed as a deduction in computing the taxable income of the estate unless there is filed a statement to the effect that the items have not been claimed or allowed as a deduction from the gross estate of the decedent and that of the right to have such deduction allowed for estate tax purposes are waived. The Commissioner permits a fiduciary managing a decedent's estate to wait for the full limitation period before filing a waiver of the right to deduct administration expenses on the estate tax return. Actually, the rule is that the estate's income tax return can be filed without claiming the deduction; later, when it is possible to compute the

estate tax with and without these deductions, a comparison can be made and, if the advantage lies with an income tax deduction, the waiver can be filed and a refund claim filed using the expenses as income tax deductions. The election or a switch from estate to income tax deductions, accompanied by the waiver can be made in this manner even if the estate tax return has been filed, provided neither return is closed. But once a waiver is filed, it is conclusive and can not be revoked in order to claim the estate tax deduction. 

The above provision does not apply to deductions for items accrued at the date of a decedent's death, so that they are allowable deductions for estate tax purposes as claims against the estate and are also allowable as deductions in respect of a decedent for income tax purposes. 

Example (1): A cash-basis individual dies, owing $100 interest. The interest is paid by the estate. The estate can deduct the interest for income tax purposes as well as for estate tax purposes, since this item had "accrued" at the date of death.

Items not accrued at the date of death, so that they are allowable for estate tax purposes only as administration expenses, are not deductible in computing the taxable income of the estate unless a statement is filed waiving the deduction for estate tax purposes.

Example (2): A cash-basis debtor dies. On the first of the following month, $100 of interest on the debt becomes due and is paid by the estate. The estate may not deduct the interest for income tax purposes unless it waives the deduction for estate tax purposes.

As long as there is no duplication of deductions, administration expenses may be the basis of estate tax deduction in one year and income tax deduction the next.10 Statutory fees for regular probate services and fees for preparation of estate and income tax returns, if they are ordinary and necessary expenses of the estate, are, to the extent not attributable to tax-exempt income, deductible either on the estate tax return or on the income tax return filed by the estate, but not on both. 

If selling expenses connected with the sale of property, broker's commissions, etc., on sales of real estate, were taken as a deduction in computing the taxable estate for estate tax purposes, they may not be allowed to reduce the sales price when reported for income tax purposes. 11 Funeral expenses are not deductible by an estate on its income tax return under the provisions of Code Sec. 642(g).¹ 12 [62] Depreciation deduction is to be apportioned.-Depreciation is deductible by an estate or trust, but only to the extent the deduction is not apportioned to the beneficiaries.13 For the purpose of allocating the depreciation deduction, the term "beneficiaries" includes charitable beneficiaries. 

In the case of property held in trust, the deduction is apportioned between the income beneficiaries and the trustee in proportion to the income allocable to each, unless the trust instrument or local law requires or permits the trustee to maintain a reserve for depreciation. This means that if the trustee does charge depreciation (crediting an equivalent amount to corpus, in order to maintain the trust principal intact), the depreciation allowable for income tax purposes will first be allocated to the trustee, up to the amount he actually
