However, if under the trust instrument or local law, the income of the trust computed without regard to depreciation is to be distributed to a named beneficiary, then the beneficiary is entitled to the deduction to the exclusion of the trustee. The Commissioner in his regulations state that no effect shall be given to any allocation of the depreciation deduction which gives any beneficiary or the trustee a share of such deduction greater than his pro rata share of the trust income, irrespective of any provisions in the trust instrument, except when the trust instrument or local law requires or permits the trustee to maintain a reserve for depreciation.¹4 

But see the discussion of Rev. Rul. 56-105 in ¶ 63 below.

A life tenant is entitled to the entire amount of allowable depreciation. In other words, the deduction is computed as if the life tenant were the absolute owner of the property, without any apportionment between the life tenant and the remainderman.

It has been held that where, under the terms of the trust instrument, no provision was made for the deduction of depreciation by the trust, the beneficiary, who was entitled to all of the trust income, could deduct depreciation suffered by the trust property though in the particular year there was no income from the property available for distribution.15 

For estates, there is only one rule. Depreciation is apportioned between the estate and the heirs, legatees and devisees on the basis of the estate income allocated to each.16 [63] How is the depletion deduction treated?-Life tenants (but not remaindermen), trusts and beneficiaries (including charitable beneficiaries) are all entitled to their respective shares of the depletion allowance. In the case of trust property, the same general rules used for depreciation are applicable. The allowable deduction for depletion is to be apportioned between the income beneficiaries and the trustee on the basis of the trust income allocable to each. However, if the trust instrument or local law requires or permits the trustee to maintain a reserve for depletion in any amount, then the deduction is first allocated to the trustee to the extent that income is set aside for a depletion reserve. Any part of the deduction in excess of the income set aside for the reserve is then to be apportioned between the income beneficiaries and the trust on the basis of the trust income (in excess of the income set aside for the reserve) allocable to each. If under the trust instrument or local law the income of the trust computed without regard to depletion is to be distributed to a named beneficiary, the beneficiary is entitled to the deduction to the exclusion of the trustee. The Commissioner's "proposed" regulations (Reg. § 1.611-1(c)(4)) also state that no effect shall be given to any allocation of the depletion deduction which gives any beneficiary or the trustee a share of such deduction greater than his pro rata share of the trust income, irrespective of any provisions in the trust instrument, except when the trust instrument or local law requires or permits the trustee to maintain a reserve for depletion.¹7

The Commissioner has ruled that where the manner in which the deduction for depletion may be apportioned and allocated between the income

However, the ruling appears to be contrary to the Commissioner's own depreciation regulations and "proposed" depletion regulations wherein it is stated that no effect shall be given to an allocation of the depreciation or depletion deduction which gives any beneficiary or trustee a share of the deduction greater than his pro rata share of the trust income, except for the reserve situation.

[164] Are special amortization deductions permitted?-An estate or trust is allowed amortization deductions for emergency and grain storage facilities in the same manner and to the same extent as in the case of an individual. However, apportionment is required between the beneficiaries and the fiduciary under the same principles as for depreciation and depletion, as described in ¶ 62 and ¶ 63.19 

[¶ 65] What are the deductible losses of an estate or trust? Deductions for losses are allowed to an estate or trust to the same extent as in the case of an individual. That is, the losses sustained during the taxable year, not compensated for by insurance or otherwise, from a trade or business, from a transaction entered into for profit, or from a casualty or theft are deductible. 

Casualty and theft losses are allowed only if at the time of the filing of the return the loss has not been claimed as a deduction from the gross estate of the decedent in a Federal estate tax return. See [61. Where an individual has a legal life estate in property, the Tax Court has ruled that he is entitled to a casualty loss deduction.19 However, since the damage sustained by the property is an injury to both the life estate and the remainder interest, it is necessary to apportion the loss between the two interests. Both the life tenant and the remainderman are entitled to deduct their proportionate shares, subject to the usual limitation that the deductible loss may not exceed the taxpayer's adjusted basis. For more detailed discussion and an example showing how the casualty loss is to be apportioned, see ¶53:09. Although there is no case or ruling by the Commissioner as authority, it would appear that a casualty loss sustained during administration of an estate should also be apportioned between the estate and the party receiving the property from the estate on termination.

Capital losses from the sale of trust or estate corpus, title to which is in the trust or estate, are deductible only by the fiduciary. Therefore, if a trust or estate sells part of its corpus at a loss, even though the beneficiary may conceivably suffer to the extent that the trust or estate sources of future income will be less than formerly, only the trust or estate, as legal owner of the capital assets, may deduct from its gross income the losses resulting from such sales.20 However, if under local law, legal title to real estate is considered 

18 Rev. Rul. 56-105, 1956-1 CB 209. 

19 Code Sec. 642(f); Reg. § 1.642(f)-1. 

19a K. B. Bliss, 27 TC -, No. 93, Dec. 22,250.

Bisbee v. Fahs, (DC) 49-1 USTC 9139, 80 Fed.

to be in the residuary legatee from the moment of the decedent's death, a loss on sale of the property may be deductible by the residuary legatee, the sale being considered as made by the estate or trust as an agent.¹ 

Capital loss carry-overs are allowed to the beneficiaries of an estate or trust on termination. See ¶ 67. [ 65.01] Distinction between decedent's and estate's losses.-A distinction must be made between losses sustained by the decedent and those sustained by the estate. A loss sustained by the decedent during his last taxable year, before death, is deductible only by him in the return filed on his behalf. If the event determining worthlessness of a debt or security occurred after his death, the loss can be claimed only by the estate while those assets were in its possession. Apportionment of losses between the decedent and the estate would only be possible in the case of a business bad debt, where partial loss is taken by the decedent, and the balance of the debt became worthless after death. Deduction for partial worthlessness of a nonbusiness bad debt is forbidden by the Code. Therefore, a loss from the worthlessness of a nonbusiness bad debt is to be claimed by either the decedent or his estate and cannot be allocated between them on the basis of partial worthlessness. Also, a nonbusiness bad debt is subject to the capital loss limitations. 

An estate or trust is not entitled to a deductible loss in respect to income, or a right thereto, received under Code Sec. 691(a)(1), or from the sale or other disposition of such a right under Code Sec. 691(a)(2). Beneficiaries (including trusts) who receive no distribution from an estate during a year when the allowable deductions of an estate exceed the gross income thereof, are not entitled to offset against income from other sources their share of such a loss of the estate. As to unused loss carry-overs and excess deductions on termination of an estate or trust, see ¶ 67, below.

A taxpayer who has a life estate in the income of a trust may not deduct amounts representing shrinkage (by whatever name called) in the value of such interest due to the lapse of time.*

If a remainderman purchases the life interest in his estate, in the Seventh Circuit he can amortize the cost of his purchase over the life expectancy of the life tenant. The net effect is to convert taxable income into a recovery of capital.

Example: H and W each created a trust naming the other as life beneficiary and their son as remainderman. Subsequently, the son purchased the life interest from both life tenants. The son can write off the purchase price over the period of the life expectancies of his parents. The purchase does not merge the life and remainder interests.

Losses from sales and exchanges of property between the persons or groups listed below are specifically disallowed deduction by Code Sec. 267. (1) A grantor and a fiduciary of any trust; (2) A fiduciary of a trust and a fiduciary of another trust, if the same person is a grantor of both trusts; (3) A fiduciary of a trust and a beneficiary of such trust; (4) A fiduciary of a trust and a beneficiary of another trust, if the same person is a grantor of both trusts; 1 Arrott v. Heiner, (CA-3) 37-2 USTC 1 9491, 92 

Fed. (2d) 773. 'Hoffman, 36 BTA 972, Dec. 9820. • Studebaker, 2 BTA 1020, Dec. 902. • Code Sec. 273. 

L. Bell v. Harrison, (CA-7) 54-1 USTC | 9351, 212 Fed. (2d) 253. ¶ 65.01

(5) A fiduciary of a trust and a corporation more than 50% in value of the outstanding stock of which is owned, directly or indirectly, by or for the trust or by or for a person who is a grantor of the trust. 

The good faith of the parties in the transaction has no bearing on the application of the above provision, which is strictly construed. 

The Internal Revenue Service has ruled that where stock owned by an estate was sold at a loss by the executor to a trust, which had been created by the decedent-husband for his wife before his death, the loss was a capital loss which was deductible from the estate's gross income. The loss was not disallowed by Code Sec. 267.6 

[66] The net operating loss deduction is allowed. The net operating loss deduction under Code Sec. 172 is allowed to estates and trusts. However, the following exceptions and limitations apply: (1) In computing gross income and deductions for the purposes of Code Sec. 172, a trust is to exclude that portion of the income and deductions attributable to the grantor or another person under Code Secs. 

671-678 (relating to grantor trusts (see ¶ 89-93)). (2) An estate or trust is not, for the purposes of Code Sec. 172, to avail itself of the charitable contributions deduction under Code Sec. 

642(c) and the deduction for distributions under Code Secs. 651 and 661."

Beneficiaries succeeding to the property of an estate or trust on termination are allowed to claim the benefits of any unused net operating loss carryover as explained in ¶ 67 below. [67] Unused loss carry-overs and excess deductions on termination.- 

An unused capital loss carry-over or an unused net operating loss carry-over remaining on termination of an estate or trust is allowed to the beneficiaries succeeding to the property of the estate or trust. The carry-overs are the same in the hands of the beneficiaries as in the hands of the estate or trust, and are deductible from gross income in computing adjusted gross income. For this purpose, the last taxable year of the estate or trust (whether or not a short taxable year) and the first taxable year of a beneficiary to which a loss is carried are each considered to be a taxable year for the purpose of determining the five-year period to which a loss can be carried. 

Example (1): An estate distributes all of its assets to X, the sole remainderman, and terminates on December 31, 1957, when it has a capital loss carry-over of $10,000 from 1955. X, who reports on the calendar-year basis, has capital gains of $4,000 for 1957 and ordinary income of $10,000. X would offset his $4,000 capital gains and $1,000 of ordinary income against the carry-over. The balance of the carry-over may be used only in 1958 and 1959. 

Example (2): A trust distributes all of its assets to A, the sole remainderman, on December 31, 1956, when it has a net operating loss carry-over of $10,000 from 1955. A reports on the calendar-year basis. He will be allowed to carry over the loss only to 1957, 1958 and 1959 to the extent that it does not offset his income for 1956.

A net capital loss sustained by an estate or trust in its last taxable year is allowed to the beneficiaries for the taxable year in which or with which the estate or trust terminates, and for the next four taxable years. 

Rev. Rul. 56-222, 1956-1 CB 155. 

* Code Sec. 642(d); Reg. § 1.642(d)-1. 

* Code Sec. 642(h)(1); Reg. § 1.642(h)-1. 

¶66

Where an estate or trust sustains a net operating loss in the year of its termination, the estate or trust would of course have to first observe the carryback provisions of Code Sec. 172. Then, if the net operating loss is not absorbed by the income of the carryback years (the two years immediately preceding the year of termination), the net operating loss, or remaining portion thereof, would become an unused net operating loss carryover, which under the provisions of Code Sec. 642(h) (1) is allowable to the beneficiary. Where the estate or trust has no carryback years, being in the process of administration for but one taxable year, or where there is no income in the carryback years which could be used to offset the net operating loss, a net operating loss in the year of termination would, in effect, be available only to the beneficiary. 

See Example (3) at ¶ 67.01 below. A beneficiary cannot carryback any part of a net operating loss made available to him through the provisions of Code Sec. 642(h) (1), even if the estate or trust had no preceding taxable years eligible for a carry back.

When the last taxable year of an estate or trust is the last year to which a loss can be carried, to the extent it is not absorbed in that taxable year by the estate or trust, it becomes an "excess deduction" which is allowed to the beneficiaries to the extent explained in ¶ 67.01 below. [ 67.01] Excess deductions.-If the deductions (other than the personal exemption and charitable contributions) of an estate or trust for its last taxable year exceed gross income, the excess is allowed as a deduction to the beneficiaries for the taxable year of the beneficiaries in which or with which the estate or trust terminates. The deduction cannot be carried over. This deduction is allowable only from adjusted gross income, so that it cannot be taken if the standard deduction or optional tax table is used. In determining excess deductions, any item of income or deduction which is taken into account in computing the net operating loss or capital loss carryover for the last taxable year may not be taken into account in determining excess deductions. 

Example (3): A decedent died in January, 1957, leaving a will which provided for distribution of the estate to B. The period of administration terminated on December 31, 1957, at which time all of the property was distributed to B, who reports on the calendar-year basis. The estate had the following income and deductions: 

Business income 

Nonbusiness income 

Business expenses 

Administration expenses and corpus commissions.

Total deductions 

$ 3,000 2,500 $ 5,500 $ 5,000 9,800 $14,800

The estate has a net operating loss of $2,000 ($5,500 gross income reduced by $5,000 of business expenses and $2,500 of nonbusiness expenses, the deduction for nonbusiness expenses being limited to nonbusiness income). The beneficiary may deduct this net operating loss in 1957 and the next four years. The excess deductions are $7,300, this being the amount of administrative expenses and corpus commissions not taken into account in determining the net operating loss ($9,800 minus $2,500 which was offset against nonbusiness income in computing the net operating loss). The excess deductions will be allowed to B only for the calendar year 1957.

Code Sec. 642(h) (2); Reg. § 1.642(h)-2. 

¶ 67.01

[¶ 67.02] Allocation.-The carry-overs and excess deductions described above are allocated among the beneficiaries succeeding to the property of an estate or trust proportionately according to the share of each in the burden of the loss or deductions. A person who qualified as a beneficiary succeeding to the property of an estate or trust with respect to one amount and does not qualify with respect to another amount is a beneficiary succeeding to the property of the estate or trust as to the amount with respect to which he qualifies. 

Example (4): A decedent's will leaves $100,000 to A, and the residue of his estate equally to B and C. His estate is sufficient to pay only $90,000 to A, and nothing to B and C. There is an excess of deductions over gross income for the last taxable year of the estate or trust of $5,000, and a capital loss carry-over of $15,000. A is a beneficiary succeeding to the property of the estate to the extent of $10,000, and since the total of the excess of deductions and the loss carry-over is $20,000, A is entitled to the benefit of one-half of each item, and the remaining half is divided equally between B and C.¹º 

[68] Payments for widows' allowances can be deductible.-Payments to widows by an estate as dower or other statutory allowances are deductible by the estate, to the extent of distributable net income as explained at ¶ 73 and 74 below, only if they are payable out of and chargeable to income under the court order or decree making the allowance or under local law.¹¹ 

Where the deduction is allowed, a corresponding amount will be taxable to the widow. Although the Commissioner's regulations do not make this specific, this would follow under the law, since the beneficiary of an estate is required to include in gross income "amounts required to be distributed currently" and "other amounts" actually distributed (or required to be distributed), to the extent of the distributable net income of the estate, as explained at ¶ 76 and 78 below. [69] A deduction for alimony and separate maintenance payments.- 

In computing its net income, a trust or estate is entitled to a deduction for amounts in the nature of alimony and separate maintenance payments (including those paid under a private separation agreement) which are required to be included in the wife's income. Such deduction is limited to the extent that such amounts are paid, credited or to be distributed out of income of the estate or trust for its taxable year. But the wife is taxed on such payments whether made out of income or corpus.' 12 [70] The charitable contributions deduction. The law contains a special provision on the deduction of charitable contributions by an estate or trust. An estate or trust is allowed an unlimited deduction for any part of its gross income which pursuant to the terms of the will or deed of trust- (1) is, during the taxable year, paid or permanently set aside for the purposes and in the manner specified in Code Sec. 170(c) dealing with contributions generally, or (2) is used exclusively for religious, charitable, scientific, literary or educational purposes, or for the prevention of cruelty to children or animals, or for the establishment, acquisition, maintenance or operation of a public cemetery not operated for profit.¹³

The term "gross income" of an estate or trust for purposes of the charitable contributions deduction includes an amount received by an estate or trust 

10 Reg. $1.642(h)-4. 

11 Reg. 1.661(a)-2(e). 

12 Code Secs. 71 and 682. 

18 Code Sec. 642(c); Reg. § 1.642(c)-1. 

¶ 67.02

which is includible in its gross income as "income in respect of a decedent' under Code Sec. 691 (a) (1).14 Accordingly, an estate or trust will be entitled to a charitable contribution deduction for "income in respect of a decedent" which is paid, permanently set aside, or used exclusively for charitable purposes as outlined above. 

The Internal Revenue Service has ruled that where, under applicable state law, real property is subject to administration, the income derived therefrom is includible in the gross income of the estate for the period that the estate is under administration; and if, under the terms of the decedent's will the net income from such property is to be used exclusively for a charitable purpose, it is deductible by the estate under the provisions of Code Sec. 642(c).15 

The deduction described above is allowed in lieu of, and not in addition to, the deduction for charitable contributions allowed individuals. 

Contributions which are discretionary with the trustee are includible in the deduction.16

This unlimited deduction, however, does not apply to a trust which is required to distribute all of its income currently, since the definition of such a trust excludes one which is permitted to pay, permanently set aside, or use any amounts for charitable purposes.17 [¶ 70.01] Deduction limited to sums paid out of "gross income.”—Although an estate's or trust's deduction for charitable contributions is stated above as being "unlimited," the deduction is in reality only unlimited to the extent it consists of items includible in the estate's or trust's gross income. In other words, if the contribution consists of items of income which do not enter into the gross income of the estate or trust for tax purposes, such as tax-exempt interest or foreign income of a foreign trust, only that portion of the contribution which represents items of gross income is deductible. 

In determining whether charitable contributions include items of trust income not included in gross income, the governing instrument will control if it specifically provides for the source of payment. In the absence of a specific provision, charitable contributions are deemed to consist of the same proportion of each class of the items of income of the estate or trust as the total of each class bears to the total of all classes. However, no part of the $50 dividends excluded from gross income under Code Sec. 116 is deemed included in the charitable contribution.18

Example (1): R transfers property to a trust. $3,000 per year is to be paid out of income to the D Church, the remainder of the income to be accumulated for R's son. During the taxable year the income was $10,000, consisting of $4,000 derived from rent and $6,000 derived from exempt interest on municipal bonds. 

The trustee during that year pays the contribution of $3,000 to the D Church. Of the $3,000, only the portion allocable to the trust's gross income is deductible. That portion is $4,000/$10,000, or $1,200. 

An adjustment must also be made if a charitable contribution includes long-term capital gains. The adjustment is necessary in order to prevent a double deduction, since the law allows a deduction of 50% of the excess of net long-term capital gains over net short-term capital losses in computing taxable income. The adjustment is computed by first determining what portion of the

contribution consists of long-term capital gains. This portion would equal the same proportion of the contribution which the long-term capital gains included in income bear to the total income of the estate or trust. The amount so determined is then reduced by 50%. The resulting amount is then subtracted from the total contribution to arrive at the allowable deduction.19 

Example (2): Under the terms of the trust instrument, the income of the trust is currently distributable to A during his life and capital gains are allocable to corpus.

No provision is made in the trust instrument for the invasion of corpus for the benefit of A. Upon A's death the corpus of the trust is to be distributed to O University, an organization exempt from taxation. During the taxable year 1957, the trust has long-term capital gains of $100,000 which, being allocable to corpus, are permanently set aside for charitable purposes. The trust must include $100,000 in gross income but is allowed a deduction of $50,000 under Code Sec. 1202 with respect to such gains and a charitable contributions deduction of $50,000 under Code Sec. 642(c) ($100,000 permanently set aside for charitable purposes less $50,000 allowed as a deduction with respect to such $100,000). 

Example (3): In 1957, a trust has ordinary income of $10,000, a long-term capital gain of $5,000, and no other capital gains or losses. A charitable contribution of $9,000 is made. The amount deductible for the contribution is determined as follows: The portion of the contribution consisting of long-term capital gains is $5,000/$15,000 of $9,000 or $3,000. 50% of $3,000 is $1,500. $9,000 minus $1,500 equals $7,500. Accordingly, the amount deductible as a charitable contribution in this example is $7,500. The effect is to limit the deduction to the same proportion of taxable income as it was of actual income. [ 70.02] Income "permanently set aside" for charity.-Normally, when a trust instrument provides for life beneficiaries with a remainder over to charity, capital gains on the sale of trust principal are exempt from tax as income "permanently set aside" for donation to charity. If under the will, however, the trustee may invade the principal to provide for "comfort, support, maintenance and/or happiness" of the life beneficiary, the latitude of the trustee's discretion is regarded as so broad as to make the amount of principal payable over to a charitable remainder uncertain and, therefore, not "permanently set aside." 20 The same result was reached by the Internal Revenue Service where the payment of legacies to charity were contingent upon the sale of corpus.¹ 

But the right of the trustee to invade principal, in his sole discretion, to provide for "proper support and maintenance" of the testator's wife was not fatal to the exemption of capital gains as income "permanently set aside" for charity, where there was little likelihood of the necessity for invading corpus.2 

The provision as to income "permanently set aside" does not make the deduction depend upon the action of the executors in crediting the income upon their books. It is the permanent setting aside of the income by the will itself that is important. [¶ 70.03] Discretion to invade corpus.—Where the entire income is to be paid to the life beneficiary, the question often raised is whether the authorization to the trustee to invade the corpus, in the event the ordinary income is insufficient to meet minimum payments, prevents capital gains from being "permanently set aside" for charities or the other purposes contemplated by Sec. 642(c). The principle that the mere existence of the power to invade requires a denial of a deduction has not been judicially accepted. That section has been construed with the view to encourage such contributions. 

G. C. M. 26031, 1949-2 CB 59. 

'Isaacson v. Clauson, (DC) 51-1 USTC ¶ 9161, 

95 Fed. Supp. 482. 

19 Reg. § 1.642(c)-3. 

20 Merchants Nat'l Bank of Boston, (S. Ct.) 43-2 USTC 9630, 320 U. S. 256. ¶ 70.02

The divergence of opinions in the cases comes from the attempt, on the one hand, to prophesy future income and, on the other, to arrive at probabilities of value by a fixed standard in all cases. To be allowed as a deduction, a "standard fixed in fact and capable of being stated in definite terms of money" must be found somewhere in the case. [¶ 70.04] Cases where deduction by trust can be limited. In the case of a trust, the unlimited charitable deduction otherwise allowable under Code Sec. 642(c) is, in general, subject to the percentage limitations applicable to individuals (that is, 20% of taxable income plus 10% for amounts paid to a church, an educational organization, a hospital, or medical research organization) under the following circumstances: (1) To the extent that the deduction is allocable to "unrelated business income"; (2) If the trust has engaged in a "prohibited transaction"; (3) If income is accumulated for a charitable purpose and the accumulation is (a) unreasonable, (b) substantially diverted to a noncharitable purpose, or (c) invested against the interests of the charitable beneficiaries.³

If the circumstances set forth in (1) and (3) above are applicable, the deduction is limited to income actually paid out for charitable purposes, and is not allowed for income only set aside or to be used for those purposes. "Unrelated business income" for the purposes of (1) above is defined as the same income which, if the trust were exempt as a charitable, religious, etc., trust under Code Sec. 501, would be its unrelated business taxable income under Code Sec. 512 (relating to income derived from certain business activities and from certain leases). Unless the facts clearly indicate the contrary, the deduction otherwise allowable under Code Sec. 642 (c) is allocable to the unrelated business income on the basis of the ratio which the unrelated business income bears to the taxable income of the trust computed without any deduction under Code Sec. 642 (c).a 

Example (4): The X trust has income of $50,000. There is included in this amount a net profit of $31,000 from the operation of a trade or business. The trustee is required to pay half of the trust income to A, an individual, and the balance of the trust income to the Y charity, an organization which is not a church, college, or hospital. The trustee pays each beneficiary $25,000. Under these facts, the unrelated business income of the trust (computed before the charitable contributions deduction which would be allowed under Code Sec. 

512(b)(11)) is $30,000 ($31,000 less the deduction of $1,000 allowed by Code Sec. 

512(b)(12)). The deduction otherwise allowable under Code Sec. 642(c) is $25,000, the amount paid to the Y charity. The portion allocable to the unrelated business income is $15,000, that is, an amount which bears the same ratio to $25,000 as $30,000 bears to $50,000. The portion allocable to the unrelated business income, and therefore disallowed as a deduction, is $15,000 reduced by $6,000 (the charitable contributions deduction which would be allowable under Code Sec. 

512(b)(11)), or $9,000. [¶ 70.05] Information returns.-Trusts claiming charitable contributions deductions under Code Sec. 642(c) are required to file an information return on Form 1041-A, in addition to Form 1041.4

¶ 70.05

[171] The distinction between simple and complex trusts.—In determining the deduction for distributions to beneficiaries, different rules may apply depending upon whether the trust is a "simple trust" or a "complex trust." A "simple trust" is one which is required to distribute all of its income currently, whether or not the trustee actually does so, and has no provision in the trust instrument for charitable contributions.5

A trust is not a simple trust if the terms of the trust instrument require that none of the income be distributed until after the year of its receipt by the trust. However, if the fiduciary is under a duty to distribute the income currently, but as a matter of practical necessity, the income is not distributed until after the close of the trust's taxable year, treatment as a simple trust will still be proper.

Example (1): Under the terms of a trust instrument, all of the income is currently distributable to A. The trust reports on the calendar year basis and as a matter of practical necessity makes distribution to A of each quarter's income on the fifteenth day of the month following the close of the quarter. The distribution made by the trust on January 15, 1958, of the income for the fourth quarter of 1957 does not disqualify the trust for treatment as a simple trust. 

In the year in which a trust distributes corpus, it loses its classification as a simple trust. Thus, a trust can never be a simple trust in the year of its termination or in a year of partial liquidation."

A trust, which otherwise qualifies as a simple trust, which may make a distribution of corpus in the discretion of the trustee, or which is required under the trust instrument to make a distribution of corpus upon the happening of a specified event, will be disqualified as a simple trust only for the taxable year in which an actual distribution of corpus is made. 

Example (2): Under the terms of a trust, which is required to distribute all of its income currently, half of the corpus is to be distributed to beneficiary A when he becomes 30 years old. The trust reports on the calendar year basis. 

On December 28, 1957, A becomes 30 years old and the trustee distributes half of the trust corpus to him on January 3, 1958. The trust will be disqualified for treatment as a simple trust only for the taxable year 1958, the year in which an actual distribution of corpus is made.

If a trust's income for one taxable year is required or permitted to be accumulated, and in another taxable year its income for the year is required to be distributed currently (and no other amounts are distributed), the trust is a simple trust for the latter year.8

Example (3): The terms of a trust provide that income may be accumulated until a beneficiary is 21 years old, and thereafter must be distributed currently. 

The trust is a simple trust for taxable years beginning after the beneficiary reaches the age of 21 years, assuming that no accumulated income or other "non-current" amounts are distributed.

In determining whether all the income is required to be distributed currently, it is not necessary that the amount of income for each beneficiary be specified in the instrument.

Example (4): A fiduciary is required to distribute all of the income of a trust currently, but has discretion to sprinkle the income among a class of bene- 

Reg. § 1.651(a)-1. 

• Reg. § 1.651(a)-2. 

Reg. § 1.651(a)-3. 

8 Reg. § 1.651(a)-2(c). 

Reg. § 1.651(a)-2(b). 

¶ 71

ficiaries, or among named beneficiaries in such amounts as he may see fit. All the income is required to be distributed currently, even though the amount distributable to a particular beneficiary is unknown until the fiduciary has exercised his discretion.

A trust may qualify as a simple trust, although the trust instrument provides for a charitable remainder, if either (1) the remainder is subject to a contingency so that no deduction would be allowed for capital gains or other amounts added to corpus as amounts set aside for a charitable purpose, or (2) the trust receives no capital gains or other income which could be added to corpus for the taxable year and for which a charitable deduction would have been allowed.10

The term "complex trust" applies to all trusts other than those described above. A decedent's estate is treated for income tax purposes in the same manner as a complex trust.¹¹ For both simple and complex trusts, the deduction for distributions to beneficiaries is determined by reference to "distributable net income" which is defined and discussed at ¶ 72 below. [172] How do you determine "distributable net income"?-Before the amount of the deduction for distributions to beneficiaries may be determined, it is necessary to compute what is termed the "distributable net income" of the estate or trust. This is so because "distributable net income" limits the amount deductible by the estate or trust for distributions and the amount of the distributions taxable to the beneficiaries. It is also used to determine the character of the distributions (dividends income, interest income, etc.) to the beneficiaries.

According to the regulations (which seem to be more liberal in some respects than a strict reading of the law would require), distributable net income for any taxable year is the taxable income of the estate or trust, with the following modifications: 12 (1) The deduction for distributions to beneficiaries is disallowed. 

(2) The deduction for personal exemption is disallowed. 

(3) Capital gains are ordinarily excluded unless they are (a) allocated to income under the terms of the governing instrument or local law, (b) allocated to corpus, but actually distributed as capital gains to beneficiaries during the taxable year, or (c) used in determining the amount which is distributed or required to be distributed. However, if capital gains are paid, permanently set aside, or to be used for charitable purposes, so that a charitable contribution deduction is allowed in respect of the capital gains, they must be included in the computation of distributable net income. 

Example (1): A trust is created to pay the income to A for life, with a discretionary power in the trustee to invade principal for A's benefit. In the taxable year, $10,000 is realized from the sale of securities at a profit, and $10,000 in excess of income (note that this corresponds to the $10,000 of capital gain, but legally is identifiable only as $10,000 of corpus) is distributed to A. The capital gain is not allocated to A by the trustee. During the taxable year the trustee received and paid out $5,000 of dividends. No other cash was received or on hand during the taxable year. The capital gain will not ordinarily be included in distributable net income. However, if the trustee follows a regular practice of distributing the exact net proceeds of the sale of trust property, capital gains will be included in distributable net income. 

10 Reg. § 1.651(a)-4. 

12 Code Sec. 643; Reg. §§ 1.643-1—1.643(c)-2. 

11 Reg. § 1.661(a)-1. 

1 72

(4) Capital losses are to be excluded except to the extent that they enter into the determination of any capital gains that are paid, credited, or required to be distributed to any beneficiary during the taxable year. But see ¶67 as to capital loss carry-overs in the year of termination of an estate or trust. (5) The 50% deduction for long-term capital gains under Code Sec. 1202 is usually disallowed. But it is to be taken into account in computing distributable net income to the extent that it is allocable to capital gains which are paid, permanently set aside, or to be used for charitable purposes. (6) In the case of a simple trust only, there are excluded extraordinary dividends (whether paid in cash or in kind) or taxable stock dividends which are not distributed or credited to a beneficiary because the fiduciary in good faith determines that under the terms of the governing instrument and local law such dividends are allocable to corpus. Taxable dividends of this sort would be included in distributable net income of a complex trust. (7) Tax-exempt interest and foreign income of a foreign trust, reduced by nondeductible expenses attributable to such amounts, are to be included. 

If the estate or trust is allowed a charitable contribution deduction under Code Sec. 642(c), the items of exempt income included in the computation of distributable net income must be reduced to the extent that the amount of income which is paid, permanently set aside, or to be used for charitable purposes consists of items of exempt income. If the governing instrument specifically provides for the source out of which amounts are paid, permanently set aside, or to be used for such charitable purposes, the specific provisions will control. 

Otherwise, the contribution will be deemed to consist of the same proportion of the tax-exempt interest or foreign income as the interest or foreign income bears to the total of all income. 

Example (2): Assume that a trust has income of $130,000, including $20,000 of tax-exempt interest. Assume also that the trustee contributed $27,950 to charity, there being no designation of the source of the payment. In such case, the charitable contribution would reduce distributable net income. It would also reduce the tax-exempt interest includible in distributable net income to the extent that such interest is allocable to the charitable contribution. The amount of taxexempt interest includible in the charitable contribution is $20,000/130,000 of $27,950, or $4,300. Therefore, the amount of tax-exempt interest includible in distributable net income is $20,000 minus $4,300, or $15,700. The charitable contribution deduction is $27,950 minus $4,300, or $23,650. (8) The $50 dividends-received exclusion under Code Sec. 116 is to be added back to income; that is, in computing distributable net income the $50 dividend exclusion is not allowable.

The example below illustrates the computation of distributable net income as described in the above provisions.

The trust instrument was silent on depreciation, and no reserve was required or allowed under local law (so all depreciation is allocable to the beneficiaries).

What is "income" of estates and trusts?—The term "income" as used for estates and trusts, when not preceded by the words "taxable," "distributable net," "undistributed net," or "gross" means the income of the estate or trust as determined under its governing instrument and the applicable local law. 

In other words, "income" usually means the amounts for which a fiduciary would be required to account in rendering an accounting to the court having jurisdiction of the estate or trust.13 Items of gross income constituting extraordinary dividends or taxable stock dividends which the fiduciary, acting in good faith, allocates to corpus are not considered income.14 [73] What is a simple trust's deduction for distributions to beneficiaries? A simple trust may deduct the amount of income which is required under the terms of the trust instrument to be distributed currently. If the amount of income required to be distributed currently exceeds the distributa-

ble net income, the deduction is limited to the amount of the distributable net income. For this purpose, distributable net income is to be computed without regard to items of trust income (and deductions allocable thereto) which are not included in the gross income of the trust. Therefore, the modifications specified in items (7) and (8) at ¶ 72 above (tax-exempt interest, foreign income of a foreign trust, and excluded dividends) are not to be taken into consideration in computing distributable net income.15

$25,000 50,000 $25,000 975 24,025 $99,025 7,925 $91,100 $24,025 50 24,075 $67,025 [74] What is an estate's or complex trust's deduction for distributions to beneficiaries?-Estates and complex trusts are allowed to deduct (1) the amount of income for the taxable year which is required to be distributed currently, and (2) any "other" amounts properly paid or credited or required to be distributed for the taxable year.

The deduction is limited to distributable net income.16 A further limitation, as explained below, may also apply.

Income which is required to be distributed currently includes any amount required to be distributed which may be paid out of income or corpus, such as an annuity, to the extent that it is paid out of income for the taxable year. "Other" amounts for which a deduction is allowed (item (2) above) include all amounts properly paid, credited or required to be distributed during the taxable year other than income required to be distributed currently. The term includes, (a) amounts distributed in the discretion of the fiduciary, (b) payment of an annuity to the extent that it is not paid out of income for the taxable year, and (c) a distribution of property in kind.17

Widows' allowances to the extent payable out of and chargeable to income under a court order or decree or local law requiring the payments qualify as "income required to be distributed currently" or as an "other" amount depending upon how the fiduciary pays the allowance. If there is a standing court order requiring monthly payments of a widow's allowance it would probably qualify as an "amount required to be distributed currently." If the 

15 Code Sec. 651; Reg. § 1.651(b)-1. 

16 Code Sec. 661(a). 

17 Reg. § 1.661(a)-2. 

174

fiduciary every so often secures a court order for payment of a widow's allowance, it would probably qualify as an "other" amount. See also ¶ 68. The above rule also applies to amounts used to satisfy any person's legal obligation (but not alimony payments), such as, a parent's legal obligation to support his minor child. [¶ 74.01] Character of amounts distributed.-The deduction for distributions is to be treated as consisting of the same proportion of each class of items entering into the computation of distributable net income as the total of each bears to the total distributable net income. However, if the trust instrument or local law requires a different method of allocation, the items will be allocated accordingly.18 Thus, if 4 of the distributable net income consists of tax-exempt interest, then 4 of the beneficiary's distribution will constitute tax-exempt interest, in the absence of a contrary provision. And no deduction will be allowable to the estate or trust for that part of a beneficiary's distribution which consists of exempt income. 

In determining the items which make up the distributions deduction, all deductions entering into the computation of distributable net income (including charitable contributions) are to be allocated among the different types of income which make up such distributable net income according to the principles set forth in (1) to (3) below. Where a charitable contribution is involved, however, the amounts paid, permanently set aside, or to be used for charitable purposes, are first ratably apportioned among each class of items of income entering into the computation of the distributable net income as explained in ¶ 70.19 (1) Deductions directly attributable to one class of income (except excludable dividends) are allocated thereto. For instance, repairs to, taxes on, and other expenses directly attributable to the maintenance of rental property or the collection of rental income are allocated to rental income. Similarly, all expenses directly attributable to a business are allocated to the income from such business. (2) Deductions which are not directly attributable to a specific class of income may be allocated to any item of income (including capital gains) included in computing distributable net income. But a portion of such deductions must be allocated to nontaxable income, except the dividends exclusion. For example, if trust income is $30,000 (after direct expenses), consisting equally of $10,000 of dividends, tax-exempt interest, and rents, and income commissions amount to $3,000, one-third ($1,000) of such commissions should be allocated to tax-exempt interest, but the balance of $2,000 may be allocated to the rents or dividends in such proportions as the trustee may elect.

The fact that the governing instrument or local law treats certain deductions as attributable to corpus or income will not affect this allocation. Examples of expenses which are considered as not directly attributable to a specific class of income are trustee's commissions, the rental of safe deposit boxes, and State income and personal property taxes. (3) To the extent that any deductions which are directly attributable to a class of income exceed that class of income, they may be allocated to any other class of income (including capital gains) in the manner provided in (2) above. But the amount allocated to any one item of income 19 Reg. 88 1.661(b)-1, 1.661(b)-2. 

18 Code Sec. 661(b); Reg: § 1.661(b)-1. 

¶ 74.01

may not exceed the amount of that item of income. If there is an excess, the excess must be reallocated to another item of income. However, any excess deduction attributable to tax-exempt income (other than the dividend exclusion) may not be offset against any other class of income. 

Thus, if the trust has rents, taxable interest, dividends, and tax-exempt interest, and the deductions directly attributable to the rents exceed the rental income, the excess may be allocated to the taxable interest or dividends in such proportions as the fiduciary may elect. However, if the excess deductions are attributable to the tax-exempt interest, they may not be allocated to either the rents, taxable interest, or dividends.20 [¶ 74.02] Limitation of distribution deduction.-As stated previously, the distribution deduction may not exceed the distributable net income of the estate or trust. Also, the distribution deduction may not consist of any amount which is treated as consisting of an item of distributable net income which is not included in the gross income of the estate or trust.¹ 

Example (1): A trust has distributable net income of $20,000, which is deemed to consist of $10,000 of dividends and $10,000 of tax-exempt interest. It distributes $10,000 to beneficiary A. The deduction, without the special limitation noted above, would be $10,000, consisting of $5,000 of dividends and $5,000 of tax-exempt interest. The deduction actually allowable, because of the special limitation, is $4,975, since no deduction is allowable for the $5,000 of tax-exempt interest and the $25 deemed distributed out of the $50 of dividends excluded under Code Sec. 116, they being items of distributable net income which are not included in the gross income of the estate or trust. In computing the taxable income of the trust no deduction is allowable for the portions of the charitable contributions deduction ($2,000) and trustee's commissions ($1,000) which are treated as attributable to the tax-exempt interest excludable from gross income. Also, of the dividends of $4,000 deemed to have been distributed to A, $25 (25/50ths of $50) is deemed to have been distributed from the excluded dividends and is not an allowable deduction to the trust. Accordingly, the distribution deduction allowable is deemed to be composed of $3,500 rental income, $3,975 of dividends, and $4,000 partially tax-exempt interest. 

No deduction is allowable for the portion of tax-exempt interest or for the portion of the excluded dividends deemed to have been distributed to the beneficiary. (f) The trust is entitled to the credit allowed by Code Sec. 34 with respect to dividends of $5,975 ($9,950 less $3,975 distributed to A) included in gross income. Also, the trust is allowed the credit provided by Code Sec. 35 with respect to partially tax-exempt interest of $6,000 ($10,000 less $4,000 deemed distributed to A) included in gross income. (g) Dividends of $4,000 allocable to A are to be aggregated with his other dividends (if any) for purposes of the dividend exclusion under Code Sec. 116 and the dividend received credit under Code Sec. 34 on his own individual return. [175] There is still a limited option to apply the 65-day rule.-Under the 

1954 Code, distributable net income is the measure for determining the distribution deduction by the estate or trust and taxability of the distribution to the beneficiary. This approach eliminates the necessity for tracing distributions to the income of the estate or trust for the current taxable year and, therefore, for applying the 65-day rule which was provided in the 1939 Code. 

Under the former 65-day rule, if, within, the first 65 days of the taxable year of the estate or trust, income for a period commencing before the beginning of such taxable year became payable, such income, not to exceed the income of the 12 months preceding such taxable year, was considered paid, credited or to be distributed on the last day of the preceding taxable year. 

If income for any period became payable after the first 65 days of the taxable year of an estate or trust, such income, not to exceed the income of the last 12 months of such period, was considered paid, credited or distributable within such taxable year.

A limited use of the 65-day rule is retained under the 1954 Code. The fiduciary of a trust may elect to treat distributions within the first 65 days of the taxable year of the trust as amounts which were paid or credited on the last day of the preceding taxable year. The election is available only if the trust was created prior to January 1, 1954, and if the terms of the trust in- ¶ 75

strument provide that the trust may not distribute in any taxable year amounts in excess of the trust income for the immediately preceding taxable year. The election had to be made not later than the time for filing the return for the first taxable year of the trust which began after 1953 and ended after August 16, 1954 (date of approval of the 1954 Code). Once the election was made it applies to all subsequent taxable years.2 

Application of the 65-day rule was to some extent mandatory for the first taxable year of an estate or a trust to which the 1954 Code applied. If an amount paid, credited or distributed during the estate's or trust's first 1954 Code taxable year would, under the 1939 Code, have been thrown back to the last day of its preceding year under the 65-day rule, that was also to happen under the 1954 Code.³

(b)-2.

PART IV-BENEFICIARY'S TAX LIABILITY How Is the Beneficiary of a Simple Trust Taxed? 

[¶[76] What amounts are included in beneficiary's gross income?—The beneficiaries of a simple trust (see the definition at ¶ 71) are to include in their gross income for the taxable year the amounts of income required to be distributed currently to them for such year, whether or not distributed. If the amount of the income required to be distributed currently to beneficiaries exceeds the distributable net income of the trust, each beneficiary includes in his gross income an amount equal to his proportionate share of the distributable net income.¹ Each item of income of the trust has the same character in the hands of the beneficiary that it had in the hands of the fiduciary. In other words, dividends distributed to the beneficiary are dividends in his hands for purposes of determining his dividend exclusion and dividend received credit on his individual Form 1040. Tax-exempt interest distributed would also be taxexempt in the hands of the beneficiary. Capital gains distributed would be capital gains to the beneficiary. This is commonly known as the "conduit" rule. See ¶ 34.

The deductions of a trust that enter into the computation of distributable net income, which as indicated above limits the income taxable to the beneficiaries, are to be allocated among the items of income in accordance with the rules set forth in ¶74. The example below illustrates the allocation of such expenses and the effect on the amount and character of income taxable to the beneficiaries.

Example (1): A simple trust is required to distribute all income currently to A and B in equal amounts. During the taxable year it had the following income: Rents, $25,000; dividends, $50,000; tax-exempt interest, $25,000. Rental expenses were $5,000, depreciation was $5,000 (no provisión in the trust instrument having been made for depreciation), and trustee's commissions were $3,900. 

The allocation of expenses and the character of the income received by the beneficiaries are as follows, assuming that the trustee allocates to rents all expenses not directly attributable to any other item of income and not allocable to tax-exempt interest: 

Tax-exempt interest

Trustee's commissions allocable to tax-exempt interest (25,000/100,000 of $3,900) $25,000 975 $24,025

Each beneficiary is therefore deemed to have received $12,012.50 of tax-exempt interest. Rents

Expenses allocable thereto: 

Rental expenses 

Trustee's commissions ($3,900 minus $395 allocable to tax-exempt interest) $25,000 $5,000 

2,925 7,925 $17,075

Each beneficiary is therefore deemed to have received $8,537.50 of rents. 

No expenses were allocated to the $50,000 of dividends, and each beneficiary is therefore deemed to have received $25,000 of dividends. Also, each beneficiary

93 is allowed a deduction of $2,500 for depreciation of rental property attributable to the one-half of rental income distributed to him. 

If the income of the trust which is required to be distributed currently exceeds the distributable net income, each beneficiary is considered to have received only his proportionate share of the distributable net income. The amount taxable to each beneficiary in such case is the same fractional part of distributable net income as the amount of trust income required to be distributed currently to such beneficiary bears to the trust income required to be distributed to all beneficiaries. Only a proportionate part of each particular item of gross income (and its related deduction) is allocated to each beneficiary. 

That is, a beneficiary is considered to have received the same proportion of each class of income, such as dividends, rent, interest, etc.; as the total of each class bears to the total distributable net income, unless the trust instrument specifically allocates different classes of income to different beneficiaries." 

Example (2): Under a trust instrument, beneficiary A is to receive 40% of the trust income and beneficiary B is to receive 60%. The trust instrument provides that in no event shall A's annuity be less than $4,000, or B's annuity less than $6,000. During the taxable year, the distributable net income of the trust is only $3,000, all from dividends. There are no deductions to be allocated. A will be considered to have received $4,000/$10,000 × $3,000, or $1,200; and B will be considered to have received $6,000/$10,000 x $3,000, or $1,800. These amounts will retain their character as dividend income in the hands of the beneficiaries. 

If the trust instrument specifically allocates different classes of income to different beneficiaries, or if local law requires such an allocation, each beneficiary is deemed to have received the items of income specifically allocated to him. A trust instrument will be considered specifically to allocate different classes of income to different beneficiaries only to the extent that the specific allocation has an economic effect independent of the income tax consequences of the allocation. The Commissioner's regulations exemplify this latter statement with the following examples:

Example (3): Allocation pursuant to a provision in a trust instrument grant ing the trustee discretion to allocate different classes of income to different beneficiaries is not a specific allocation by the terms of the trust. 

Example (4): Allocation pursuant to a provision directing the trustee to pay half the income to A, or $10,000 out of the income to A, and the balance of the income to B, but directing the trustee first to allocate a specific class of income to A's share (to the extent there is income of that class and to the extent it does not exceed A's share) is not a specific allocation by the terms of the trust. 

Example (5): Allocation pursuant to a provision directing the trustee to pay all of one class of income (whatever it may be) to A, and the balance of the income to B, is a specific allocation by the terms of the trust. [177] Do different taxable years of beneficiary and trust affect tax?

In the case of a trust required to distribute all of its income currently, if the trust and the beneficiary have different taxable years, the amount includible in the gross income of the beneficiary is based on the income of the trust for the taxable year or years ending with or within his taxable year. This rule applies to short taxable years of the trust as well as to taxable years of normal duration. The income of the trust is determined in accordance with its accounting method and without regard to that of the beneficiary.⁹ 

↑ Code Sec. 652(a); Reg. § 1.652(a)-2. 

Reg. 1.652(b)-2. 

• Code Sec. 652(c); Reg. § 1.652(c)-1. 

¶ 77

Example: A trust has a taxable year ending January 31. Its income is to be distributed currently to B, who makes his returns on a calendar year basis. 

The income of the trust for its year ended January 31, 1957, is $9,000. Therefore, B's return for 1957 will include $9,000 as income from the trust regardless of the income of the trust for that calendar year. 

Where a beneficiary dies the above rule does not apply. The gross income for the last taxable year of a beneficiary on the cash basis includes only income actually distributed to him before his death. Income required to be distributed, but in fact distributed to his estate, is included in the gross income of the estate as "income in respect of a decedent" under Code Sec. 691.10 

If the existence of a beneficiary which is not an individual terminates, the amount to be included in its gross income for its last taxable year is computed as if the beneficiary were a deceased individual, except that income required to be distributed prior to the termination but actually distributed to the beneficiary's successor in interest is included in the beneficiary's income for its last taxable year.¹¹ 11 

? 

[78] Income taxed on a "tier" basis.-A beneficiary of an estate or complex trust is taxed on (1) the income of the estate or trust required to be distributed to him currently, whether or not actually distributed, and (2) all "other" amounts properly paid, credited or required to be distributed to him for the taxable year." 12 The phrase "income required to be distributed currently," includes any amount required to be paid out of income or corpus, such as an annuity, to the extent that there is income not paid, credited or required to be distributed to other beneficiaries for the taxable year. What may constitute "other" amounts was previously discussed in ¶74. In general, an "other" amount would include current income distributable in the discretion of the fiduciary, payments out of corpus, and distributions out of accumulated income. 

The classification of distributions into groups (1) and (2) provides a "tier" system of taxing beneficiaries.

The maximum amount which can be taxed to a beneficiary is, as in the case of a simple trust, limited by the distributable net income of the estate or trust.

If the amount of income required to be distributed currently, that is, the income in tier (1), is less than the distributable net income of the estate or trust (computed without the deduction for charitable contributions), the entire amount of such income is taxable to the beneficiaries. However, if the amount of income required to be distributed currently to all beneficiaries exceeds the distributable net income (computed without the deduction for charitable contributions), then there is included in the gross income of each beneficiary an amount which bears the same ratio to distributable net income (as so computed) as the amount of income required to be distributed currently to the beneficiary bears to the amount required to be distributed currently to all beneficiaries.18 1.652(c)-2. 

10 Reg. 

11 Reg. 1.652(c)-3. 

12 Code Sec. 662(a); Reg. § 1.662(a)-1. 

18 Reg. 1.662(a)-2. 178

Example (1): A trust instrument requires all of the net income to be distributed, 40% to A and 60% to B. Net income for the year is $15,000; distributable net income is $10,000. A's share of the net income is $6,000; his proportionate share of the distributable net income is $4,000 (6,000/15,000 of $10,000). He is therefore taxable on $4,000 of the distribution. In similar fashion, B's share of the net income is $9,000; his proportionate share of the distributable net income is $6,000. He is therefore taxable on $6,000 of the distribution. If distributable net income had been $15,000, both A and B would be taxable on their full shares of the income required to be distributed currently. 

Distributions which fall into tier (2), that is, amounts "other" than income which is required to be distributed currently, are taxed to the beneficiaries only if the distributable net income exceeds distributions which fall into tier (1). Distributions in tier (2) are taxable to a beneficiary to the extent that the distributable net income exceeds the amounts in tier (1), that is, amounts required to be distributed currently. If more than one beneficiary is involved, each beneficiary is taxed on his proportionate share of such excess. 

His proportionate share will be an amount which bears the same ratio to the excess of distributable net income over the amount required to be distributed currently as the "other" amounts distributed to him bear to the "other" amounts distributed to all beneficiaries.¹4 

Example (2): A trust instrument requires payments of $5,000 annually to X, payable only out of net income, and $3,000 per year to Y, payable out of income or corpus. The trust has $5,000 of net income for the taxable year, all of which is distributed to A. The trust also has $5,000 of distributable net income. The $3,000 payment to Y is made out of corpus. X is taxable on the full amount of his distribution. There is no tax on the distribution to Y because the distributable net income after reduction by the amount required to be distributed currently is zero.

Example (3): The terms of a trust require the distribution annually of $10,000 of income to A. If any income remains, it may be accumulated or distributed to B, C, and D in amounts in the trustee's discretion. He may also invade corpus for the benefit of A, B, C, or D. In the taxable year, the trust has $20,000 of income after the deduction of all expenses. Distributable net income is $20,000. The trustee distributes $10,000 of income to A. Of the remaining $10,000 of income, he distributes $3,000 each to B, C, and D, and also distributes an additional $5,000 to A. A includes $10,000 in income. The "other amounts distributed" amount to $14,000, includible in the income of the recipients to the extent of $10,000, distributable net income less the income currently distributable to A. A will include an additional $3,571 (5,000/14,000 × $10,000) in income, and 

B, C, and D will each include $2,143 (3,000/14,000 × $10,000). 

The amounts taxable to beneficiaries of estates and complex trusts retain the same character (rents, dividends, etc.) as they had in the hands of the estate or trust. In allocating the various types of income to the beneficiaries, the amount of a particular item reflected in distributable net income is first charged with directly related expenses and a proportionate share of other expenses, including the unlimited charitable contribution deduction to the extent it is chargeable to income of the current year. See the explanation of these allocation rules at ¶ 74.01. Then each beneficiary's share of income is multiplied by fractions for each class of income, in which the numerator is the amount of such income includible in distributable net income, and the denominator is the distributable net income. However, if the governing instrument 

14 Reg. § 1.662(a)-3. 

¶ 78

specifies or local law requires a different allocation, such allocation is to be followed.15
