Special rules. The law contains special exclusions from the rules for taxing beneficiaries of estate and complex trusts as explained above. See ¶ 81 et seq. Also, distributions from accumulated income are subject to the 5-year throwback rule explained at ¶ 86-88. [¶ 78.01] "Interest" on deferred legacies may be exempt to beneficiaries. -Under the 1939 Code, so-called "interest" on a specific pecuniary legacy which is required by State law to be paid where administration of an estate is protracted, has been held by the Commissioner, the Tax Court and the Third Circuit to be taxable to the legatees under 1939 Code Sec. 22(a) as interest received, and deductible by the estate as interest paid, whether the estate paid it out of income or corpus.15 However, in the case of Howard C. Davidson v. 

U. S.15b the Court of Claims held that "interest" on deferred legacies, which was paid in the year the estate terminated, was to be treated under 1939 Code Sec. 162(c) as a discretionary distribution of estate income. As a result, the interest in the hands of the legatees was exempt from tax to the extent it exceeded the net income of the estate for the year of its termination. 

If the principle applied by the Court of Claims in the Davidson case were followed under the 1954 Code, the result could be even more beneficial to specific legatees than under the 1939 Code, because of the 1954 Code "two-tier” treatment of distributions to beneficiaries and the distributable net income concept. Under the language used in the Davidson case, interest on a legacy would fall within the category of a discretionary distribution and therefore an "other" amount distribution under the 1954 Code. Consequently, in many foreseeable circumstances, interest on a specific legacy would not be fully taxed to the recipient and, in the year of the estate's termination, would probably not be taxed at all.

Example (5): A decedent's will provides for the payment of a $10,000 legacy to A. No provision is made in the will for payment of interest on the legacy, but State law does require the payment of such interest. The will also provides that monthly payments, in the total amount of $5,000 each year, are to be made (out of income or corpus) to B during the period the estate is in the process of administration. On termination of the estate, its assets and accumulated income are to be distributed to B as residual legatee. The estate is in the process of administration during the years 1955 and 1956, and has accumulated income of $10,000 at the end of 1956. In 1957, both net income and distributable net income are $5,500. The estate in that year makes the regular current distribution of $5,000 to B and also pays the $10,000 legacy, plus $1,500 interest on the legacy, to A.

The $5,000 payment to B, since it is income required to be distributed currently, is taxable in full to B. The $1,500 interest payment to A, treating it as a distribution of an "other amount" under the Davidson principle, is taxable to A only to the extent of $500, the excess of distributable net income of $5,500 over the $5,000 required to be distributed currently. (If distributable net income had been $5,000, none of the $1,500 interest payment would have been taxable to A.) 

15 I. T. 1720, II-2 CB 54; Minnie L. Wolf, 32 15b 57-1 USTC ¶ 9462. 

BTA 959, CCH Dec. 9021, aff'd by CA-3, 36-2 

USTC 9328, 84 Fed. (2d) 390; W. W. Clarke, 3 TCM 758, CCH Dec. 14,061 (M). ¶ 78.01

NI Example (6): Assume the same facts as in Example (5) except that B is the decedent's widow and that the will did not provide for distribution of estate income or corpus during the period the estate was in administration. The court having jurisdiction of the estate, however, has ordered that regular payments, in the total amount of $5,000 each year, be made to B, out of income or corpus, as a widow's allowance. Under Reg. § 1.661(a)-2(b) and (e), the allowance would presumably be treated as an amount required to be distributed currently, to the extent payable out of and chargeable to the estate's income. Therefore, assuming the widow's allowance was paid out of estate income, the result would be the same as in Example (5)..

Example (7): Assume the same facts as in Example (5) except that B is the decedent's widow and that the will did not provide for distribution of estate income or corpus during the period the estate was in administration. The court having jurisdiction of the estate, however, makes an award to B from time to time, on motion of the executor, payable out of income or corpus. In 1957 there were two awards to the widow, one for $3,000 and one for $2,000, both paid out of estate income. In this situation, the regulations would apparently classify the awards as "other" amounts, on the same tax "tier" as the interest payment to A. Therefore, in this case, 5,000/6,500 of the $5,500 distributable net income would be taxed to B and 1,500/6,500 of the $5,500 would be taxed to A. 

Example (8): Assume the same facts as in Example (5) except that the estate is terminated in 1957 and that its net income (which is also its distributable net income) for 1957 is $3,000. On termination the estate pays A his $10,000 legacy plus $1,500 as interest on the legacy. It distributes the assets of the estate and all remaining income, accumulated and otherwise, to B. 

Since, on termination of an estate, the income for the year of termination becomes "currently distributable" to the residuary legatee,' 15c B would be taxable in full on $3,000 of the income distributed to him. The $3,000 consumes the estate's distributable net income and, therefore, no portion of the $1,500 interest payment to A (treating it as an "other amount" under the Davidson rule) would be taxable to him. Also, the accumulated income distributed to B, which would be treated as an "other amount" for 1954 Code purposes, would not be taxable to him. 

The result would be the same if B were the decedent's widow and, in the year the estate terminated, she received a widow's allowance or award as in Examples (6) and (7), whether the allowance or award is considered to be an amount required to be distributed currently or an "other amount" distribution.

As can be seen from the above examples, if the Davidson case is followed under the 1954 Code, interest on a legacy would probably not be taxed to the recipient if payment of the legacy is made in the year the estate is terminated. 

The net effect of this is that the residual legatee will bear the tax cost of the interest payment, as well as bearing the economic burden through a reduction in assets and income distributed to him on termination of the estate. To avoid this result it may be wise to make payment of the specific legacies and interest in the year or years prior to the year the estate is terminated. 

In this way, if no current distributions are made, the estate will get distribution deduction and the specific legatee will pay the tax on the interest distribution, both limited of course by the distributable net income concept. 

On the other hand, if the Commissioner's rule were followed under the 1954 Code, the interest on the legacy would be taxable in full to the specific legatee under Sec. 61(a) and would be deductible in full by the estate as interest expense, the same as under the 1939 Code. However, under 1954 

Code Sec. 642 (h), deductions in excess of gross income for the year of termina- 

15c H. K. Carlisle, 8 TC 563, CCH Dec. 15,675, aff'd by CA-6, 48-1 USTC 9171, 165 Fed. (2d) 645. ¶ 78.01

tion of an estate are made available to the remainderman to whom the property is distributed. Accordingly, if the deduction of the interest on the legacy results in an excess deduction in the year of the estate's termination, which would have been the case in the Davidson example above, the remainderman I would be entitled to the tax benefit from the excess deduction. Under this approach, it could be desirable to make the distribution in the year the estate is closed, rather than in an earlier year. (Under the 1939 Code, excess deductions were wasted when the estate terminated.) [¶ 79] Separate shares may be treated as separate trusts.-Where a trust has two or more beneficiaries and is to be administered in well-defined and separate shares, such shares are to be treated as separate trusts for the purpose of determining the amount of distributable net income allocable to the beneficiaries. The purpose of this rule is to limit the tax liability of a beneficiary on income which is accumulated for the benefit of another beneficiary. 

Example (1): Income of a trust is accumulated for beneficiary A but a distribution is made to beneficiary B of both income and corpus in an amount exceeding the share of income that would be distributable to B had there been separate trusts. In the absence of a separate share rule B would be taxed on income which is accumulated for A. The division of distributable net income into separate shares will limit the tax liability of B. 

The separate share treatment is mandatory and not elective. A trustee may be required to apply it even though separate and independent accounts are not maintained for each share and even though there is no physical segregation of assets. The separate share rule does not apply to estates. It also does not affect situations in which a single trust instrument creates not one but several separate trusts, as opposed to separate shares in the same trust. 

The treatment of separate shares as separate trusts applies only for the purpose of determining distributable net income in computing the distribution deduction allowable to the trust and the amount includible in the income of the beneficiary. It cannot be applied to obtain more than one deduction for personal exemption or to split the income of the trust into several shares for the purpose of being taxed at lower-bracket rates.16 

The example below illustrates in brief the operation of the separate share rule.

Example (2): A trust instrument provides that trust income is to be divided into two equal shares and that it is to be accumulated or distributed to beneficiaries A and B in the discretion of the trustee. For years prior to the taxable year the income of the trust was accumulated. During the taxable year no distribution was made to A but a distribution of $10,000 was made to B. The trust's distributable net income for the taxable year was $16,000. The trust is assumed to qualify for the separate share treatment.

Under the separate share rule, the distributable net income must be divided into two equal parts for the purpose of determining the trust's distribution deduction and the amount of the distribution includible in B's gross income. 

Therefore, the distributable net income of each share of the trust is $8,000. Since the $10,000 distributed to B during the taxable year exceeds the distributable net income of $8,000 allocated to his share, the trust is deemed to have distributed to him $8,000 of the taxable year's income and $2,000 of amounts other than the taxable year's income. Accordingly, the trust is allowed a distribution deduction of $8,000 in determining its taxable income. The amount of the distribution 

16 Code Sec. 663(c); Reg. §§ 1.663(c)-1-1.663 

(c)-4. ¶ 79

includible in B's gross income is $8,000, the portion of the distributable net income of the trust allocated to his share. Also, the excess distribution of $2,000 will be subject to the throwback provisions discussed at ¶ 86-88. [80] What is the effect of different taxable years?—As in the case of a "simple" trust and its beneficiaries (see ¶77), if the taxable year of a beneficiary is different from that of a "complex" trust or a decedent's estate, the amount includible in the beneficiary's gross income is based on the distributable net income of the trust or estate and the amounts required to be distributed or properly paid or credited to the beneficiary for any taxable year or years of the estate or trust ending with or within his taxable year.¹7 

If a beneficiary dies, so that an amount is paid, credited or required to be distributed by an estate or a complex trust for a taxable year which does not end with or within the last taxable year of the beneficiary, the amount to be included in the gross income of the beneficiary for his last taxable year or in the gross income of his estate is determined by the computations under Code Sec. 662 for the taxable year of the estate or trust in which his last taxable year ends. The gross income for the last taxable year of a beneficiary on the cash basis includes only income actually distributed to the beneficiary before his death. Income required to be distributed, but in fact distributed to his estate, is included in the gross income of the estate as "income in respect of a decedent" under Code Sec. 691.18

If the existence of a beneficiary which is not an individual terminates, the amount to be included in its gross income for the last taxable year is computed as if the beneficiary were a deceased individual. However, income required to be distributed prior to the termination but actually distributed to the beneficiary's successor in interest is included in the beneficiary's income for its last taxable year.19

There Are Special Exclusion Rules for Estates and Complex Trusts 

[[81] Gifts and bequests may be exempt.-Gifts or bequests of specific sums of money or specific property are not allowed as deductions to a complex trust or to an estate, and are not includible in the taxable income of the beneficiary.20 The gift or bequest must be required by the specific terms of the will or trust instrument, and it must be paid or credited all at once or in not more than three installments in order to come within this special exclusion rule. 

In order to qualify as a gift or bequest of a specific sum of money or of specific property, the amount of money or the identity of the specific property must be ascertainable under the terms of the testator's will as of the date of his death, or under the terms of an inter vivos trust instrument as of the date of the inception of the trust.

The amounts listed below are not gifts or bequests of a sum of money or of specific property: (1) An amount which can be paid or credited only from the income of an estate or trust, whether from current or accumulated income; (2) An annuity or periodic gifts of specific property in lieu of or having the effect of an annuity; (3) A residuary estate or the corpus of a trust; or 

17 Code Sec. 662(c); Reg. § 1.662(c)-1. 

18 Reg. $1.662(c)-2. 

19 Reg. § 1.662(c)-3. 

20 Code Sec. 663(a)(1). ¶ 80

There Are Special Exclusion Rules for Estates and Complex Trusts 

103 (4) A gift or bequest paid in a lump sum or in not more than three installments, if the gift or bequest is required to be paid in more than three installments under the terms of the governing instrument.¹ 

Example (1): Testator's will directs that a bequest of $30,000 be paid to taxpayer, and that the residue of the estate be held in trust for the benefit of taxpayer's children. After taxes, the only asset left in the estate is an apartment building worth $200,000. So that the executor-trustee will not be required to sell the apartment building, the taxpayer voluntarily allows him to pay the legacy over a period of years out of rent. The net rentals are $10,000, which the executortrustee pays to the taxpayer in three annual installments. The payments are not taxable.

Example (2): Under the terms of a will, a legacy of $5,000 was left to A, 1,000 shares of X company stock were left to W, and the balance of the estate was to be divided equally between W and X. No provision was made in the will for the disposition of income of the estate during the period of administration. 

The estate had income of $25,000 during the taxable year, which was accumulated and added to corpus for estate accounting purposes. During the taxable year, the executor paid the legacy of $5,000 in a lump sum to A and transferred the X company stock to W. No other distributions to beneficiaries were made during the taxable year. The distributions to A and W qualify for exclusion. 

Example (3): Under the terms of a will, the testator's estate was to be divided equally between A and B. No provision was made in the will for the disposition of income of the estate during the period of administration. The estate had income of $50,000 for the taxable year. In accordance with an agreement among the beneficiaries that part of the assets of the estate would be distributed in kind to the beneficiaries, stock in corporation X was distributed to A during the taxable year. The fair market value of the stock was $40,000 on the date of distribution. No other distribution was made during the year. The distribution does not qualify as an exclusion, since it is not a specific gift to A required by the terms of the will. Therefore, the fair market value of the property ($40,000) represents a distribution, to be considered in determining the estate's distribution deduction and includible as such in A's gross income. 

Example (4): Under the terms of a testamentary trust instrument, A and B 

are each to receive $75,000 in installments of $25,000, to be paid in alternate years. 

Thus, there is a total of six payments spread over six years, but not more than three payments to a beneficiary. Therefore, the bequests for both A and B qualify for the exclusion. The gifts to each beneficiary are to be separately 

In determining whether a gift or bequest of a specific sum of money or of specific property is required to be paid or credited to a particular beneficiary in more than three installments, gifts or bequests of articles for personal use (such as personal and household effects, automobiles, and the like) are disregarded and specifically devised real property, the title to which passes directly from the decedent to the devisee under local law, is not taken into account. Such items would qualify for the exclusion regardless of the treatment to be accorded other items of property bequeathed under a decedent's will.2 [82] Charitable contributions not allowed as part of distribution deduction. An estate or complex trust is allowed an unlimited deduction for charitable contributions under Code Sec. 642 (c), as explained at ¶ 70. Because of this, the law specifically disallows charitable contributions to be deducted 

1 Reg. § 1.663(a)-1(b). 

2 Reg. § 1.663(a)-1(c). 

¶ 82

under Code Sec. 661, that is, as part of the distribution deduction. Also, such charitable contributions are not to be treated as amounts distributed for purposes of determining the amounts includible in the income of the beneficiaries under Code Sec. 662.³ 

[83] Double deductions are denied.-Double deduction of certain payments or credits to beneficiaries is specifically denied. An amount which is paid, credited or distributed in the taxable year of the estate or trust will not be deductible by it under Code Sec. 661 or be treated as an amount distributed to beneficiaries under Code Sec. 662 if Code Sec. 651 or 661 (deductions of 

"simple" trusts distributing current income only) applied to such amount for a prior taxable year.*

Example: All of the income of a trust is required to be distributed currently to beneficiary A and both the trust and A report on the calendar year basis. For administrative convenience, the trustee distributes in January and February 1958 a portion of the trust income required to be distributed in 1957. The portion of the income for 1957 which was distributed in 1958 may not be claimed as a deduction by the trust for 1958 since it is deductible by the trust and includible in A's gross income for the year 1957. 

How About Sales and Assignments of Trust Interests? [84] Does capital gain result from a sale of life interest?—At present, two Courts of Appeal have held that the consideration received by life beneficiaries of trusts for the transfer of their life interests in the trusts to the remaindermen, is capital gain proceeds and not ordinary income. If such a transaction resulted in a loss the loss would be a capital one. However, in both these cases the Tax Court was of the opposite view and there is nothing to indicate since those decisions that it has changed its mind. 

No tax should result against the trustee in a transaction of the above character, even though its assistance is required or offered in effecting the dissolution or the formal transfer of the interest affected. [185] It makes a difference whether you assign trust interest or trust income. The Supreme Court has held that a life beneficiary of a testamentary trust, who made an absolute assignment to his children of portions of his interest in the trust is not taxable upon income later distributed in accordance with the assignment. But an assignment of a sum of money payable out of the income of a trust is not the same as an assignment of a share of income without retention of any control over the interest assigned. Accordingly, where the life beneficiary of a testamentary trust assigned income from the trust for the succeeding year, the Supreme Court held that such income is taxable to the assignor."

The validity of assignments of trust income is largely governed by the laws and court decisions of the State in which the trust is administered. Under the laws of Pennsylvania, for instance, the beneficiary of a spendthrift trust has the power to revoke an assignment of trust income at any time, and the trust income is not deliverable to the assignee until it accrues to the assignorbeneficiary of the trust. Thus, it is held that such an income beneficiary of a

How About Sales and Assignments of Trust Interests 

105 spendthrift trust in that State who makes an assignment of the trust income s, notwithstanding the assignment, liable for Federal income tax on such ncome.8 

The Internal Revenue Service has ruled, that where the lifetime income beneficiary of a testamentary trust from time to time gives his written consent, pursuant to the terms of the will, to pay a certain portion of the trust income to another individual, the amounts so paid will be taxable as income to the beneficiary inasmuch as he, in substance, has parted with no substantial interest in property other than the specified payments of income. However, in the case of an irrevocable assignment, valid under local law, of trust income for a period of not less than 10 years, such income will be taxable to the assignee, provided the facts are not such as would subject the lifetime income beneficiary (the assignor) to the tax on income under the grantor trust's principles of Code Sec. 677 (see ¶92). In such a case the position of the assignor is analogous to that of the grantor of a trust. Under such circumstances, because of the period and character of the assignment, the lifetime income beneficiary will be considered to have made a disposition of a substantial interest in the trust property.9 

I. T. 4035, 1950-2 CB 60. 'Rev. Rul. 55-38, 1955-1 CB 389. ¶ 85

PART V-THE FIVE-YEAR THROWBACK RULE 

[86] What does the throwback rule accomplish?-In order to prevent the accumulation of income and later distribution of such income in a lowincome year of a beneficiary to avoid taxes, a five-year throwback rule is provided in the law.10 The throwback rule has the effect of carrying back to the five preceding years, in inverse order, distributions in excess of distributable net income for the distribution year, taking the same amounts into taxable income of the beneficiary as he would have done if they had been distributed in the prior years. This additional income is taxed as part of his distributive share for the distribution year, but the tax attributable to such income cannot be more than it would have been if he had actually received the additional amounts in the prior years. Also, the beneficiary is allowed a credit against tax for that portion of the taxes paid by the trust in the prior years which would not have been paid if the trust had actually distributed the amounts treated as additional distributions under the throwback rule. This credit eliminates any double tax on the income.

This throwback rule applies only under the 1954 Code. This means that it is applicable only to distributions by a trust of amounts which were accumulated during taxable years beginning after December 31, 1953 and ending after 

August 16, 1954.

The throwback rule does not apply to estates or generally to simple trusts, that is, trusts which are required to distribute all income currently. Only complex trusts are affected. However, the throwback rule will affect a prior taxable year in which a trust qualified as a simple trust. This could happen where a trust which is required to distribute all of its income currently received, in a preceding year, extraordinary dividends or taxable stock dividends which the trustee in good faith allocated to corpus but which are later determined to be currently distributable to the beneficiary. The trust would be treated as a complex trust for the preceding year only for purposes of the throwback rule.

A complex trust which distributes income accumulated in prior years is required to file Schedule J (Form 1041) along with its regular Form 1041. 

Schedule J provides for the computation of amounts deemed distributed to beneficiaries under the throwback rule as well as the beneficiaries' shares of income and credits. [187] Special definitions and terms.-There are two terms used in connection with the throwback rule which must be defined. The first is "accumulation distribution". This term is defined as the amount (but only if more than $2,000) by which any "other" amounts (meaning the amounts over and above those required to be distributed currently) properly paid or credited or required to be distributed exceed the distributable net income reduced (but not below zero) by any amount of income required to be distributed currently." 

Example: A trust distributes $20,000 to the beneficiary during the taxable year, of which $9,000 is income required to be distributed to him currently. The distributable net income of the trust is $15,000. The accumulation distribution is computed as follows: 

10 Code Secs. 665-668; Reg. §§ 1.665-1-1.668 

(b)-2. 

1 Reg. $ 1.665(b)-1. ¶ 86

The Five-Year Throwback Rule 

107 

Total distribution 

$20,000 

Income required to be distributed currently. 9,000 

Other amounts distributed. $11,000 

Distributable net income.. 

Less: Income required to be distributed currently. $15,000 9,000

Balance of distributable net income. 6,000 

Accumulation distribution $ 5,000

For the purpose of determining an accumulation distribution, the following "other" amounts distributed are not to be taken into consideration: (1) distributions of income accumulated before the beneficiary's birth or until he becomes 21, (2) amounts paid to cover emergency needs of the beneficiary, (3) a final distribution made by the trust more than nine years after the last transfer to such trust, and (4) amounts properly paid or credited to a beneficiary upon his attaining a specified age provided (a) there will be no more than four such distributions to him, (b) the period between distributions is at least 4 years, and (c) they are specifically provided for in the trust instrument as of January 1, 1954.12

The other term to be defined is "undistributed net income." Undistributed net income is defined as the amount by which the distributable net income for the year exceeds the sum of (a) any amount of income for the year required to be distributed currently, (b) any "other" amounts properly paid, credited, or required to be distributed for such year, and (c) the amount of taxes properly allocable to the undistributed portion of the distributable net income.13 

The amount of taxes in (c) is determined by subtracting from the amount of income taxes paid by the trust the amount it would have paid had it distributed all of the distributable net income. Thus, this difference represents the portion of the taxes paid by the trust which is attributable to items of gross income which are not includible in distributable net income, such as capital gains allocable to corpus, and, in the case of a simple trust, extraordinary dividends and taxable stock dividends allocable to corpus. [88] Here is how the throwback rule operates. The accumulation distribution for the taxable year is treated as an "other" amount distributable for each of the preceding five years, beginning with the latest year, to the extent it exceeds the total of the undistributed net income for each of the intervening years. If the amount so treated is at least equal to the undistributed net income for the prior year, the full amount of tax paid by the trust for that year is included in the amount treated as an additional distribution under the throwback rule; if the amount so treated is less than the undistributed net income of the prior year, a proportionate part of the taxes for that year is treated as an additional distribution. 

12 Reg. $1.665(b)-2. 

18 Reg. 1.665(a)-1. 1 88

The amounts treated as additional distributions for a prior year are allocated to each beneficiary, and their character determined under the conduit rule, just as though they had been "other" amounts distributed as explained at 174. Each beneficiary then computes his tax on the additional amounts as outlined at ¶ 86, treating them as though they had been distributed on the last day of any prior year involved.

Example: A trust established on January 2, 1955, is required to distribute currently one-half of its income to the beneficiary, with the balance distributable at the trustee's discretion. All income is taxable interest, and there are no deductions except the $100 exemption. For 1955-1957, its accounts show: 1955 1956. 1957. $10,000 $2,640 $7,460 10,000 2,640 7,460 (* $100 trust exemption deducted in computing taxable income, but not in computing distributable (or undistributed) net income.) 

The accumulation distribution for 1957 is $12,970, consisting of the excess of "other" amounts distributed ($28,020) over $15,050 (the distributable net income ($30,100) less the income required to be distributed currently ($15,050 (50% of $30,100))). The beneficiary will, therefore, include in his income for 1957, not only the $30,100 taxable interest received by the trust, but additional amounts as follows:

The amount included in income of the beneficiary for 1957 is the sum of the regular amount for that year ($30,100, the distributable net income for 1957) plus the additional distributions for 1956 ($10,100) and 1955 ($7,459.92), or $47,659.92. 

He will first compute his tax for 1957 by including this entire amount in his income for that year. Next, he will compute his tax for 1957 by including only $30,100 in his income for that year and adding to it the amount of additional taxes that would have been due in 1956 and 1955 if he had actually been taxed in those years on the amounts which are treated as additional distributions for those years, under the throwback rule. Then he will compare this total with the tax computed by including the entire $47,659.92 in his income for 1957, and his tax for 1957 will be the lesser of the two amounts. Finally, he will be allowed a credit against tax for the amount of taxes paid by the trust which would not have been paid if the trust had actually distributed the amounts treated as additional distributions for 1956 and 1955 under the throwback rule. The amount of credit would be $4,761.18, the sum of $2,640 (no tax would have been paid for 1956) and $2,121.18 (the difference between the $2,640 paid for 1955 and the $518.82 tax which the trust would have paid on taxable income of $2,540.08 if it had actually made the $7,459.92 additional distribution in that year). ¶ 88

PART VI-FAMILY TRUSTS 

[89] Can family trusts save taxes?-A taxpayer who desires to split his income with a family member, or otherwise to relieve himself of the liability for taxes on income from certain property, may do so by conveying the property in trust for the family member. This is particularly desirable where the family member is a minor child or one to whom the taxpayer does not wish to entrust property at the time. The taxpayer may thus avoid tax on part of his income, subject to the general requirement that he must in fact transfer the income-producing property, rather than just the income from such property. 

In the case of a transfer to a trust there are several limitations in the law which are designed to prevent the taxpayer from escaping tax on the income from the property because of the control which he retains over the trust. 

These restrictions on trusts are explained in 90 through 93 below. These rules do not necessarily invalidate the entire trust; they apply only to that portion of the trust over which the prohibited powers, etc., are retained. 

Where, under the tests described at 90-93, it is determined that the grantor or another person is treated as the owner of any portion of a trust, there is required to be included in computing the taxable income of the grantor or such other person those items of trust income, deductions, and credits against tax which are attributable to that portion of the trust, to the extent such items would be taken into account in computing taxable income or credit of an individual. Any remaining portion of the trust is subject to the rules described at 45-83.

Where part of the trust income is taxable to the grantor or other person, such income should not be reported by the fiduciary on Form 1041, but it should be shown, together with the deductions and credits applicable thereto, in a separate statement to be attached to Form 1041. 14 [90] The Clifford doctrine taxes income to grantor.-Following a landmark decision by the U. S. Supreme Court in the Clifford case, the courts and the Internal Revenue Service have treated the grantor of a trust as the owner of the trust income where the corpus or the income is to revert to him within a short time or where the grantor retains the power to determine the beneficiary.

In the Clifford case, a trust was created by a husband for the benefit of his wife. The trust was to last for five years and at the end of that time was to revert to the husband-grantor if he were living. The husband was trustee, with sole discretion as to amounts distributable to his wife, and otherwise retained wide powers of control over the trust. This trust was not in connection with divorce; the couple remained married. The Supreme Court held that the income of the trust was taxable to the husband. The reasoning of the court was that, considering the solidarity of the family and the express reservation of powers, the income could be so held or used with an eye to the grantor's benefit that in substance and effect the income was his. 

The law states definite rules for determining whether the grantor is the substantial owner of a trust, thereby making him taxable on the trust's income under the Clifford doctrine. The broad criteria are: (1) Reversion of the trust corpus or interest to the grantor within a short time; (2) power of the grantor or nonadverse party to control the beneficial enjoyment of a trust; and (3) reserva- 

14 Helvering v. Clifford, 40-1 USTC ¶ 9265, 309

U. S. 331. 

¶ 90

tion of important administrative powers to the grantor or a nonadverse party other than in a fiduciary capacity.15 [¶ 90.01] Reversionary interests. The grantor is treated as the owner of income from the portion of a trust with respect to which the corpus or income will or reasonably may be expected to revert to him within 10 years after the transfer in trust. The foregoing rule does not apply, however, where the income from a trust in which the grantor has a reversionary interest is, under the terms of the trust instrument, irrevocably payable to some types of charities for at least 2 years. The eligible donees are: a church, a convention or association of churches, a religious order, an educational organization, or a hospital.16 

Where the grantor's reversionary interest will take effect in possession or enjoyment only upon the death of the beneficiary to whom the income is payable, the grantor will not be treated as the owner of the income.¹7 

Example (1): A, who is 60 years old, creates an irrevocable trust for the benefit of his wife, who is 65. Upon the death of the wife, the income shall be payable to A. The income paid to the wife is not taxable to A, even though the wife's life expectancy is less than 10 years so that A's reversionary interest reasonably may be expected to take effect within 10 years after the transfer in trust. 

If the date for reacquisition of the reversionary interest by the grantor is postponed, the postponement is treated as a new transfer in trust. Such new transfer is deemed to be made on the date the postponement is effected and to last for the period prescribed by the postponement.¹

Example (2): A creates a trust for a term of 15 years, at which time the corpus is to revest in him. At the end of the 14th year, the trust is extended for 

5 years from the original termination date, so that it will have a total term of 20 years. A new 6-year trust is considered to have been created. 

Where there is a postponement of the effective date for reacquisition of a reversionary interest, resulting in the creation of a new short-term trust for a period of less than 10 years, the income from the short-term trust will not be taxed to the grantor for any year in which it would not have been taxed to him in the absence of the postponement.

Example (3): Assume the same facts as in Example (2), above, in which the extension of a 15-year trust at the end of the 14th year resulted in the creation of a new 6-year trust. The income for the first year of the 6-year trust would not be taxable to the grantor, since such year constitutes the 15th year of the 15-year trust, the income from which was not taxable to the grantor. [¶ 90.02] Control of beneficial enjoyment.—Where the grantor of a trust, or a non-adverse party, or both, may exercise a power of disposition over the beneficial enjoyment of the trust income or corpus, the income is taxable to the grantor.19 Exceptions are made for the following powers: Power to apply income to the support of a dependent, if it is not exercised; power affecting beneficial enjoyment only after expiration of a 10-year period; power exercisable only by will; power to allocate among charitable beneficiaries; power to distribute corpus for a beneficiary; power to withhold income temporarily; power to withhold income during disability of a beneficiary; power to allocate receipts and disbursements between corpus and income; some powers exercisable by independent trustees; and the power in the trustees (other than the grantor or the spouse) to allocate income, if limited by a standard.20 

15 Code Secs. 673, 674, and 675. 

16 Reg. §§ 1.673(a)-1 and 1.673(b)-1. 

17 Reg. § 1.673(a)-1(b). 

18 Reg. 

19 Reg. 

20 Reg. 1.673(d)-1. 1.674(a)-1. 1.674(b)-1—1.674(d)-2. ¶ 90.01

Family Trusts 

111 [ 90.03] Administrative powers.-The income from a trust is taxable to the grantor where the administrative control of the trust is exercisable primarily for his benefit. The prohibited administrative controls are: (1) A power exercisable by the grantor or a nonadverse party which enables the grantor or any one else to deal with trust corpus or income for less than an adequate consideration. (2) A power exercisable by the grantor or a nonadverse party enabling the grantor to borrow from the trust, directly or indirectly, without adequate interest or adequate security. (3) Borrowing of trust funds by the grantor without complete repayment of the loan and interest before the beginning of the taxable year. An exception is made where the loan provides for adequate interest and adequate security and is made by an independent trustee. (4) General powers of administration exercisable in a nonfiduciary capacity by any nonadverse party without the approval or consent of any person in a fiduciary capacity. [91] How is income of a revocable trust treated?—If a grantor creates a trust and reserves to himself a right to revoke it, the income of such a trust is expressly treated under the law as his income and not that of the trust.2 

However, a limiting provision states that the trust income will be taxed to the grantor only where he would be treated as the owner if the power to revoke were a reversionary interest. This provision is correlated with the shortterm trust provision that a grantor retaining a reversionary interest (90.01) in the trust corpus or income is treated as the owner and is taxable on the trust income if the reversionary interest may reasonably be expected to take effect in possession or enjoyment within a period of 10 years. 

Example (1): J sets up a trust for the benefit of his son, reserving to himself a right to revoke it at any time after five years. The income of such trust is taxable to J.

Example (2): L sets up a trust for the benefit of his grandson under which he has reserved a right of revocation. In order to exercise the right, however, he is required to give the trustees notice of his intention to do so at least twelve months prior to the date of exercise. The income of the trust is taxable to L. 

Example (3): Z sets up a trust for the benefit of his daughter. Pursuant to the trust instrument he transfers to the trust two parcels of property, (1) and (2). Under the terms of the trust instrument, he reserves the right to revoke the trust as to property (1), but not as to property (2). The income from property (1) is taxable to him. The income from property (2), assuming no other facts, is not taxable to him.

If the power to revoke a trust is in another person, or in the grantor and another person jointly, then the income of the trust will nevertheless be taxed to the grantor if such other person does not have a substantial adverse interest. 

A trustee has been held not to be a person with a substantial adverse interest.* 

Obviously, a beneficiary should be a person with a substantial adverse interest as to the part of the trust corpus or income which represents his interest. But this may not be true of a beneficiary closely related to the grantor. 

The effect of this provision is to treat the trust, or that portion of the corpus as to which it is revocable, as though the trust had not been created. 

Even though the power to revoke is not exercised and the beneficiaries receive the income, the amounts received, as to them, would be gifts. 

¹ Reg. § 1.675-1. 

2 Code Sec. 676(a); Reg. § 1.676(a)-1. 

* Code Sec. 676(b); Reg. § 1.676(b)-1. 

• Reinecke v. K. G. Smith, (S. Ct.) 3 USTC 

1089, 289 U. S. 172. ¶ 91

Example (4): T sets up a trust reserving to himself a right to revoke it at any time. During the current year, the trust received $25,000 in income derived from the trust corpus, incurred $3,000 in administrative expenses and trustee's fees, and paid $22,000 to the named beneficiary. T will include the full $25,000 in his gross income and the $22,000 paid out is treated as a gift from T. T will be allowed a deduction for as much of the $3,000 as is attributable to the production of the income or the management, conservation or maintenance of the property from which the income is derived. 

[[[92] What about trusts for the benefit of the grantor?-Income from a trust is taxable to the grantor where the income is, or in the discretion of the grantor or a nonadverse party may be: (1) distributed to the grantor, (2) held or accumulated for future distribution to the grantor, or (3) applied to payment of premiums on life insurance covering the grantor, except policies irrevocably payable to charities.5 

The grantor is therefore treated as the owner of the income whether its distribution or accumulation for his benefit is mandatory, or whether it is discretionary with him or a nonadverse party. 

As in the case of revocable trusts, if the exercise of the power by the grantor or nonadverse party cannot affect the beneficial enjoyment of the income within a period of 10 years, the income is not taxable to the grantor. 

Also, as in the case of revocable trusts, the grantor is taxable on only that portion of the trust income which is or may be distributed or accumulated for his benefit. Income has been held taxable to the grantor even if future distribution to him is dependent on the death of a beneficiary; and the father of a grantor has been held not to have a substantial adverse interest although by virtue of the discretion given him he could pay the income to his wife and other children instead of to the grantor. If income of a trust may be used to any extent in satisfaction of the parental obligations of the grantor, then to that extent it is regarded as distributable to the grantor; but if the discretion so to use the income is not in the grantor acting as such but in another person, the trustee, or in the grantor acting as trustee or co-trustee, then such income is includible in the grantor's gross income only. to the extent it is actually so used.⁹ The income that is made taxable to the grantor constitutes a gift to the beneficiaries if it is actually distributed to them. [93] Trust income can be taxed to a person other than the grantor.— 

The law provides rules under which a person other than the grantor of a trust may be taxed on its income by reason of a power to acquire the corpus or income of the trust. This provision could apply, for example, in the case of a trust established by a husband for the benefit of his children, but under which the grantor's wife may at any time take the trust property. In such a case the wife is treated as the owner and the income is taxed to her. This provision will not apply if the power is renounced or disclaimed within a reasonable period of time.10

PART VII-THE FIDUCIARY'S DUTIES AND LIABILITIES 

[194] Fiduciary can be personally liable for tax. The fiduciary is required to make and file the return and pay the tax on the taxable income of an estate or a trust. Liability for the payment of the tax on the taxable income of an estate attaches to the person of the executor or administrator up to and after his discharge if, prior to distribution and discharge, he had notice of his tax obligations or failed to exercise due diligence in ascertaining whether or not such obligations existed. Liability for the tax also follows the assets of the estate distributed to heirs, devisees, legatees, and distributees, who may be required to discharge the amount of the tax due and unpaid to the extent of the distributive shares received by them. The same considerations apply to trusts.11

The extent of an estate fiduciary's liability is found in Sec. 3467 of the 

U. S. Revised Statutes (31 USC 192). Under Sec. 3467, every executor, administrator, or assignee or other person who pays, in whole or in part, any debt due by the person or estate for whom or for which he acts without first satisfying and paying debts due the United States is personally liable to the extent of such payments. 13 The debts due the United States must have been determined or determinable at the time the other debts were paid. The fiduciary must also have been "chargeable" with knowledge of the debts due the United States.12 

However, in one case ¹³, the Tax Court pointed out that knowledge on the part of the fiduciary as to the existence of the liability to the Government is not a specific requirement of the law, and that if the Government has made a prima facie showing the burden is on the fiduciary to establish lack of knowledge.

According to the language used in one Tax Court case 14, before liability can attach to the fiduciary, he must have had a choice between payment of the tax owing and payment of a debt owing by the estate. However, other cases have been to the effect that a fiduciary may not hasten payment of debts and distribution of an estate before Federal taxes are finally determined and, by so doing avoid personal responsibility for the taxes.15 Thus, he should not make distributions before the Commissioner's audit of the final return is completed, particularly if there is a difference of opinion (or possibility thereof) as to treatment of an item.

There is no personal liability on the part of a fiduciary for amounts paid out for debts which have priority over Federal taxes. Such debts include probate costs of administration, decedent's funeral expenses and headstone 16, and the statutory allowance to a widow required by local law where the assets used in payment of the allowance are assets of the estate. A distinction was made where the assets used to pay the allowance were assets to which the estate was not entitled, but which it, as transferee, held subject to liabilities of the insolvent transferor.18 

11 Reg. § 1.641(b)-2. 

12 G. Terranova, 2 TCM 616, Dec. 13,431(M). 

18 L. T. McCourt, 15 TC 734, Dec. 17,951. 

14 G. P. Fitzgerald, 4 TC 494, Dec. 14,291. 

15 U. S. v. Hurst, (DC) 2 Fed. (2d) 73; Pennsylvania Cement Co. v. Bradley, (DC) 274 Fed. (2d) 1003. 17. 

16 U. S. v. Weisburn, (DC) 43-1 USTC ¶ 9247, 48 

Fed. Supp. 393. 

17 J. Smith, 24 BTA 807, Dec. 7275. 

18 L. T. McCourt, 15 TC 734, Dec. 17,951. 194

Discharge of the administrator or executor by the probate court does not terminate his liability where he paid the other debts of the estate without satisfying the prior claim for Federal taxes.19 A fiduciary who distributes property and is later subjected to personal liability under Sec. 3467 may compel reimbursement of payment from the distributees.20 In order to indemnify himself against personal liability for taxes later found due, a fiduciary who distributes property may safeguard his interests in doubtful cases by exacting a bond from the distributees. [95] How is fiduciary's liability enforced?-In general, it may be said that the personal liability of a fiduciary is assessed, collected, and paid in the same way as a deficiency in tax. The Code provisions relating to delinquency in payment after notice and demand and the amount of interest attaching because of such delinquency, the authorization of distraint and proceedings in court for collection, the prohibition of claims for abatement and claims and suits for refund, the filing of a petition with the Tax Court, and the filing of a petition for review of the Tax Court's decision are applicable as in the case of any other income tax deficiency. 

Special criminal penalties are incurred where the fiduciary willfully fails to make a return when required by law or regulations to do so, or the fiduciary willfully attempts in any manner to evade or defeat the tax. These penalties are in addition to the civil penalties applicable to the tax due by the estate or trust.

The estate of a decedent is liable for penalties incurred by the decedent during his lifetime. The penalties provided in the Code are remedial compensation for loss to the Government, and not personal punishment, and they survive the death of the taxpayer.¹

Estates of insane, deceased, or insolvent persons are not exempt from penalties for delinquencies or violations, the administrators, executors, etc., being personally liable in such cases. 

The enforcement of the estate's or trust's tax liability against the fiduciary does not convert it into a tax due from the fiduciary. For instance, the fiduciary upon whom this liability is imposed cannot, in his individual return, deduct as a payment of tax the amount so paid. Therefore, since the liability for the tax is not extinguished, where not actually collected in an action against the fiduciary, the Government may proceed against legatees or other persons to whom property has been distributed by subjecting them to liability as transferees. [ 95.01] Notice of fiduciary relationship.-The assessment and collection of income and estate taxes from those acting in a fiduciary capacity are facilitated by Code Sec. 6903 which provides for the giving of notice by the fiduciary that he is acting in such capacity. After giving such notice, the fiduciary is to assume the powers, rights, duties, and privileges of the taxpayer for whom he acts, and thereupon the Commissioner will deal directly with him. The estate or trust, however, pays the tax which becomes a personal liability of the fiduciary only on the conditions specified in ¶ 94. 

The notice of fiduciary relationship does not impose any new liability upon the fiduciary. The function of the notice is to assure the receipt by the fiduciary of notification of imposition of the tax against the estate or trust 

1 Kirk (Briden Est.), (CA-1) 50-1 uSTC 9169, 179 Fed. (2d) 619; Reimer, (CA-6) 50-1 USTC 

9189, 180 Fed. (2d) 159. 

2 I. T. 1424, I-2 CB 101. 

19 M. Viles, (CA-6) 56-1 USTC [9539, 233 Fed. (2d) 376. 

20 S. M. 2526, III-2 CB 179. ¶ 95

The Fiduciary's Duties and Liabilities 

115 which he represents, and to afford the fiduciary an opportunity of contesting this liability. The omission to file such a notice may result in prejudicing the interests of the estate or trust. 

The notice provided for in Code Sec. 6903 must be in writing, signed by the fiduciary, and filed with the Commissioner. It must state the name and address of the person for whom the fiduciary is acting and the nature of the liability of such person; that is, whether it is a liability for tax, and, if so, the year or years involved, or a liability at law or in equity of the transferee of property of a taxpayer, or a liability of a fiduciary under Sec. 3467 (Rev. Stats.).

Unless there is already on file with the Commissioner satisfactory evidence of authority of the fiduciary to act for such person in a fiduciary capacity, such evidence must be filed with and made a part of the notice. If the fiduciary capacity exists by order of court, a certified copy of the order may be regarded as such satisfactory evidence. When the fiduciary capacity has terminated, the fiduciary, in order to be relieved of any further duty or liability as such, should file with the Commissioner written notice that the fiduciary capacity has terminated as to him, accompanied by satisfactory evidence of the termination of the fiduciary capacity. The notice of termination should state the name and address of the person, if any, who has been substituted as fiduciary. The termination notice cannot, of course, affect any liability for taxes already incurred by the fiduciary. 

[[96] What about claims for credit or refund?—The fiduciary is the proper person to make claim for credit or refund of overpayments made by the estate or trust. This is a duty devolving upon the fiduciary which may possibly be overlooked in the case of an estate on account of its short duration and the desire of the heirs or legatees to curtail administrative expenses. 

When a taxpayer dies, his administrators or executors may file claims for refund if they are still acting as such, and upon their discharge the right passes to the beneficiaries entitled to the estate. A beneficiary could be a trustee if the decedent created a testamentary trust. Signature of only one co-executor is required on the claim.³ 

If a return is filed by an individual and, after his death, a refund claim is filed by his legal representative, certified copies of the letters testamentary, letters of administration, or other similar evidence must be annexed to the claim, to show the authority of the legal representative to file the claim. If an executor, administrator, guardian, trustee, receiver, or other fiduciary files a return and thereafter a refund claim is filed by the same fiduciary, documentary evidence to establish the legal authority of the fiduciary need not accompany the claim, provided a statement is made in the claim showing that the return was filed by the fiduciary and that the latter is still acting. 

In such cases, if a refund is to be paid, letters testamentary, letters of administration, or other evidence may be required, but should be submitted only upon the receipt of a specific request therefor. If a claim is filed by a fiduciary other than the one by whom the return was filed, the necessary documentary evidence should accompany the claim. A claim may be executed by an agent of the person assessed, but in such cases a power of attorney must accompany the claim.* [197] A fiduciary can shorten the assessment and collection period.- 

Under a special provision in the law a fiduciary can shorten the regular 

Rev. Rul. 54-468, 1954-2 CB 248.

Reg. § 301.6402-2(e). 

197

3-year period (after return filed) for assessment and collection of the tax due from a decedent or his estate. The law provides that any tax for which a return is required (except estate taxes) and for which a decedent or an estate of a decedent may be liable is to be assessed, or a proceeding in court without assessment for the collection of such tax is to be begun, within 18 months after the receipt of a written request for prompt assessment.5 

The fiduciary must file the request for prompt assessment with the District Director for the Internal Revenue District in which the regular return was filed. The request, in order to be effective, must set forth the classes of tax and the taxable periods for which the prompt assessment is requested, and must clearly indicate that it is a request for prompt assessment under the provisions of Code Sec. 6501 (d). The request must also be filed after the return in question has been filed, and must be transmitted to the District Director in an envelope separately from any other document. 5a The effect of such a request is to limit the time in which an assessment of tax may be made, or a proceeding in court without assessment for collection of tax may be begun, to a period of 18 months from the date the request is received by the proper District Director. The request does not extend the time within which an assessment may be made, or a proceeding in court without assessment may be begun, beyond 3 years from the date the return was filed. This special period of limitations will not apply to any return filed after a request for prompt assessment has been made unless an additional request is filed. 

The privilege of a request for prompt assessment does not apply to trusts. 

Also, the privilege does not apply if the income tax returns filed by the decedent or estate were fraudulent. 

[98] What is the liability of a beneficiary-transferee?-The Code pro vides that the liability of a transferee of property of a taxpayer for income taxes imposed upon such taxpayer shall be assessed and collected in the same manner as any other deficiency imposed by the Commissioner. The term "transferee" is defined as including an heir, legatee, devisee, donee, and distributee. In the case of a transferee, the assessment must be within 1 year after the expiration of the period of limitation for assessment against the transferor. Assessment of the liability of a fiduciary must be made within one year after the liability arises or within the limited period for collection of the tax, whichever is the later. In the case of the decedent-transferor, it is the period that would be in effect had death not occurred. 

A question may arise as to when a personal liability of the fiduciary "arises." It is probable that it will be held to arise immediately upon the distribution of the assets out of which the amount of the tax should have been withheld.

In order to establish transferee liability under Code Sec. 6901, the Commissioner has the burden of proving the decedent's insolvency at the time of his death. Where he fails to do so, the estate is no more liable as a transferee than the decedent would have been if he were alive. If the insolvency of the estate is due to the personal representative's failing to observe priority of payment of debts and legacies, it would seem that the Commissioner has the election of proceeding against the fiduciary under Sec. 3467 (Rev. Stats.), or against the estate, the fiduciary, or beneficiary under Code Sec. 6901, or he may invoke both remedies to the extent necessary for the collection of the tax. 

Code Sec. 6501(d); Reg. $301.6501(d)-1. 

5a Rev. Rul. 57-319, I. R. B. 1957-27, 21. • Code Sec. 6901. ¶ 98

The Fiduciary's Duties and Liabilities 

117 [¶ 98.01] Insurance proceeds.-A lien for taxes may be enforced against the cash surrender value of a life insurance policy which is available to the insuredtaxpayer prior to the date of the insured-taxpayer's death. According to the 

Second and Seventh Circuits, the enforcement of the lien does not necessarily have to occur during the taxpayer's lifetime, but may be enforced even after the insured-taxpayer's death against the insurance proceeds in the hands of the beneficiary. In this respect, state exemption statutes exempting life insurance proceeds from payment of debts are of no avail against Federal tax claims.9 Where the beneficiary pays for and holds the life insurance policy, so that the insured cannot defeat her rights by change of beneficiary, it has been held that a tax lien against the insured-taxpayer cannot be enforced against the policy.¹0

If a tax lien arises before the insured-taxpayer names an irrevocable beneficiary or assigns the life insurance policy, the Government has a prior right to the cash surrender value of the policy as against the rights of the irrevocable beneficiary or assignee.¹¹

The Commissioner and the Tax Court hold that a beneficiary of a life insurance policy may be liable for taxes as transferee of the insured-taxpayer's property provided the insured-taxpayer had the power to change the beneficiary and his estate is insufficient in value to pay income taxes assessed. The beneficiary would be liable to the extent of the proceeds of the life insurance due him under the terms of the policy. 

But the Second 12, Third 12a, Fifth 13, Sixth 14, and Seventh 15 Circuits hold otherwise in varying degrees. They hold that the beneficiary is not liable as a transferee to the full extent of the life insurance proceeds. However, the 

Second, Third, and Seventh Circuits indicate that transferee liablity can arise but that such liability would be limited to the cash surrender value of the policy as of the date of the insured's death. The Sixth Circuit in the Stern case specifically disagreed with that proposition. The Sixth Circuit holds that a widow-beneficiary is not liable as a transferee of the proceeds of the policy or as a transferee of the cash surrender value. [99] Executors and trustees are eligible for long-term income benefits. -Ordinarily a fiduciary would be taxable on the entire amount of his fees and commissions paid or accrued during the taxable year even though they may be attributable in whole or in part to services performed in a prior year or prior years. In view of the graduations in the income tax rates, a fiduciary under such circumstances may have a greater tax burden than that of a taxpayer who is paid as the services are performed. However, under a special 

Code provision, this inequity is eliminated if certain conditions are met. 

The statute is to the effect that if at least 80% of the total compensation for personal services covering a period of 36 calendar months or more (from the beginning to the completion of such services) is received or accrued in one taxable year, then the tax attributable to any part of the amount received

or accrued in that year which is included in gross income shall not be greater than the aggregate of the taxes attributable to such part had it been included in gross income ratably over that part of the period of service which precedes the date of receipt or accrual.16

This long-term income treatment applies only to compensation from "an employment." The term "an employment" is defined to mean an arrangement or series of arrangements for the performance of personal services to effect a particular result, regardless of the number of sources from which compensation is received. It has been held that a trustee or executor cannot divide or separate services between principal and income,¹ or according to accounting periods,18 or between ordinary and extraordinary services (such as acting as tax counsel).19

Timing the receipt of executor's commissions and trustee's fees which cover services rendered over a substantial period can result in substantial tax savings under this provision.

Example: Suppose a taxpayer began his duties as executor on January 

10, 1954. On January 10, 1957, he was entitled to a fee of $8,000. If in his estimation his duties would not terminate before 1958 and the services to be performed by him during the remainder of 1957 would be only a small part of what he had already performed, say 20% or less, he may want to petition immediately for payment of $8,000 for services performed up to January 10, 1957. The $8,000 being 

80% of the total compensation of $10,000 which he ultimately expects to receive, the tax on the $8,000 for 1957 is not to exceed the aggregate of the amounts by which his 1954-1957 taxes would have been increased had the $8,000 been received ratably over the period of 36 months (from January 10, 1954 to January 10, 1957).

This treatment, of course, assumes that his estimate of the additional allowance is accurate. But if he is entitled to only $7,500 (75% of the aggregate amount expected) or has doubts as to the later allowance, it may be best to delay all the fees to the next year. So far, there has been no decision that long-term compensation must be collected as quickly as possible. [100] When is a fiduciary liable for self-employment tax?-Generally speaking, the Internal Revenue Service holds that a professional executor, trustee or administrator, that is, one who regularly engages in such services and handles a number of estates and/or trusts, is engaged in a trade or business and therefore subject to the self-employment tax. But nonprofessional êxecutors, trustees, and administrators who merely marshal and gather the assets of an estate in isolated cases would not be engaged in a trade or business and would not be subject to the self-employment tax. However, nonprofessional fiduciaries would be subject to self-employment tax on their fees if they actually carried on a business, such as operating a store in connection with the administration of an estate.20 The Tax Court says that the extent of the nonprofessional fiduciary's activities is the prime factor to be considered in determining whether he is engaged in a trade or business and, therefore, subject to the self-employment tax.¹
